<meta>This page continues the dedication from Ludwig Lavater to Johann Steiger. The previous page ended with the observation that people talk about things seen and heard which "men..." this page completes the thought with "terrify."</meta>

# E P I S T L E.

terrify [them]: likewise regarding those marvelous things that frequently precede the death of men—and especially of great princes—and significant changes of empires. Many who have never seen or heard anything of these matters believe that everything said about them is mere nonsense and old wives' tales <note>original: "aniles fabulas." A common Latin idiom for superstitions or folklore considered trivial or illogical.</note>: for [they believe] that simple men, as well as the fearful and superstitious, persuade themselves that they have seen and heard this or that, when the facts of the matter are quite different. Conversely, there are others who, as soon as they hear something—especially at night—immediately think some **Specter** <note>original: "Spectrum." Lavater uses this to mean any kind of ghost or supernatural apparition.</note> is wandering about, and they suffer great distress in their mind because they cannot distinguish natural physical phenomena from **Specters**. Some indeed (especially those who hunt for profit from the souls of the deceased <note>Lavater, a Protestant Reformer, is here critiquing the Roman Catholic practice of selling indulgences or requiring paid masses to theoretically free souls from Purgatory.</note>) claim that most things heard and seen are the souls of the dead seeking help from the living, so that from the greatest torture—

<meta>catchword: -morum (completing "maximorum," meaning "of the greatest [torments]")</meta>

<summary>Lavater describes the varied reactions to reports of ghosts and omens, ranging from total skepticism to extreme fear or the exploitation of such beliefs for financial gain.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication from Ludwig Lavater to Johann Steiger. The previous page discussed how some believe the souls of the dead appear to ask for help to be freed from torture; this page completes that thought with "of the greatest pains of Purgatory."</meta>

# E P I S T L E.

of the greatest pains of **Purgatory** <note>original: "Purgatorii." A reference to the Catholic doctrine of a place of purification after death, which Lavater, as a Protestant, is critically examining.</note> they may be set free. Many people, not only commoners but even distinguished men, wonder whether **Specters** <note>original: "Spectra."</note> exist, and exactly what they might be. Friends have also asked me more than once to declare my opinion on these matters to them.

It seems, therefore, that I shall be performing a worthwhile task if I set forth briefly and clearly what ought to be thought concerning these things, based on the **Word of God** <note>original: "verbo Dei."</note> and the testimony of proven writers. For the ministers of the **Churches** <note>original: "Ecclesiarum."</note> can do nothing more useful than to teach people purely and clearly from the **Word of God**—which, as the **Psalmist** <note>original: "Psaltes." Referring to the author of the Psalms, traditionally King David.</note> says, is a *Lamp unto our feet and a light to our paths* <note>A direct reference to Psalm 119:105.</note>—about necessary matters that have become subjects of controversy, so that they may free them from errors and **superstitions** <note>original: "superstitionibus."</note> and remove all doubt and hesitation from their minds. The zeal and diligence of those who for several years now have [written] books from the sacred [scriptures] against various errors is also to be highly praised...

<summary>Lavater expresses his intention to provide a clear, biblical perspective on ghosts and specters to resolve public confusion and combat superstition. He argues that church leaders have a duty to use Scripture to clarify controversial spiritual matters.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Ludwig Lavater. It transitions from praising the ministers of the Church to commending the scholars who translate and publish sacred works in common languages, highlighting the invention of the printing press as a divine tool for religious reform.</meta>

-># E P I S T L E.<-

[arranged] from the sacred scriptures <note>original: "literis." In this context, it refers to the study and arrangement of holy texts.</note>, they have published them in German and other languages. Likewise, [it praises] those who even in our own time teach, instruct, and strengthen the unlearned and inexperienced through the publication of books. For among many other excellent blessings that **God** <note>original: "Deus."</note>, our heavenly Father, has bestowed upon the human race, this one is also great and magnificent: that in this old age of the world <note>original: "senecta mundi." This reflects a common 16th-century belief that the world was in its final, declining days before the Last Judgment.</note>, He has brought to light not only so many good books but also the **sacred scriptures** <note>original: "sacras literas."</note> of the Old and New Testament <note>original: "Instrumenti." A term used by scholars like Erasmus to refer to the Bible as a legal or foundational document.</note> in various languages by the **art of printing** <note>original: "arte typographica." The Protestant Reformers viewed the printing press as a miraculous invention that allowed the Gospel to be spread without the interference of church hierarchy.</note>. Through these, He not only teaches us most fully what is to be believed and what is to be done, but also most forcefully attacks the various and manifold errors that have gradually crept into the **Church** <note>original: "Ecclesiam."</note>. Certainly, all those who do not recognize this immense blessing of His are deeply ungrateful toward God.

Moreover, this treatise of mine **Concerning Specters and Portents** <note>original: "De Spectris & ostentis." This is the title of Lavater's work, which explores ghosts and supernatural occurrences from a Protestant perspective.</note>, for the bett-

<meta>catchword: -er (original: "rem.")</meta>

<summary>Lavater praises the dissemination of religious books in vernacular languages and credits the invention of printing as a divine gift used to correct long-standing errors within the Church. He introduces his own work on ghosts and portents as a contribution to this educational effort.</summary>

<keywords>printing press, Old and New Testament, Church reform, education, vernacular translation, divine blessings, end of the world</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Ludwig Lavater's dedication. Having praised the power of the printing press to spread the Gospel, he now transitions to explaining the structure and purpose of his specific treatise on ghosts and spirits.</meta>

-># EPISTLE.<-

<margin>The parts of this book.</margin>
...phantoms, and for the full understanding of this matter, I have divided the work into three parts. In the first part, I show that **specters** <note>original: "Spectra." Lavater uses this term to refer to any supernatural apparition or ghostly sighting.</note> and spirits do exist, and that they sometimes appear to men; and also that many wonderful things happen beyond the **order of nature** <note>original: "naturae ordinem." In the 16th century, the distinction between "natural" events and "supernatural" or "preternatural" events was a central topic of scientific and religious debate.</note>. In the second part, I discuss what they actually are: not the souls of the dead <note>original: "animae vita defunctorum." This is a crucial Protestant argument; Lavater argues against the Catholic belief that ghosts are souls returning from Purgatory.</note>, as some people believe, but either good or evil **angels** <note>original: "angeli."</note>, or otherwise the secret and hidden operations of **God** <note>original: "Dei."</note>. In the third part, I explain why God sometimes allows specters to appear and various **omens** <note>original: "praesagia." Portents or signs believed to predict future events, often involving strange sightings or sounds.</note> to occur; likewise, how men ought to behave when they encounter such things. In these chapters are contained the principal matters that can be debated concerning this business.

Furthermore, I have sought to explain this obscure matter—which is entangled in many questions—by drawing from the oracles of the **sacred Scriptures** <note>original: "sacrae Scripturae oraculis." By calling the Bible "oracles," Lavater emphasizes that it is the direct and infallible word of God.</note> upon which we can rely, from the **ancient Fathers** <note>original: "vetustis Patribus." The early theologians of the Christian Church, such as Augustine or Jerome, whose authority was still highly respected by Reformers.</note>, from approved **historians** <note>original: "Historicis."</note> and other good writers, as well as from experience...

<summary>Ludwig Lavater outlines the three parts of his book, arguing that while specters exist, they are not the souls of the deceased but rather angelic or divine manifestations.</summary>

<keywords>specters, ghost stories, Protestant theology, angels, omens, Church Fathers, demonology, Purgatory, Ludwig Lavater</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Ludwig Lavater's dedicatory epistle. He is concluding his explanation of the book's method—promising a clear, fair analysis of supernatural phenomena—before listing other contemporary scholars who have written on related subjects like magic and divination.</meta>

-># EPISTLE.<-

...I will explain [this matter] so clearly and lucidly, I hope, that students and lovers of divine truth will understand what they should judge concerning those **apparitions** and marvelous things. I hope that even our adversaries, if they set aside their passions for a moment, will say that I have presented and refuted their arguments in **good faith** <note>original: "bona fide."</note>, omitting insults and curses. For according to the doctrine of St. Paul <note>original: "D. Pauli." The 'D.' stands for 'Divi' (Saint/Divine), a standard respectful prefix for biblical authors.</note>, I have set before me the goal of **Edification**, not **Destruction**. <note>original: "Ædificationem, non Diſſipatio-nem." A reference to 2 Corinthians 10:8, where Paul speaks of authority given for building up rather than tearing down.</note>

<margin>
Those who have written on this subject.
</margin>
Regarding divinations, superstitions, adjurations, blessings, deceptions <note>original: "præſtigiis." In this context, it refers to demonic illusions or "sleights of hand" that trick the human senses.</note>, incantations, and various forms of **witchcraft** <note>original: "veneficiis." While literally "poisonings," in the 16th century this term specifically referred to harmful magic or sorcery.</note>, and other works of the devil in general, several very learned men have written in our time: namely Caspar Peucer <note>A German physician and scholar whose work *Commentarius de praecipuis divinationum generibus* (1553) was a standard text on the various types of fortune-telling.</note>, Johann Weyer <note>original: "Joannes Vierus." A physician famous for his 1563 work *De praestigiis daemonum*, which argued that many accused witches were actually suffering from mental illness.</note>, Ludwig Milich <note>A Lutheran theologian who wrote *Schrapteufel* (1563), a work on the "Magic of the Devil."</note>, and perhaps others as well, whose labors I have not yet seen. Johannes Rivius <note>A German educator and theologian.</note>, a learned and elo- <meta>catchword: quens (The sentence continues on the next page with the word "eloquent")</meta>

<summary>Lavater promises a clear and fair-minded treatment of the supernatural, citing the biblical goal of edification. He acknowledges previous scholars like Peucer and Weyer who have investigated divination and demonic deceptions.</summary>
<keywords>divination, superstition, witchcraft, Caspar Peucer, Johann Weyer, Ludwig Milich, Johannes Rivius, edification vs. destruction</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle of Ludwig Lavater. He is continuing his survey of contemporary scholars who have addressed the topics of ghosts and the supernatural, transitioning into a discussion of his own editorial philosophy—aiming for clarity and brevity—while deferring to the greater expertise of the scholar Joachim Camerarius.</meta>

-># EPISTLE.<-
->* 7<-

...following, published a small book in the Latin language several years ago regarding **Specters** and **superstition**, in which he treats this matter and other superstitions briefly indeed, but as is his custom, quite elegantly and eloquently. Although I am writing much more extensively about this subject, I nevertheless did not wish to gather all those things which I could have said and brought forward regarding this argument. Instead, I have collected those which seem most important, partly so as not to cause the readers weariness <note>original: "fastidium." In this context, it suggests a sense of boredom or being overwhelmed by too much detail.</note>, and partly so that this book would not grow excessively large.

I hope that the most illustrious man **Joachim Camerarius** <note>A renowned German classical scholar (1500–1574) who was a central figure in the Renaissance recovery of ancient texts.</note>, who has studied the ancient Greek and Latin writers with singular judgment and possesses a great practical knowledge of things, will write elegantly and at length about this subject and others that have an affinity with it. For he promised as much in his preface to the small books of **Plutarch** <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer whose works were foundational to Renaissance thought.</note>, *On the Obsolescence of Oracles* <note>original: "De defectu oraculorum." This work by Plutarch explores why the famous ancient Greek oracles had fallen silent.</note> and *The Letter E at Delphi* <note>original: "figura E I consecrata Delphis." An essay exploring the cryptic symbol inscribed on the Temple of Apollo at Delphi.</note> (in which concerning [the nature]...

<meta>catchword: natura</meta>

<summary>Lavater explains that he has limited the scope of his book to avoid tiring the reader, while expressing his hope that the great scholar Joachim Camerarius will produce a more exhaustive work on ancient oracles and spirits as previously promised.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Camerarius, Plutarch, editorial brevity, oracles, Delphi, scholarly tradition</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle by Ludwig Lavater. Having just mentioned the scholar Joachim Camerarius and his work on Plutarch, Lavater now explains his decision to translate his own work from the original German into Latin to reach a wider audience. He then begins the formal dedication of the book to a high-ranking city official (the Consul).</meta>

-># EPISTLE.<-

...treats the nature and properties of demons <note>original: "natura & affectionibus dæmonum."</note>) and seems to promise as much in his other writings. I had previously written this treatise in the common tongue <note>original: "populari lingua," referring to German, the language of the general public in Lavater's Switzerland.</note>; but now, because I am confident it will bring benefit to others as well, I have translated both into the Latin language, adding certain things here and there.

<margin>Dedication</margin>
Truly, this book, which I have collected from many writings with great labor and study, I offer and dedicate to you, most distinguished Lord Consul <term>Consul: A high-ranking magistrate or chief administrative officer of a city-state, such as Zurich, where Lavater lived.</term>, according to ancient custom and practice. Not because I judge that you have need of my instruction regarding these matters which are treated in this book (for I am not unaware of the teachers from whom you learned true doctrine <note>original: "veram doctrinam." In the context of the 16th-century Reformation, this specifically refers to Protestant theology.</note>, and that, being skilled in many languages, you have studied good authors and continue to do so today); but partly so that this book of mine [might find favor] among others, to whom your faith, piety, and constancy—which you have shown until now in propagating true doctrine and defending good laws <note>original: "bonis legibus."</note>, and even still—

<meta>The catchword "often-" appears to be a fragment from an English translation or a misread; the Latin text ends with "etiamnum" (even now), likely leading into "ostendis" or "declaras" (you show/declare) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Ludwig Lavater explains that he translated this work from German into Latin for a broader scholarly audience and begins a formal dedication to a city Consul, praising the official's learning and commitment to the Reformation.</summary>
<keywords>translation, Latin, vernacular, dedication, Consul, true doctrine, Reformation, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory epistle to a high-ranking official in Bern. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the recipient's reputation for piety and defense of the law as a means to grant the book greater authority.</meta>

-># EPISTLE.<-

...[which you] show, is known and well-perceived, I might gain authority [for this work]; and partly so that I might show myself grateful to you in some way. For many of your benefits exist toward me—though I am unknown to you by face—and toward other ministers of the Churches, by which you have so bound me to you that I am unable to repay the debt.

Therefore, I ask you again and again that you do not reject this expression, such as it is, of a grateful and well-wishing mind; but rather, when you are somewhat freed from the affairs and labors of the Republic <term>Republic: Refers to the city-state of Bern, which was a sovereign territory within the Swiss Confederacy.</term>, you might deign to read through this book. I hope that, because of its clear arrangement and the many histories <note>original: "historias." In this context, Lavater refers to the various documented accounts and anecdotes of ghosts and spirits he has collected.</note> which I recount, it will be not unpleasant for you and others to read.

God, most good and great <note>original: "Deus optimus maximus." A common Renaissance formatting of the divine title, echoing the Roman address to Jupiter but applied here to the Christian God.</note>, who has so filled you with His gifts that because of them—though you are still a young man—you have been promoted to the highest ranks of dignity in your most distinguished republic, keep you, together with your family, safe for a long time for the preservation of the most distinguished city and territory of Bern <note>original: "ditionis Bernensis."</note>.

<meta>catchword and</meta>

<summary>Lavater concludes his dedication by expressing personal gratitude for the recipient's support of the clergy and praying for the continued prosperity of the city-state of Bern. He highlights that his book is organized clearly and filled with historical examples to make it an engaging read for the busy official.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, gratitude, Bern, Republic, God most good and great, histories, church ministers</keywords>

and may [He] deign to keep [you] safe and to increase and enrich [you] with His gifts. My lords and brothers, the ministers of the Church of Zurich <note>original: "ecclesiæ Tigurinæ." Zurich was commonly referred to by the Latin name Tigurum.</note>, send their greetings to you; likewise, that man distinguished in learning, virtue, and noble birth, Georgius Grebel <note>Georgius Grebelius (George Grebel) was a member of a prominent Zurich family; his inclusion here highlights the social and intellectual networks connecting the cities of Zurich and Bern.</note>, your old companion <term>companion: The Latin "contubernalis" refers to a close associate, often a roommate or comrade-in-arms, suggesting the recipient and Grebel shared a close bond during their studies or earlier careers.</term>.

### *On the words that often occur in the subject of ghosts and various forewarnings.*

### FIRST PART

book, and especially in reading the ancient authors, they may better understand everything.

<margin>Spectrum</margin>
*Spectrum* <note>original: "Spectrum"</note> among the Latins signifies a phantom or an image presenting itself to the eyes. *Scaliger* <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated Italian scholar and physician.</note> says that a *Spectrum* is that which presents itself, either in reality or through a corrupted imagination. Theologians accept it as an incorporeal substance which, when seen or heard, strikes terror into men.

<margin>Vision</margin>
*Visum* <note>original: "Visum," literally "a thing seen."</note> signifies a *Spectrum* or some appearance which we seem to see while sleeping and even while awake: as we read concerning *Brutus*, who saw his own Spirit <note>original: "Genium." Reference to the famous apparition seen by Marcus Junius Brutus before the Battle of Philippi.</note>. *Cicero*, in the first book of his *Academic Questions*, writes that a vision is called a *phantasia* <note>Greek: φαντασία</note> by the Greeks.

<margin>Vision</margin>
The Latins also call *Visiones* <note>original: "Visiones"</note> that which the Greeks call *phantasias*.

<margin>Bugbear</margin>
*Terriculamenta* <note>original: "Terriculamenta," derived from the Latin "terrere," to frighten.</note> are phantasms or *Spectra* that strike terror; the Latins also use the word *terricula* for that which causes fear.

<margin>Phantasm</margin>
That which St. Matthew in his 14th chapter and Mark in his 6th call a *phantasma* <note>Greek: φάντασμα; referring to the disciples seeing Jesus walking on the water and thinking he was a ghost.</note>, *Erasmus* translates as *Spectrum*. The ancient translator <note>A reference to the Vulgate, the late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible.</note> retained the Greek word.

<margin>Appearance</margin>
*Phasma* <note>Greek: Φάσμα</note> also signifies a ghost <note>original: "larvam"</note>, a sight, or a *Spectrum*. *Suidas* <note>The author of the Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> distinguishes between a *phantasma* and a *phantasia*, stating that phan-

He establishes that these phenomena can be real or products of the imagination, but are unified by the terror they inspire.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological categorization of supernatural entities. ### ON GHOSTS.

...a phantasm <note>original: "phantasma"</note> is an imagination, a form, or a vision of that which does not exist, such as those things which people seem to see while sleeping; however, a *fantasy* <note>original: "phantasia"</note> is only of that which truly exists.
<margin>Spirit <note>Greek: πνεῦμα (pneuma)</note></margin> In the 24th chapter of *Luke*, the word *Spirit* <note>Greek: πνεῦμα (pneuma)</note> is used to mean a soul or a ghost <note>original: "spectro"</note>. However, some qualifying word is almost always added if it is meant to signify evil spirits, such as "many evil spirits" <note>Greek: πνεύματα, πλεῖνα, πονηρὰ (pneumata, pleina, ponera)</note>.
The Pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>, as *Augustine* and other Church Fathers testify, believed that the souls of men became demons <note>The author uses "dæmonas" in the classical sense of a spirit, not necessarily an evil one.</note>. If they had been of good merit, they were called *Lares*; if of evil merit, they were called either *Lemures* or *Larvae*.
But if it were uncertain whether they were of good or evil merit, then they were called *Manes* <note>Roman spirits of the dead, often worshipped as ancestral deities.</note>. *Apuleius* <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), author of "The Golden Ass" and "On the God of Socrates."</note> and others of the ancients have recorded that these were the same as the *genii* and the *Lares*.
<margin>Lares. Guardians.</margin> *Festus* <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian whose work is a key source for ancient Latin terms.</note> testifies that they thought the *Lares* were the souls of men, or inferior gods. They were called *Guardian Lares* <note>original: "Lares præstites"</note> because they kept all things safe before their eyes; that is, they preserved and protected them.
<margin>Hostilii.</margin> They say they were also named *Hostilii* <note>Derived from "hostis," meaning enemy.</note> because they were thought to keep enemies at bay. They were believed to be in charge of guarding not only private homes and crossroads, but even the city itself. They were likewise worshipped privately in houses and publicly at street corners. See more concerning the *Lares* in the *Commentary*.

<meta>catchword: mentary.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the differences between phantasms and fantasies and explores Greco-Roman classifications of spirits, such as Lares, Lemures, and Manes, based on the moral merit of the deceased.</summary>

FIRST PART

## Commentaries of Antonio Constanzi of Faenza,
### on the book of Ovid's *Fasti*. <note>Ovid’s *Fasti* is a Latin poem that explains the Roman calendar and the origins of various religious festivals.</note>

<margin>Genius.</margin>
The *Genius*, as the Grammarians say, is the natural God <note>original: "naturalis Deus"</note> of every place, thing, or person. They write that when we are born, we are allotted two *genii*: one of whom urges us toward good, while the other leads us toward evil. *Censorinus* <note>A 3rd-century Roman scholar and author of *De Die Natali*</note> says: "The *Genius* is the God under whose protection a person lives from the moment they are born: either because it sees to it that we are conceived, or because it is born together with us, or because it takes us up and protects us once we are born." Certainly, the *genius* is so named from the word for "begetting" <note>original: "à gignendo"</note>.

<margin>Penates.</margin>
The *Penates* <note>Deities who specifically protected the inner parts of the home, particularly the pantry</note> are also domestic gods. *Macrobius* <note>A late Roman writer known for his encyclopedic works on myth and language</note> says they are the gods through whom we breathe from within <note>original: "penitus spiramus"</note>, through whom we have a body, and through whom we possess the faculty of reason. *Nonius* <note>Nonius Marcellus, a Roman lexicographer and grammarian</note> says that *lemures* are nocturnal phantoms <note>original: "larvas"</note>, and the terrifying appearances of images and beasts. Others say *lemures* are harmful images that appear at night; they are also said to be the shadows of those who have been struck down or insulted <note>original: "sugillatorum umbras"</note>.

<margin>Shadows.</margin>
Some call the shadows of all the dead *lemures*. *Apuleius* <note>The author of *The Golden Ass*, who also wrote on Platonic philosophy and spirits</note> says: "Of those *lemures* who are allotted the care of their descendants, and who occupy the home with a peaceful and quiet name, they are called the *lar familiaris* <note>The ancestral spirit who guards the household</note>." And since among the ancients, just as...

# ON SPECTERS.

...just as the *Lares* <note>Protective household gods</note> were considered good, so the *lemures* were considered evil, and sacred rites were performed to ward them off.

<margin>Lemures.</margin>
Others say *lemures* are shadows that remain with bodies. *Porphyry* <note>A 3rd-century scholar and philosopher</note> calls them "wandering shadows" <note>original: "vagantes umbras"</note> of those who died before their time. He says they were named after *Remus* as if they were "Remures," whose shadow pursued his brother *Romulus*; when Romulus wished to appease him, he established the festival of the *Lemuria* <note>A Roman festival held in May to exorcise the hungry ghosts of the dead from the home</note>. *Servius* <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> says that these shadows are called *larvae* <note>Malevolent spirits of the dead who were thought to frighten the living</note>. Indeed, they called the souls of the dead "shadows." From the word *larvae* comes the term *larvati*, meaning those who are of unsound mind and troubled by the incursion of ghosts. According to *Nonius* <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian</note>, these people are also called *Ceriti* <note>Derived from the goddess Ceres; it refers to people struck with madness or "moonstruck" by divine influence</note>.

*Servius* says the *Manes* <note>The ancestral spirits of the dead, often treated as minor deities</note> are souls at that time when they have left one body but have not yet passed into another <note>This suggests a belief in the transmigration of souls or a state of limbo</note>. He believes they are named by "antiphrasis" <note>A figure of speech where a word is used to mean its opposite</note> from the ancient adjective *manus*, meaning "good"—because they were not good at all. For the ancients thought the *manes* were gods of the underworld and counted them among the "unfavorable deities" <note>original: "numina læva"</note>; they appeased them with sacrifices so they would not cause harm. Some say the *manes* are good gods of the underworld, and from this comes the word *mane*, which means "good." Thus, the "divine manes" <note>original: "dii manes"</note> are seen as good and prosperous; from this we also get the word *immanes*, meaning "not good" <note>While *immanes* usually means "monstrous" or "huge," the author is explaining it as "not-manus" (not-good)</note>. Others think that souls separated from their bodies were called by this name...

### FIRST PART

...whence we see old tomb monuments inscribed to the "Divine Manes" <note>original: "diis manibus"; a standard Roman funerary dedication to the ancestral spirits of the deceased</note>. I mentioned shortly before that *Apuleius* <note>A 2nd-century Platonic philosopher and author who wrote extensively on the nature of demons and spirits</note> held this opinion. There are those who think the *manes* are the same as what the ancients called *genii* <note>Attendant spirits or "inner sparks" assigned to a person</note>, and that two *manes* were assigned to bodies from the very moment of conception; these do not leave the body even in death, and even when the bodies are consumed by decay, they inhabit the tombs. Therefore, those who demolished tombs were thought to violate the Divine Manes. The Augurs <note>Religious officials who interpreted the will of the gods</note> called both the celestial and the infernal gods "manes" because they believed all things flow <note>original: "manare"</note> through them, according to the author *Festus* <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian whose work is a major source for ancient Latin terms</note>. Others [derive the name] from "flowing" because the places between the lunar and terrestrial circles, from which they flow down, are full of souls.

<margin>Maniae.</margin>
*Maniae* <note>In Roman folklore, these were ugly or terrifying female spirits, often used as a "bogeyman" figure</note> are deformed personas, according to *Festus*. Likewise, they are *larvae* <note>Malevolent spirits of the dead</note>, which nurses are accustomed to use to threaten children.

*Momo* <note>original: Μομώ; a female spirit or monster in Greek folk belief</note> is a woman with an almost monstrous face, a *lamia*; hence it stands for a *larva*: just like a *mormolykeion* <note>original: μορμολύκειον; a frightening mask or a bugbear used to scare children</note>, a bugbear, *spectrum*, or *larva*. *Nicephorus* <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine historian</note> in his *Ecclesiastical History* says that a nocturnal woman is called a *Gilo* <note>Also known as Gyllo or Gello, a child-stealing demon in Greek mythology</note>.

<margin>Lamiae.</margin>
*Lamiae* <note>In antiquity, these were mythical monsters, often depicted as women who preyed on children or seduced young men to consume them</note> were thought by the ancients to be women with removable eyes, or certain phantasms of demons, which

# ON SPECTERS

...having assumed the appearance of beautiful women, they devoured children and youths alike, lured by their charms. *Philostratus* <note>Flavius Philostratus, a 3rd-century Greek scholar, in his *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*</note> writes a marvelous story—or perhaps a fable—concerning a certain *Menippus*, who was loved by a *lamia* <note>A female demon who, in classical mythology, was said to prey on children and seduce young men</note>. The same author writes that *lamiae* are called *larvae* by some, *lemures* <note>Restless spirits of the dead</note> by others, and *empusas* <note>A shape-shifting female demon in Greek mythology, often associated with Hecate</note> by still others: names that nurses use as a "bugbear" to frighten infants, and so on. *Dion Chrysostom* <note>A 1st-century Greek orator and philosopher</note> writes that in the heart of Africa there are certain wild beasts with women’s faces, which are also called *lamiae*; their breasts and their entire chests are so beautiful that no painter’s skill could represent them. By baring these, they entice men into a trap and devour those they have captured. *In the Lamentations of Jeremiah, chapter 4*, it is said: "The *lamiae* <note>The Latin Vulgate uses the word *lamiae* here; most English Bibles translate this as 'sea monsters' or 'jackals'</note> bared their breasts, etc." *Apuleius* writes that *lamiae* are things used to frighten children.

<margin>Striges.</margin>
*Lamiae* are also called *Striges* <note>Screech-owls or "night-hags," derived from the Greek *strix*</note>. People say *striges* are ill-omened birds that suck the blood of infants in their cradles; for this reason, the name is given to wicked women <note>witches or sorceresses</note>, whom *Festus* writes are also called *volaticae* <note>original: "volaticas"; meaning "winged" or "flying ones"</note>.

<margin>Gorgons.</margin>
The name of the *Gorgon* was devised for the frightening of children. They say the *Gorgons* are voracious, just as the *Lamiae* are imagined to be.

### PART ONE

<margin>Incubi. Succubae.</margin>
*Nightmares* and *morning-mares* <note>original: *Ephialtæ* & *hyphialtæ*</note>, *Incubi* and *Succubi* <note>Demons traditionally thought to lie upon or beneath sleepers; "Incubi" were considered male and "Succubi" female</note>, are nocturnal *Specters* or assaulting demons. Physicians say this is a disease <note>Referring to sleep paralysis, which was historically attributed to demonic pressure on the chest</note>.

<margin>Empusa.</margin>
*Empusa*, a *demon-like phantom* or *Specter* that forces itself upon the wretched and changes its form in many ways, and shows itself most often at noon. More on this can be found in *Suidas* <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note>.

<margin>Dicelon. Hecataea.</margin>
*Dicelon* <note>A term for a frightening apparition</note>, so named because it is sent to strike terror: *Apollonius* <note>Likely Apollonius of Tyana, a 1st-century philosopher</note> writes that the Greeks call this class of terrors *Hecataea*, because they are sent by *Hecate* or *Proserpina* <note>The goddess of the underworld and magic</note>. For this reason, she is also called *Bemo* <note>original: βεμὼ</note> from the word for "frightening" <note>original: ἀπὸ <unclear>τ͂</unclear> βεμῶν</note>, surely because they believed nocturnal terrors were sent by her.

<margin>Acco. Alphito.</margin>
*Plutarch* writes that *Acco* and *Alphito* were monstrous women by whom mothers kept their children in line and deterred them from misbehaving <note>These were nursery bogies used as threats to ensure children's obedience</note>.

<margin>Telchines</margin>
*Cardano* <note>Girolamo Cardano, a 16th-century Italian polymath who wrote extensively on the supernatural</note> calls those subterranean demons who crush miners with falling ruins *Telchines*. Among the Greeks, *telchines* <note>original: τελχῖνες; historically, these were also mythical smiths and sorcerers</note> are called wicked men, inspired by a spirit and driven mad.

To this category belong monsters reflecting a form partly human and partly beast-like, which they say are found in the woods and have often appeared to farmers.

### ON SPECTERS

...ruisse <note>The conclusion of a word from the previous page, likely describing the actions of the Telchines.</note> <margin>Pan. Fauns.</margin> They say that **Panes** and **Fauns** are the same, and that they end in the feet of a goat <note>Forest deities in Greek and Roman mythology associated with nature, often depicted with goat horns and legs</note>.

<margin>Satyrs. Sileni.</margin> They also report that **Satyrs** are of almost human form; those who are of a more mature age, they call **Sileni** <note>In mythology, Satyrs were youthful spirits of the wild, while Sileni were older, wiser, and often drunken companions of the wine god Dionysus</note>.

<margin>Onocentaur.</margin> The *Onocentaur* is a monstrous animal which is said to have the appearance of a human in its upper part, and that of a donkey in its lower part <note>From the Greek "onos" (donkey) and "centaur"</note>.

<margin>Onosceli.</margin> According to Plutarch, the *Onosceli* are demons with donkey legs.

<margin>Hippocentaur.</margin> Antiquity invented *Hippocentaurs*, representing a human in the upper part and a horse in the lower <note>Commonly known today simply as Centaurs</note>.

<margin>Sphinxes.</margin> They imagine the **Sphinx** to be a human-shaped <note>original: *ἀνθρωπόμορφον* (anthrōpomorphon)</note> animal.

<margin>Scylla. Harpies.</margin> *Scylla* and the *Harpies* are predatory demons with the faces of young women <note>Scylla was a sea monster, and Harpies were bird-like spirits of sudden gusts of wind</note>.

<margin>Triton. Nereids. Sirens.</margin> Regarding the "sea-humans"—*Tritons*, *Nereids*, and *Sirens*—to whom antiquity attributed human faces, see *Gesner* in his history of aquatic animals in the appropriate sections <note>Conrad Gesner (1516–1565) was a Swiss naturalist; his monumental "History of Animals" attempted to categorize all known creatures, including those reported by ancient sailors</note>. He proves from many authors that sea monsters are found which in some way reflect the human form. Some of these monsters belong to the genus of apes, and some are purely mythical; nevertheless, the Devil can still delude humans under the appearance of such things.

# FIRST PART

These are the terms regarding the words which must be used in the discussion of spirits and Specters <note>original: *spiritibus & Spectris*</note>.

<margin>Omens</margin>
To these I have joined **omens** or **prophecies** <note>original: *ostenta vel præfagia*</note> which generally precede great events. I connect them to these subjects because they share a close affinity with them. For certain **Specters** are also presented to the senses: armed men are seen on the earth or in the sky; likewise other images; voices are heard, as well as noises, clashing, and other things of that kind.

<margin>Portent</margin>
Regarding the specific words themselves, a **Portent** <note>original: *Portentum*</note> is that which "portends" something in the future, such as when unusual bodies appear in the sky—comets, thunder in a clear sky, or whirlwinds. **Festus** <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian who wrote a major dictionary of Latin terms</note> says that a Portent is something which, although natural, rarely reveals itself and announces a future event after an interval of time.

<margin>Omen</margin>
An **Omen** <note>original: *Ostentum*, literally a "showing"</note> is some unusual thing that immediately shows something is about to happen. They <note>likely referring to grammarians or ancient authorities</note> provide the same examples for this.

<margin>Prodigy</margin>
A ***Prodigy*** <note>original: *Prodigium*</note> is something that, even if it occurs naturally and happens often, always foretells some evil, as if something must be "driven forth" <note>original: *quasi porrò agendum*; the author is using a traditional etymology linking the word to future action or driving a warning forward</note>.

<margin>Monster</margin>
A **Monster** <note>original: *Monstrum*</note> is that which occurs against nature, such as when a creature is born with the limbs of another species; which for a monster

<meta>catchword: also</meta>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of supernatural signs (portents, omens, prodigies) and moves into a philosophical and theological defense of the existence of spirits, specifically addressing the skeptical views of natural philosophers and Epicureans.</meta>

# ON SPECTERS

...it is also called a **Monster** <note>original: *monstrum*</note>, as if pointing out <note>original: *monstrans*</note> or warning <note>original: *monens*</note> of something further ahead. However, these terms are often confused with one another and are taken to have the same meaning: namely, that they have the same purpose, which is to predict or signify future things.

The **Specters** <note>original: *Spectra*</note> of which we treat have nothing to do with natural philosophers <term>physicos: scholars who study the physical world and natural laws rather than theology</term>, nor do those things which we have joined to them. For if a sudden crash, sound, roar, or noise occurs—as if the whole house were collapsing—and these things happen by natural causes, they do not properly belong to this subject. But these things having been mentioned beforehand, we shall proceed to the matter itself, with God as our leader and author.

## CHAPTER II.

->***Those suffering from melancholy and madness imagine***<-
->***many things to themselves which do not truly exist.***<-

<margin>Some deny that Specters exist</margin>
There have been many in every age who denied that Spirits or Specters exist. The **Epicureans** <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Epicurus; in the Renaissance, this term was often used for those who denied the immortality of the soul or the presence of the supernatural in the world</note> laughed at everything reported about them, regarding them as fictions and mockeries with which children, the unlearned, and the sim-

# FIRST PART

...ple-minded people might be terrified. *Cassius*, who was also an *Epicurean*, when he understood from the account of *Brutus* that he had seen a certain *Specter*, according to the testimony of *Plutarch*, attempted to refer everything to natural causes. In the *Acts of the Apostles, chapter 23*, it is read that the *Sadducees* did not believe in a future resurrection of the dead; likewise, they denied that *Spirits and Angels* exist. Today as well, many pious and good men hold all those things which are said back and forth about *Specters* to be fables, partly because no such things appeared to them in their whole lifetime; partly, and especially, because in several past centuries, men were so often deceived by *Monks* and petty priests <note>original: *sacrificulis* — a disparaging term for those who perform sacrifices, used here by the author to criticize Catholic clergy</note> with fabricated apparitions, visions, and miracles, that now they consider true things to be false as well. Whatever the case may be, it can be proven by many testimonies of writers and by daily experience that *Spirits and Specters* sometimes appear, and that many marvelous things truly happen. It is indeed certain that many falsely persuade themselves that they see or hear *Specters*; for that which they imagine themselves to see or hear arises from melancholy <term>melancholia: a medical condition characterized by an excess of black bile, thought to cause hallucinations and dark moods</term>, madness, a weakness of the senses, fear, and other emotions; or [they mistake] animals, exhalations <note>original: *exhalationes* — vapors or gases rising from the earth, often thought to create visual illusions</note>, and other things...

### ON SPECTERS.
B 3

...natural things, which, while seeing and hearing, they imagine to be some kind of vision presented to them—a fact I will subsequently demonstrate one by one with many illustrious examples. It is by no means to be doubted that nearly all those things which are commonly thought to be *Specters* <term>Spectra: Latin for apparitions or ghosts; the author distinguishes between psychological delusions and actual spirits</term> are nothing of the sort. Meanwhile, it is by no means to be denied that *Specters* and many other things of this kind are indeed sometimes seen and heard.

<margin>The various imaginings of the melancholic.</margin>
In the first place, no one can deny that men who are sad and melancholic—either by their natural temperament or because of great calamities they have suffered—imagine wondrous things for themselves. Sometimes they solemnly affirm that they hear and see these or those things, which nevertheless neither they <note>meaning: they do not actually see them with their physical eyes, only in their mind</note> nor anyone else truly sees or hears. This is what we sometimes experience in those who are afflicted by great headaches, or are subject to other bodily diseases, or who pass sleepless nights, or are of a disturbed mind. Those who live with such people, although they pity their unhappy condition, nevertheless often cannot keep from laughing when they hear from them such absurd tales, unheard-of things, and marvelous visions. *Aristotle writes in his book on marvelous things* <note>original: "de rebus mirandis" — likely referring to the work *De mirabilibus auscultationibus* (On Marvelous Things Heard) attributed to Aristotle</note>*, concerning a certain man of unsound mind* <term>mente capto: literally "captured in mind," a common Latin phrase for insanity or being mentally overwhelmed</term>*, who*

When his relatives had cured him through the efforts of doctors, he took it most poorly, saying that he had never lived more pleasantly than when he was not in his right mind. Athenaeus <note>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian</note> writes in his twelfth book about a certain Thrasylaus <note>original: *Thrasilao*</note>, who possessed such a damaged brain that he believed all the ships that docked in the port of Piraeus were his own. He used to say that throughout that entire time in which he had been out of his mind, he had led a sweet life, until a doctor freed him from this insanity.

The same logic applies to others who are seized in their mind: either because they have turned a certain matter over in their mind for a long time and at great length, as sometimes happens to clever craftsmen; or because they have suffered from a long-lasting illness, or because they burn with love, etc. All of them handle strange things. You will find one who seems to himself to be armed with bull’s horns; others seem to themselves to be earthenware vessels, and therefore they move out of the way lest they be broken. Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose work dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> writes of such a person in Book 3 of *On the Affected Parts* <note>original: "de locis affectis"</note>, chapter 6, and likewise in Book 4, chapter 1. He thought that certain men had occupied a corner of the house where he lay down, and that they were constantly playing music and beating out rhythms. He believed he saw them—some standing there, others sitting—and they played so incessantly that he could not find even the slightest moment of rest, whether by day or by night. Consequently, he never stopped crying out and ordering that they be thrown out of the house. After he had fully recovered, he recounted everything that everyone had said or done during his illness, and he specifically remembered the exhaustion caused by that imaginary annoyance of the pipers <note>original: "tibicinum"; referring to flute-players or musicians</note>.

<margin>Lycanthropy</margin>
*Paul of Aegina <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician whose medical encyclopedia was highly influential in the Middle Ages and Renaissance</note> writes in Book 3, chapter 15* that those seized by Lycanthropy (which is a type of insanity) leap out of their houses at night. They imitate wolves in every way, and until dawn, they mostly frequent the monuments of the dead. At other times, Satan, the enemy of the human race, deceives men so that they seem to themselves to be beasts. One can read about this matter in *Augustine’s <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, the influential early Christian theologian</note> The Literal Meaning of Genesis, Book 7, chapter 11.*

<margin>Hydrophobia</margin>
Those bitten by rabid dogs dread water; they call this condition *Hydrophobia* <note>From the Greek words for "water" and "fear"</note>. Concerning this, *Paul of Aegina* reports in *Book 5, chapter 3* that those suffering from this evil flee when they see water or when it is brought near; others turn away from absolutely all moisture.

Ephialtes, which they call the *Incubus* <note>Known today as sleep paralysis, historically interpreted as a demon or spirit sitting on a sleeper's chest</note>, is a disease of the stomach; consult Paul of Aegina <note>A 7th-century Byzantine physician</note>, Book 3, chapter 16, regarding this. Indeed, many who are seized by it believe they are being weighed down by an enormous man, who violently presses their mouths with his hands so that they cannot cry out. They move their arms and legs to drive him away, but in vain. Some, led by an empty fantasy, believe that the one by whom they are being oppressed climbs up through the bed little by little and as if by ambush, then soon after descends again; they even seem to hear him. By another name, this disease is called *pnigalion* and *pnegmon* <note>Greek: πνιγαλίων and πνηγμων; terms referring to a 'suffocator' or the sensation of choking</note>.

This is something we experience in the hospitals <note>original: "Prochodochiis"; referring to ptochodochia or early infirmaries/hospices for the poor and sick</note> where the mad and maniacs are kept. <unclear>F</unclear>or indeed, if anyone is fearful by nature, or is struck by fear due to great dangers or other causes, they imagine many wondrous things to themselves which in reality do not exist—especially if an excess of black bile <note>original: "atrae bilis"; in the medical theory of the four humors, an excess of black bile was thought to cause "melancholy," leading to depression and hallucinations.</note> is involved. Women, who for the most part are more fearful by nature than men (hence St. Peter, when he speaks of the duty of spouses in his first Epistle, calls them the "weaker vessel" <note>A reference to 1 Peter 3:7.</note>), persuade themselves more frequently than men do that they have seen or heard this or that. Likewise younger people, because they are often afraid. If any mention of specters and ghosts <note>original: "spectrorum & lemurum"; *lemures* were the restless or malevolent spirits of the dead in Roman mythology.</note> is made at the table, women and children sometimes hardly dare to leave the room alone, fearing that something might encounter them; if they hear any noise, they think Specters are behind them because of this empty belief. A cowardly soldier thinks the enemy forces are more numerous than they are; the sound of a moving leaf (a fate which the Lord, in His Law, threatens the People of Israel with if they do not do their duty <note>A reference to Leviticus 26:36: "the sound of a shaken leaf shall chase them."</note>) so terrifies him that he takes to his heels. However, sometimes even brave soldiers in flight, having become separated from their comrades, are so disturbed in their minds that they mistake friends for enemies, and do not know where on earth they are or which way to turn: all of which fear brings about.

*Plutarch <note>A famous Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–120 AD) whose works were widely read in the Renaissance for their moral lessons.</note> writes in his short book On the Delayed Vengeance of the Deity <note>original: "de sera numinis vindicta"; a treatise exploring why divine punishment sometimes takes a long time to strike the wicked.</note>, a wondrous and memorable story about a certain Bessus. When he had killed his father, he remained in hiding for a long time. But when he was once going to dinner, he struck a swallows' nest with a spear and knocked it down. When those dining with him expressed their horror at this cruelty (for we turn away from those who harass little birds and other living creatures, believing them to be savage and cruel)* "Do they not," he replied, "already for a long time bear false witness against me, crying out that I killed my father?" *Those present, moved by wonder, reported these words to the King, who subjected him to torture; and after the matter was diligently investigated, he exacted the punishment for parricide <note>term: parricidium (parricide); the act of killing one's father, mother, or close relative, historically considered one of the most' grievous crimes.</note> from him.* From this you may gather what fear is capable of: the swallows were not able to speak, yet he convinced himself that they were reproaching him for his parricide. Likewise, many imagine themselves seeing and hearing many things out of fear, when they are

<margin>King Theodoric thinks he sees Symmachus.</margin>
*Procopius <note>Procopius of Caesarea (c. 500–565 AD) was a principal Byzantine historian who chronicled the wars of Justinian.</note> relates at the beginning of the Italian War, that while Theodoric <note>Theodoric the Great (454–526 AD), King of the Ostrogoths, who ruled Italy.</note> was dining—after the excellent men Boethius and his father-in-law Symmachus <note>Boethius was a famous philosopher and statesman; both he and Symmachus were executed by Theodoric on charges of conspiracy, an act for which Theodoric was said to have felt deep remorse.</note> had been killed—when the head of a fish was served, the horrifying face of Symmachus was seen in it. The face, with its lower lip bitten down, seemed to threaten him with grim eyes. Therefore the King, overcome with terror, fell into a sickness from which he later died.*

Indeed, if fear is excessive, it causes us to shudder at those very things which ought to bring us consolation. We have an example in the very Apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ in the 14th chapter of Matthew. *When they were in danger at night on a very large lake <note>The Sea of Galilee.</note>, they were greatly dismayed seeing Christ approaching them, walking on the water. For they thought they saw a Specter <note>original: "Spectrum"; here meaning a ghost or phantom.</note>, and they cried out for fear.* But the Lord was coming so that he might free them from their present danger. After the resurrection, when he appeared to them in his true body, they were greatly terrified and, as *Luke writes, they thought they saw a spirit.* And so the Lord consoles and raises them up, saying: *"See my hands and my feet, for it is I myself. Touch me and see: because a spirit [does not have] flesh..."* <note>The sentence concludes on the following page: "...and bones, as you see me have." (Luke 24:39).</note>

<margin>*The strange visions of the drunk*</margin>
Those who drink wine immoderately, so that their eyes begin to grow misty, see things far differently than sober men. They see things projecting from their heads—much like the hares we have carried for a mile or two while they were suspended from a pole—and they see things that do not exist. They think they see two lamps placed on the table when there is only one; when they wish to take up a cup, they miss it. *Pentheus in the Bacchae of Euripides* <note>A famous Greek tragedy. Pentheus, the King of Thebes, is driven mad by the god Dionysus as punishment for refusing to honor him.</note> says that he sees two suns and a double Thebes, for his brain was disturbed. It is a common saying that if wine has conquered a man, everything seems to be turned upside down: trees seem to walk, mountains to move, and rivers to be carried upward.

*Solomon, in his Proverbs, chapter 23,* <note>Specifically Proverbs 23:33.</note> discourages everyone from drunkenness, adding what inconveniences arise from it; among other things, he says: *Your eyes shall behold strange things—namely, visions—and wonderful apparitions.* For just as the fearful <note>original: "meticulosi"; the text cuts off here as a catchword for the next page.</note>

->24<-

[fearful] people imagine wonders for themselves, just as drunkards do, who deliberately corrupt their own sight.

And although God brings forth many wonders <note>original: "prodigia"; in this context, it refers to omens or supernatural signs intended as divine warnings.</note> in the air and on the earth to shake men out of their lethargy and provoke them to true repentance, we must nevertheless believe that drunkards, who sit at the table late into the night, often claim that this or that vision appeared to them, or that they saw this or that marvel, when they are utterly mistaken. Indeed, if they had returned home in good time, as was proper, and had not burdened themselves with wine, nothing of that kind would have appeared to them; for their eyes would not have been corrupted. Does it not happen that when men are warmed by pure wine, they mistake one person for another—believing they have been injured by words or deeds—and immediately seize weapons to attack them? 

The passage from Solomon <note>Referring to Proverbs 23:33.</note> already cited can also carry this meaning: *Your eyes shall behold strange things*—meaning women, specifically to lust after them. For experience teaches that drunkards frequently accost matrons and virgins with talk of debauchery <note>original: "stupro"; referring to illicit sexual acts or unchastity.</note>, something that would not even enter the minds of sober men. Wine taken immoderately is the fuel <note>original: "fomes"; literally "kindling" or "tinder," a common metaphor for what ignites passion or sin.</note> of audacity.

<summary>This page argues that many reported visions and supernatural "wonders" are actually the result of alcohol-induced hallucinations. It cautions that drunkenness not only deceives the eyes but also fuels aggressive behavior and illicit lust, distorting the truth of what a person sees.</summary>

and lust.

<margin>Certain people see themselves.</margin>
Aristotle writes that some people, because of the weak spirits <note>original: "spiritus"; in the science of the time, these were subtle fluids or vapors thought to flow through the nerves and eyes to enable sight.</note> of their eyes, see their own image in the nearby air—just as if they were looking in a mirror—and mistakenly believe they have seen their own spirits <note>original: "manes"; in Roman belief, the "manes" were the spirits of the dead or ancestral ghosts.</note>. Thus, as the saying goes, they "fear their own shadow." Do not men, even if they can see nothing in dark places, still imagine for themselves that they see various phantoms and colors? Those very things we do see, we often imagine to be far different from what they truly are.

It is well known that the sharpness of the eyes can be so dazzled <note>original: "perstringi"; to be blinded or momentarily overwhelmed by light or a trick of the eye.</note> that we entirely believe someone is swallowing a sword, spitting out money, coals, and other things; that someone eats bread and spits out flour; that someone drinks wine which later seems to flow out of his forehead; that one man cuts off another's head and then restores it to its place; or that a rooster pulls a massive beam, and so forth.

Furthermore, by natural means—such as through fumigations <note>original: "suffimigationibus"; the burning of specific herbs or chemicals to create smoke that was believed to cause hallucinations or distort the air.</note> and other methods—it can be brought about that you would solemnly swear everyone sitting at the table lacks a head, or that a grapevine is spreading itself through the room, when it is mere imposture <note>original: "impostura"; referring to stage magic, sleight of hand, or "natural magic" rather than demonic sorcery.</note>. Books on this subject are publicly available.

It emphasizes rational explanations for ghosts, such as Aristotle's theory of "weak spirits" and the use of herbal smoke to create mass hallucinations.</summary>

<page-num>16</page-num>
->### PART ONE<-

The same logic applies to hearing and the other senses. Those whose hearing is impaired sometimes seem to themselves to hear a rushing wind, a violent storm, the crackling of flames, the sudden roar of rising rivers, singing and musical instruments, or the ringing of bells, even though these and other sounds occur only because of a defect in their hearing. Others who are with them, and whose hearing is healthy, hear none of those things at all.

Sometimes something is indeed heard, such as the creaking of wooden walls and other sounds, which are physical signs of an approaching storm. There are also hollow places from which air <note>original: "spiritus"; in this context, moving air or vapors within the earth.</note> is emitted, which produces a sweet sound through pipes, like singing, so as to draw men into wonder.

We read among Natural Philosophers <note>original: "Physicos"; early scientists who studied the physical laws of the world.</note> that the same thing happens on shores that are slightly curved. Hearing is also deceived, as when we think we hear thunder, when in fact it is a wagon.

<margin>The sense of touch is deceived.</margin>
There are those who think they have touched something, yet they are mistaken. Those suffering from a fever, if they drink the best and sweetest wine, think it is more bitter than gall; if they eat good broths, they think they are tasteless. This does not happen through any fault of the cook, but because of a palate and stomach corrupted by disease. For those in whom bile overflows, to them bitter... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "bitter" (amara) on the following page.</note>

<meta>The catchword "ra" completes the word "amara" (bitter) on the next page, referring to how things taste to those with excess bile.</meta>

...bitter, everything seems so. Thus it happens that someone may think they see, hear, or touch *Specters* <note>original: "Spectra"; appearing spirits or apparitions.</note>, or that they are being touched by them, even when the reality is quite otherwise; yet, one could not easily persuade that person of the truth.

<margin>Weakness of sight and fear</margin>
If fear and weakness of sight—and a concurrence of the other senses—should meet, men immediately imagine many wonderful things for themselves. They firmly believe even the most false things, and they cannot be moved from their opinion by any reasoning.

We read that not only individual men, but sometimes entire armies have been so deceived that they thought enemies were looming over them when no one was there. Because of this, horrible retreats have sometimes occurred.

*Commines* <note>Philippe de Commines (c. 1447–1511), a writer and diplomat who served both the Burgundian and French courts.</note>, a man of the knightly order and a diligent historian, writes at the end of the first book of the deeds of *Louis XI, King of France*: *Charles, Duke of Burgundy, and other princes, when they had moved their army toward Paris <note>original: "Lutetiam"; the Roman name for Paris.</note>, learned through a report that the King had decided to attack them the following day with all his forces. On the following day, Charles sent out horsemen for the sake of scouting. Because the sky was quite dark, they thought they saw a huge number of spears in the distance. Having progressed further, now that the sky was clearer, they recognized that the place where they had thought the King had gathered all his forces and strength was actually planted, more than anything else, with*
->*thistles.*<-

In the second book of *Kings* <note>original: "Regum"; in modern Bibles, this is 2 Kings 3.</note> we read in chapter 3 that after the death of King *Ahab* <note>original: "Achabi"</note>, the *Moabites* revolted against his son *Joram* <note>original: "Joramo"</note>. They said: *The kings have fought amongst themselves and have fallen by one another's wounds.* And as they rushed, in no order, to plunder the *Israelites' camp*, they were unexpectedly slaughtered by them. In this passage, you see that an entire army mistook water for blood.

## C H A P T E R V.

### ***Many people frighten others so that they believe they have seen or heard Ghosts.***

<margin>Night-demons <note>original: "Lamiæ"; in folklore, these were female spirits or witches who snatched children.</note></margin>
Furthermore, it often happens that not only humorous men, but also malicious ones, strike terror into others by changing their attire. It is common among some people that at a certain time of year, someone—having pulled a net or a mask over their head—terrifies their children so that they might work more diligently and show themselves obedient to their parents. Later, they tell the children that they are seeing *shapeshifting specters* <note>original: "empusas"; a mythological being sent by Hecate.</note>, *child-snatching demons* <note>original: "lamias"</note>, and *hobgoblins* <note>original: "mormolycias"; Greek "mormo," a frightening spirit used to scare children into behaving.</note>. The children are fully convinced that this is the case and, for the most part, are miserably afraid. However, it is not always beneficial to frighten children in this way; for sometimes, out of fear, they fall into serious illnesses and cry out in their sleep at night. *Solomon teaches that if children neglect their duty, they should be corrected with a rod, and that fear should be instilled in them in that manner*; he does not say they should be convinced that they are to be devoured by *empusas*, the *night-woman* <note>original: "fœmina nocturna"</note>, or other monsters.

Sometimes cheerful and playful young men put on clothes by which they resemble Devils,

### CHAPTER VI.

*Priests and monks have pretended to be Spirits: how Mundus <note>Decius Mundus, a Roman knight who lived during the reign of Emperor Tiberius.</note> defiled Paulina under this pretext, and how Tyrannus <note>Likely a reference to a specific corrupt priest mentioned in historical anecdotes of church scandals.</note> defiled many noble and virtuous matrons.*

**T**o this is added the fact that in every age there have been certain priests who, by performing wondrous feats and devoting themselves to magic arts, have in various ways driven foolish people (who held those priests in the highest regard) out of their minds. They did this so that they might collect a great sum of silver and indulge in their own pleasures. Concerning this matter, I will include a few historical accounts so that godly people may become more cautious.

Therefore, according to his estimation, this sum would amount to approximately 2,600 florins.) Even so, being unable to sway her, and unable to endure the powerlessness of his love, he decided to end both his life and his love together through starvation <note>original: "inedia"; a common method of suicide in Roman literature.</note>. His plan did not escape the notice of Ide, a freedwoman of Mundus’s father, who was skilled in various but dishonorable arts. ...giving him hope that she would bring it about that he might enjoy **Paulina’s** embraces. And when he gladly accepted her entreaties, she said she needed only **fifty thousand drachmas** <note>Approximately 650 florins by the previous page's calculation.</note> to conquer the woman’s modesty. Knowing, furthermore, that she was intensely devoted to the **cult of Isis**, she devised such a plan: Meeting with several of the goddess's priests, and after securing a promise of silence and—what is most effective—showing them their reward (twenty-five thousand in hand, and another twenty-five thousand after the work was completed), she revealed the young man's love to them. She asked that they strive by every means to help him fulfill his vow. Touched by the greed for gold, they readily gave their promise.

The eldest of these priests hurried to Paulina; once admitted and having secured a private conversation without witnesses, he claimed he had been sent by **Anubis** <note>An ancient Egyptian god associated with the afterlife.</note> (**Anubis** was worshiped with a dog's head <note>original: "canino capite"</note> alongside Isis). She received the message with a willing heart; and soon she was boasting among the women of her acquaintance that she had been deemed worthy of the love of **Anubis**. She also informed her husband that a dinner and a bed had been prepared for her by **Anubis**.

*Once the whole matter was reported to her husband, she begged that he not let such a significant mockery go unpunished. The husband then reported everything to the Emperor. After **Tiberius** <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 14–37 AD</note> learned everything through a very careful investigation, he sent those impostor priests to the cross <note>original: "in crucem egit"; they were crucified</note>, and along with them **Ida** <note>original: "Iden"</note>, the mastermind behind this crime, whose primary effort was spent in corrupting the woman's **modesty** <note>original: "pudicitia"</note>. *From this history, it is easy to gather how **Satan** deluded the **Gentiles** <note>Heathens/Pagans</note>; and how their **priests** <note>original: "sacrifici"</note> persuaded them that the gods, appearing to them, said this or that, which were nonetheless entirely false. For how many times should we think they committed the most shameful acts, even if the matter was not brought into the open? ### ON SPECTERS. 37

The statue of **Saturn** <note>Saturn was a major Roman deity of agriculture and time; in Roman-occupied Egypt, he was often identified with local gods</note> seemed to emerge from wide hollows. However, that statue was actually hollowed out from behind and carefully fixed to the wall. While the lights were burning within the temple, the priest would suddenly project his voice through the hollow bronze statue to the attentive and praying woman. This was done in such a way that the unhappy woman would tremble with both fear and joy, believing herself worthy of being addressed by such a great divinity. 

After the impure "god" had discussed whatever he pleased—either to cause greater consternation or as an incitement to lust—the lights were all suddenly extinguished by a certain trick involving linen cloths drawn over them. Then, descending from his hiding place, he would inflict the deceit of adultery upon the stunned and terrified poor woman through these profane machinations. 

When these things had been carried out for a long time among the wives of those wretched men, it happened that a certain woman of modest mind shuddered at the deed. Listening more closely, she recognized the voice of **Tyrannus** <note>The name Tyrannus ironically means "Tyrant" or "Ruler" in Greek</note>. Upon returning home, she revealed the criminal fraud to her husband. He, more ardently inflamed by the injury to his wife—or rather, the injury to himself—brought the accused Tyrannus to be tortured. Once the priest was convicted and had confessed, and his other frauds were revealed, a sense of total shame and disgrace spread among the **Pagans** <note>original: "Paganorum"; referring here to the followers of the traditional Roman/Egyptian polytheistic religions as opposed to the rising Christian movement</note>.

C 3
<meta>catchword: concerning</meta>

# PART ONE

homes, where adulterous mothers, incestuous fathers, and spurious children were discovered. Once these things were publicized and betrayed, the crimes were swiftly rooted out along with the idols and the temples. One reads of **Numa Pompilio** <note>The legendary second king of Rome (715–673 BC), credited with establishing many of the city's religious institutions</note> that he feigned secret meetings with the nymph **Egeria** <note>A divine counselor in Roman mythology</note> so that he might secure authority for his own laws.

## CHAPTER VII.

*Timothy Aelurus, having lied that he was an Angel, seized the bishopric; four monks of the Order of Preachers at Bern fashioned many apparitions.*

**I**t could be tolerated in some way if these things had only happened among the pagans <note>original: "gentiles"</note>, who lacked the Word of God, were it not for the fact that we have discovered similar things to have happened often among Christians as well, and were it not to be feared that many such things occur even today. For it is "known to the bleary-eyed and the barbers" <note>A Latin idiom (lippis et tonsoribus) derived from the poet Horace, meaning something is common knowledge or public gossip</note> that many, especially from among the Monks and the petty priests <note>original: "sacrificulis," often a derogatory term for priests used by Protestant-leaning authors</note>, were sorcerers, enchanters, and <term>necromancers: those who claim to communicate with the spirits of the dead</term>, who were able to fake many **Specters**, miracles, and conversations with souls. **Theodorus Lector** <note>A 6th-century historian from Constantinople who wrote a history of the Church</note>, in his collections...

-># ON SPECTERS.<-

<margin>G 4</margin>

*In the Ecclesiastical History, Book 1, it is written concerning **Timothy Aelurus*** <note>Timothy II of Alexandria, nicknamed "the Cat" (Aelurus), was a 5th-century patriarch who opposed the Council of Chalcedon</note> that before **Proterius, the Bishop of Alexandria**, was killed, Timothy—who was aspiring to the Bishopric—went around the cells of the Monks at night dressed in a black garment. By calling out to each monk by name, he told those who responded that he was a spirit from the ministry of God <note>original: "ministerium Dei spiritum," implying he was posing as an angel or divine messenger</note> warning them that they should all separate themselves from **Proterius** and join themselves to **Timothy**. Having attained the Bishopric through these tricks and frauds, he stirred up great disturbances in the **Church**.

At this point, I cannot restrain myself from recounting in a few words—so far as it pertains to the present business—the memorable history of four Monks of the Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note> (*who were burned at the stake in **Bern, Switzerland**, in the year of our Lord **1509**, on the last day of the month of May*). By various arts, they deceived a certain simple young brother whom they had recently received into their Monastery. Regarding this matter, many pamphlets were published at the very time these events occurred, in both the **Latin and German languages**, which are still in existence today. There was a great dispute between the Monks of the **Order of Preachers** and the **Friars Minor**, or **Franciscans**, regarding the conception of the **Virgin Mary**. The **Preachers** asserted that she

At last, the matter reached such a point that the Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note> proposed to establish and confirm their opinion through false miracles. This was decided after a deliberation held in a certain synod (which they themselves call a "chapter") in the town of *Wimpfen* in *Germany*, where a convenient and suitable place for this business could be found. Finally, they chose *Bern, Switzerland*, because the people there were simple and warlike. **Four** of the leaders of the order in the Monastery of the **Preachers at Bern** therefore began to weave their web. And because the **Sub-prior**, one of these four, was skilled in the magic arts, he summoned an evil spirit <note>original: "cacodaemonem"</note> to enter into counsel with them on how they might successfully accomplish the task. The spirit, appearing in the form of an *Ethiopian* <note>In the folklore of the time, demons were frequently depicted as "Ethiopians" or "Moors" to signify their dark nature</note>, promised them all his help on this condition: that they should surrender themselves to him. They did this without hesitation, giving him written contracts signed with their own blood. It happened conveniently that in those very days, **Hans Jetzer**, a simple man and a tailor by trade, was admitted into their order; he seemed well-suited for their purpose. They began to test him...
