# PART ONE

->[Illustration: The Diabolical Contract]<-

<note>This central woodcut illustrates the secret meeting between the corrupt monks and the spirit they summoned.</note>

In the center of the image, a high-ranking **cleric**—one of the four leaders of the **Preachers** <note>Dominican Order</note>—is depicted in his formal **vestments** and wearing a **biretta** <term>a square, peaked cap worn by Catholic clergy</term>. He is shown receiving a small, folded **letter** or contract. The figure delivering this message is a dark-skinned man, described in the text as an **Ethiopian** <note>original: "Aethiops"; in the 16th century, demons were often depicted in art as dark-skinned figures to represent their "dark" or hellish nature</note>. This is the evil spirit who, as previously mentioned, promised to help the monks on the condition that they sign away their souls in blood.

The background contains two smaller scenes that represent the types of "miracles" or disasters the monks hoped to exploit:
*   In the **upper left**, a city or building is shown engulfed in **fire** and thick smoke.
*   In the **upper right**, an **assassination** or **martyrdom** is taking place, with a figure holding a raised dagger over someone lying on a bed.

In the foreground, three hooded monks are seen from behind, kneeling as they witness this unholy exchange.

<column-break/>

As they began to "weave their web," the four monks sought to convince the simple-minded **Hans Jetzer** that he was being visited by spirits. They started by **throwing** <note>This word continues the "catchword" from the previous page: "jacientes" (throwing)</note> stones and making great noise in his private cell to terrify him. 

The **Sub-prior**, who was the master of these magic arts, directed the other three in how to use the spirit’s help. They intended to use the poor tailor, Jetzer, as a vessel for their false revelations, claiming that the Virgin Mary had appeared to confirm their specific religious doctrines. By using the spirit who appeared as a messenger, they believed their plan to deceive the people of **Bern** was now fully in motion.

...[acting] in the place of Mary, saying: "By way of greeting, I am Mary, full of grace, the Lord is with me, etc." <note>original: *Salutatione ego sum Maria gratia plena, Dominus mecum, &c.* The friar is reciting the "Hail Mary" to maintain his disguise as the Virgin.</note> The Brother, recognizing the voice of the **Prior** <note>The head of the monastery.</note>, grabbed a knife and stabbed him; and when the Prior tried to defend himself, the brother forcefully shoved him back.

He rebuked him for having treated the holy Virgin so cruelly, adding: "I have been commanded to inform you that you possess the true wounds of Christ <term>stigmata</term> <note>From the Greek *stigma*, meaning a brand or mark. In Christian tradition, these are the five wounds of Jesus's crucifixion appearing miraculously on a living person.</note> on your own body, which neither I nor **Francis** <note>original: *Franciscus*. A reference to Saint Francis of Assisi, the most famous recipient of the stigmata. The Dominicans were desperate to prove their Order could produce a similar miracle to rival the Franciscans.</note> possess." He spoke at great length. However, the brother received him with such rage that the Subprior immediately fled to save himself.

Because the brother would no longer allow himself to be made a laughingstock by them, the friars became anxious and troubled. After holding a council, they told him they would freely admit that they had faked several of the apparitions, but claimed they did so with no other intent than to ensure he remained in the religious life. They insisted, however, that the core of the matter was genuine, and that he should not doubt that he truly bore the wounds of Christ on his body. And since the matter had already been made public, they begged him not to waver; otherwise, the entire Order would be brought to disgrace, and he would incur great danger along with them... <meta>The catchword "surum" at the bottom of the page completes the word "incursurum," indicating the sentence continues on the next page: "would incur."</meta>

# OF SPECTERS.

...[of] thieves, but if he were constant, this would be most useful for both himself and them. And with flattering words they persuaded him to promise that he would obey them from then on.

Therefore, after a long instruction, they placed him at the altar of the **divine Mary** on bended knees, in a certain chapel before the image of the **Holy Virgin**. One of the Monks stood behind a curtain and spoke through a **pipe** <term>cannam</term> <note>A hollow reed or tube used by the friars to project their voices, creating the illusion that a statue was speaking.</note> as if he were **Christ** speaking to **Mary**:

> Mother, why do you weep? Have I not promised that whatever you wish shall come to pass?

<margin>He replied.</margin>
The image replied: "I weep because no end is being put to this business." The image of **Christ** added:

> Believe me, it shall be made manifest,

afterward, the Monk went out from there and locked the chapel.

# PART ONE

Impiety, deceits, and crimes grew widespread, and the affairs of the Clergy <note>original: *Clericorum*. Referring to the priests, monks, and officials of the Church.</note> began to be undermined and became more and more suspicious to many pious men. This occurred when they claimed that this or that soul had experienced their help; that candles had been lit of their own accord; that this or that image had spoken or wept, or moved itself from one place to another; that this or that saint had adorned the Monastery <note>original: *Monasterium*</note> with precious relics <note>Physical remains of saints or objects they touched, believed to hold spiritual power.</note>; or that crosses had been sprinkled with the blood of Christ. The heads of the Roman Catholic Church.</note> regarding these matters, many people from then on refused to believe that things were truly so. Furthermore, it was not without reason that people began to doubt the transubstantiation <term>transubstantiatione</term> <note>The doctrine that the bread and wine of the Eucharist become the actual body and blood of Jesus.</note> of the bread into the body of Christ, since they had so often tainted the host <note>The consecrated bread used in the Mass.</note> with poison. However, when the judges promised him immunity and assured him that he would never return to the monks' power, he recounted the whole scheme in order; and when brought before the monks, he affirmed the same. They, although they were convicted and caught almost in the very act of their crime, nevertheless continued to reject the judges.

But in those very days, a persecution broke out against the *Lutherans* <note>The term used for early Protestant reformers. This refers to a period of intense religious tension in France, likely around the "Affair of the Placards" in 1534.</note>; this circumstance was the reason why the sentence—though it was quite mild for such a great outrage—was not carried out. For because the name of the *Lutherans* was so deeply hated, there was a fear that if any harsher punishment were imposed, it would be viewed not so much as a just penalty paid by those men, but as an injury committed against their religious Order. Many also judged that whatever ill befell the monks would be a most delightful spectacle for the *Lutherans*.

Furthermore, the *Franciscan* Order holds a great reputation for holiness among the common people. When the condemned men were being deported from *Paris* <note>original: *Lutetia*</note> back to *Orléans*, certain women, moved by pity, followed them all the way to the city gate with many tears and sighs. A certain Parish Priest <term>Parochus: the priest in charge of a parish</term> of *Chiavenna* <note>An Alpine town, originally Clavenna</note> (this town is not far from Lake *Como* <note>original: Larius</note>, in the territory of the *Grisons* <note>original: Rhætorum; the Rhaetian League of the Swiss Alps</note>), being seized by love for a beautiful and modest Virgin, the daughter of a citizen of that town, often followed her as she went to her father's barn outside the town; and he solicited her for debauchery <term>stupro: sexual immorality or unchaste acts</term>, but he always met with rejection. At last, that Parish Priest took from the church a cloth of blue color adorned with stars, and covered himself with it; however, baring his arms and feet, he sprinkled them with blood, and hid himself outside the town. Once again, appearing unknown to the girl, and with his face veiled by a thin linen cloth, he falsely claimed to be the *Virgin Mary*, having disguised his voice. He recounted at length what evils hung over the town on account of the *Lutheran* religion (for at other times he had also vehemently raved against the *Lutherans* in his sermons); he gave many commands to the girl, which she was to report to the townspeople, concerning the establishing of feast days, fasts, public prayers <term>supplicationibus: religious petitions or processions</term>, and so forth.

*Erasmus of Rotterdam* <note>The famous Dutch Renaissance humanist (1466–1536) who often critiqued clerical corruption</note> writes in *Book 22 of his Letters* to a certain Bishop, excusing himself concerning certain points that the Bishop had proposed to him so that he might be cautious; and among other things, making mention of wandering souls or spirits, he tells this story. ## CHAPTER X.

->*That it is not surprising that false apparitions were made in the past, nor is it surprising if they still occur today.*<-

<margin>Ambition</margin>
Many other examples of this kind could be brought forward, but these are enough for us; from them, it is clearly apparent to what extremes ambition, greed, envy, hatred, stubbornness, idleness, and love can drive men.

We learn from daily experience that the ambitious dare anything. If they are able to hinder others—whom they think will stand in the way of themselves or their kin from rising to prominence—through accusations, slanders, and other methods and means, they do not hesitate to do so. What wonder is it, then, if some among the monks and priests <term>sacrificis: literally "sacrificers"; a term often used by Protestant writers to refer to Catholic priests, implying their focus on the "sacrifice" of the Mass</term>, who wish to excel, strive to win authority for themselves even today through false miracles, apparitions, and other things of that sort?

<summary>The author concludes a story about a fraudulent "devil" in Augsburg and argues that human vices like ambition and greed naturally lead corrupt clergy to fake supernatural wonders.</summary>
<keywords>clerical fraud, Augsburg, Jesuits, religious polemic, human nature, false miracles, 16th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, moving from specific anecdotes of fraudulent Jesuits to a general moral critique. The author explores how common human vices—specifically greed, envy, and idleness—provide the motive for religious figures to manufacture "supernatural" occurrences for personal or political gain.</meta>

# ON SPECTERS.

<margin>Greed <unclear/></margin>
Everyone knows how pestilential a thing Greed is. For those who are not content with a little and wish to grow rich spare no one.

The hungry seek various ways to be satisfied; few tolerate hunger willingly. It is therefore not at all surprising if, among monks and priests <term>sacrificos: literally "sacrificers"; a derogatory term used by Reformers for Catholic priests to emphasize their focus on the "sacrifice" of the Mass rather than preaching</term>—who have always had a bad reputation for being greedy—some are still found who use false apparitions of souls to carry out their business, or who devise religious pilgrimages and other ways to catch money. For what will lazy and idle men not do to collect wealth? *Does not Paul say that those who wish to grow rich through idleness fall into the snare of the Devil?* <note>original: "An non dicit Paulus illos qui ocio ditescere volunt, incidere in laqueum Diaboli?" A paraphrase of 1 Timothy 6:9</note>

<margin>Envy</margin>
Experience teaches what zeal, stubbornness, envy, hatred, strife, and the desire for victory can achieve and what they are capable of. The Bernese Preachers <note>This refers to the "Jetzer Affair" (1507-1509) in Bern, Switzerland, where four Dominicans were executed for faking apparitions of the Virgin Mary to undermine their rivals, the Franciscans</note>, when they hoped they could not win in any other way, handed themselves over—horrible to say!—to an evil spirit <term>cacodaemoni: from the Greek for an "evil demon" or "malignant spirit"</term>, and shared their plans with him. Who can deny that today, too, priests are almost always peevish, stubborn, and argumentative?

<margin>Idleness <unclear/></margin>
Idleness is the fuel <note>fomes: literally "tinder" or "kindling" for a fire</note> of all evils.

### PART ONE

...[of so many evils], what good then can you expect from those who not only fail to apply themselves to any of the labors prescribed by God, nor devote their time to noble literature, but instead spend their days and nights in gluttony? <note>original: "helluantur"; this refers to excessive feasting or "wolfing down" food, implying a lack of spiritual discipline</note> Tell me, I pray, was it a hard-working farmer, or rather some idle man with too much time for devising harmful things, who invented those engines of war that are called bombards? <term>bombardas: an early form of cannon or mortar used in the 14th through 16th centuries</term>

The effects of desire <note>original: "amor"; while it means "love," the author uses it here to refer to carnal lust or forbidden passions fostered by monastic life</note> could be demonstrated and explained in many ways. Yet, since monks and priests <term>sacrifici: literally "sacrificers"; a derogatory term used by Reformers for Catholic priests to emphasize their focus on the ritual "sacrifice" of the Mass rather than preaching the Gospel</term> live in idleness and abound in every luxury, while being barred from holy marriage, is it any wonder that today they still manufacture various visions so that they might more easily satisfy their desires?

I will say nothing further on this point, though it is much to be feared that quite a few among them are so depraved and lost that they practice magic and other forbidden arts. To whom, then, will it seem strange hereafter if they are said to invent spirits, and afterwards claim that this or that soul has appeared to them? For wherever such conditions exist as those I have described, men will dare anything.

No one, however, is more prone to these things than those who live in monasteries and

There are now more shameless, bold, greedy, and impious people than there have been in years past.

Furthermore, the Clergy enjoy this special privilege: that people, especially the superstitious, hold them in the highest regard and affection. No one imagines that they would devote themselves to evil, to fraud, or to trickery; everyone expects far different behavior from them. Consequently, if they do turn their efforts toward evil, they can easily remain hidden—unless God, as demonstrated by several previous examples, exposes their malice through some miraculous sign and drags it into the light of day.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of natural explanations for perceived supernatural events. <page-num>69</page-num>
<header>OF SPECTERS.</header>

...fearful people think [these things] are **Specters** <note>original: "Spectra"</note>. When they hear the cries of rats, cats, polecats, <margin>*Animals of the marten kind*</margin> martens, and other animals, or a horse in the stable at the dead of night striking the ground with its hoof, they immediately tremble and sweat, thinking that some unknown Specter is wandering through the silence of the night. Sometimes the **Pelican** <note>original: "Onocrotalus"; likely referring to the Great White Pelican, whose pouch can produce deep, resonant sounds</note> or the **Bittern** <note>original: "ardea stellaris"; literally 'starry heron', known for its deep, booming call that can sound like a foghorn or a bull</note> is heard—birds which are rare in our regions—or other foreign birds; in those moments, many imagine they are hearing I know not what. If a woodworm or a timber beetle <note>original: "teredo vel cossus"</note> gnaws into a wooden wall, some think they hear someone lightly striking an anvil with a small hammer <note>This likely refers to the "Deathwatch Beetle," which makes a rhythmic tapping sound that was historically considered an omen of death</note>; sometimes it seems to several people that they are hearing it. Certain simple and foolish people, upon hearing these sounds, imagine that there are certain **Earthly Mannikins** <note>original: "virunculos Terreos"; referring to folklore entities like gnomes, kobolds, or brownies</note> <margin>*Earthly little men.*</margin> and recount their wonderful stories about them which they heard from their mothers and grandmothers: how they appeared to the household servants, performed chores, rocked the cradles, and how it is a stroke of good luck if they stay in someone's home. If such tiny creatures were truly seen, they were evil spirits. For we read that the Pagans had their **Lares** and **Penates** <note>In ancient Roman religion, the Lares were spirits who protected the household, while Penates were gods of the storeroom and the family's welfare</note>, whom they worshipped because they took care of their homes and defended the family; evil spirits sometimes showed themselves to them in human form...

<summary>The author explains how the nocturnal sounds of animals, rare birds, and wood-boring insects are often mistaken for ghosts. *Olaus Magnus, the Archbishop of Uppsala, writes in his history of the Northern peoples* <note>original: "de gentibus Septentrionalibus"; Olaus Magnus (1490–1557) was a Swedish historian and ecclesiastic famous for documenting the folklore of Scandinavia</note>, that even today in those regions, spirits exist who serve men at night in human form, and care for horses and other beasts of burden. The wind knocks something over in the house, or moves a window shutter at night; many immediately think they hear a **Specter**, and they can scarcely—indeed, not at all—be led away from this vain opinion.

<margin>Echo.</margin>
It is also natural that when someone shouts or speaks in woods, valleys, and vaulted places, an Echo murmurs back the last words or syllables, sometimes so distinctly that one might think someone is answering from the opposite side. Many would be terrified, especially at night, if they did not know well that the matter is natural.

*Cardanus, in the 18th book of On Subtlety* <note>original: "de Subtilitate"; Gerolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a Renaissance polymath whose work sought to explain the secrets of the natural world</note>, recounts a wonderful story about a certain man from **Como** <note>original: "Comensi"; a city in Northern Italy</note> who, in the dead of night near a torrent, since he did not know the ford, cried out; and when the Echo responded, he—thinking it was a human—asked if he could cross here. # ON SPECTERS

...responded in the Italian language, "here, here" <note>original: "hic hic"</note>. But there was a whirlpool and a great noise of waters, and so he asked again and again whether the torrent could be crossed by a ford: the *Echo* responded the same way every time. At last, having escaped the danger, he recounted to his friends that he had nearly been cast headlong into the torrent by the persuasion of a demon. In the same passage, he reports that the great cathedral <note>original: "basilicam"</note> of **Pavia** <note>original: "Ticini"; the Roman name for the city of Pavia in Lombardy</note> returns multiple voices, and in that place the voices gradually fade and cease like the voice of a dying person, so that it can scarcely be believed to be an Echo.

<margin>Things that shine at night.</margin>
There are certain things that shine only at night, such as certain gemstones, the eyes of certain animals, fireflies or glow-worms <note>original: "lampyrides vel cicindulae"</note>, and rotten wood. Children sometimes use these to terrify their companions so that they think they are seeing evil spirits or men made of flame <note>This refers to the phenomenon of "foxfire," a bioluminescent fungus that grows on decaying wood, which was a common source of ghost stories</note>.

*Hector Boece* <note>original: "Hector Boëthius" (c. 1465–1536), a Scottish philosopher and historian</note> writes that a certain king of **Scotland** secretly employed certain men who, dressed in shining scales and armed with sticks of rotten wood, appeared to his princes and nobles at night. They exhorted them to fight bravely against the enemy and promised them victory. The nobles, believing they had seen Angels, conducted themselves bravely and won the victory.

<margin>The tricks of lamps.</margin>
Sometimes candles or little fires are observed, which seem to wander here and there at night. And just as in **Switzerland** <note>original: "Helvetia"</note> young men—who, with their torches, gather for the fires they light at night during the "deprivation of meat" <note>original: "carnis privio"; this refers to the Shrovetide or Carnival celebrations before Lent</note>, as they call it—now gather together, then soon scatter again, and once more joined, march in a long line: so those little fires <note>The author is likely describing *ignis fatuus*, or "will-o'-the-wisps," which are caused by the combustion of organic gases in swamps</note> seem now to be gathered in one place, soon to be dispersed and run about again, and finally to vanish. Mostly they travel alone, and they greatly terrify those making a journey at night. These things indeed, and others, have their natural causes: yet I would not deny that demons also often deceive men in this way.

<margin>Exhalations.</margin>
Natural philosophers <note>original: "Physici"; in this context, those who study the physical world and natural sciences</note> report that dense vapors <note>original: "exhalationes"</note> come forth from the earth and are ignited. Sulfurous material in the hidden places and veins of the earth, if air penetrates it, is ignited and attempts to break out. Sometimes fire bursting out of the earth to the height of a tall tree is suddenly extinguished: which should also be attributed to burning matter seeking an exit. One reads concerning **Mount Etna** in **Sicily** that it once burned day and night, and threw out flames, ash, and fiery stones. Likewise concerning **Mount Vesuvius** in **Campania**, which for a thou—

# ON SPECTERS. 73:

...is five German miles <note>original: "miliari Germanicum"; approximately 23 English miles</note> distant from Naples. That mountain, as **Saint Jerome** <note>original: "D. Hieronymus"; a prominent early Christian scholar</note> writes, during the reign of **Titus** <note>Titus was the Roman Emperor during the famous eruption of 79 AD</note> emitted such a great fire from itself that it burned up the neighboring regions, the cities, and their inhabitants, and covered the nearby fields with ash. In our own memory as well, these two mountains <note>referring to Etna and Vesuvius from the previous page</note>, seething with great heat, have caused immense damage to those living nearby. Similar things are read concerning other mountains, even in our own time. In **Iceland**, fires are found bursting forth from the earth, as **Olaus Magnus** <note>A Swedish archbishop and writer known for his descriptions of Nordic geography and folklore</note> testifies. Just as mountains and fields can burn, so too can a small fire be kindled in the earth and wander far and wide. Those who make a journey, or otherwise see these things and are ignorant of natural causes, imagine out of fear that they have seen **fiery men** <note>original: "viros igneos"</note>, or other wonders about which they have sometimes heard from others. Out of fear, they sometimes fall into dangerous illnesses.

Optics also produce wonders, so that in mirrors made and positioned according to a certain method, various images show themselves: sometimes they seem to come out <note>The author is likely describing early "natural magic" or optical illusions like the *camera obscura* or catoptric effects</note> and represent people known to us.

# CONCERNING SPECTERS.

...appear to men are natural occurrences or empty terrors; but rather, that **Specters** do sometimes appear, and many wondrous things beyond nature happen from time to time. For many accounts of this kind are read in the works of several serious historians; many men of reliable character <note>original: "non fluxae fidei"; literally "not of flowing or unstable faith"</note> also affirm that they have seen **Specters** by night and even by day.

We shall note in order several histories concerning **Specters** which have appeared, taken from various approved historians.

<margin>A Triton appeared to Julius Caesar.</margin>
*Suetonius Tranquillus* <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69–122 AD), a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve Roman emperors</note> writes that when *Julius Caesar* was setting out from *Gaul* into *Italy* with his army and had already reached the *Rubicon* river (which divided *Italy* from Nearer *Gaul*), he halted for a little while and reflected on how great a task he was undertaking. While he was of a doubtful mind, wondering whether he should cross the river or not, a figure of extraordinary size and beauty suddenly appeared sitting nearby, playing upon a reed pipe. (*Melanchthon* <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a German reformer and collaborator of Martin Luther, who wrote extensively on "Physics" or natural philosophy</note> calls him a *Triton* <note>A mythological Greek sea-god, often depicted with a conch shell or pipe; here used to describe a supernatural water-spirit</note> in his *Physics*). To hear this figure, not only shepherds but many soldiers from their posts had run together, and among them were also brass-blowers <note>original: "aeneatores"; a technical term for those who played instruments made of "aes" or bronze</note> (that is, trumpeters, so called from their bronze trumpets), when, snatched from one...

### PART ONE

...with a trumpet leaped forward to the river; and with a mighty breath, having begun the battle-call <note>original: "classicum"; a Roman military trumpet signal</note>, he crossed to the other bank. <margin>Theseus seen in the Battle of Marathon.</margin>
*Plutarch* <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD), a Greek philosopher and biographer</note> writes in his *Life of Theseus* that many who were present at the Battle of Marathon against the Medes <note>The Persians; ancient writers often used "Medes" and "Persians" interchangeably</note> testified that they had seen the ghost <note>original: "umbram"; literally "shadow"</note> of *Theseus*—who had died a long time before from a fall—armed and appearing before the Greek battle line, rushing against the Barbarians. This was the reason that moved the Athenians to later venerate him as a hero.

*Pausanias* <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD</note> writes in his *Description of Attica* that on the plain of Marathon, four hundred years after the battle had taken place, the neighing of horses and the clash of fighting soldiers could be heard every night. To those who traveled there specifically to witness this spectacle, nothing happened; but those who arrived there by mere chance heard these things most clearly.

In the *Life of Cimon*, the same *Plutarch* writes that the citizens of *Chaeronea* <note>A city in Boeotia, Greece</note> recalled *Damon*—who had fled the city because of many murders—back into the city with flattering words, and afterwards slaughtered him in a bathhouse. From that time forward, many **Specters** [appeared] in that place...

### ON SPECTERS

...visions and groans were heard, so that they eventually blocked up the doors of the bathhouse.

In the *Life of Dion*, he <note>Plutarch</note> recounts that a great and horrible vision was presented to *Dion* <note>Dion of Syracuse (408–354 BC), a Greek politician and disciple of Plato</note>, a man of courage and undaunted spirit. For when the day was already declining (these are the words of *Plutarch* as translated by *Xylander* <note>Wilhelm Holtzman (1532–1576), a German humanist scholar known as Xylander, who produced a standard Latin translation of Plutarch's works</note>), and he happened to be sitting alone, lost in thought, in the courtyard of his house, he was startled by a sudden noise. Looking toward the other part of the portico, he beheld a tall woman whose dress and face were in no way different from a tragic Fury <note>The Furies, or Erinyes, were ancient goddesses of vengeance often depicted in Greek tragedies as terrifying women with snakes for hair</note>, who was sweeping the house with a broom. 

Greatly terrified and dismayed by this Specter, he called his friends to him and explained the vision to them, begging them to spend that night with him; he was utterly stunned by fear, dreading that the omen might appear to him again if he were alone. This, however, did not happen again. A few days later, his son threw himself headfirst from the upper part of the house and died; Dion himself died after being stabbed to death.

In the *Life of Brutus* <note>The Latin text says "Decius Brutus," but Plutarch’s account refers to Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Julius Caesar</note>, he writes the same thing: When *Brutus* had decided to transport his troops from *Asia* into *Europe*, in the dead of night <note>original: "concubia nocte"; the deepest part of the night when people are in their first sleep</note>, while there was a faint light in his tent and rest and silence throughout the whole camp, as he was deliberating and reflecting on some matter in his mind, [there appeared] a vision...

It is not granted to you to see all these things; rather, go away, for the end of both your works and your life is now upon you." Having heard these words, *Drusus* immediately turned his course back, and on the journey, before he reached the *Rhine* <note>original: "Rhenum"</note>, he died of illness.

He relates that other portents <term>prodigia|extraordinary events or omens that signaled divine will or impending disaster</term> also occurred before his death, which he nevertheless held in contempt. For two young men were seen riding on horses through the middle of the fortifications, and the wailing of a woman was heard, along with other things of that kind, etc.

## PART ONE

...it seems right to insert [the letter] in its entirety in this place; "leisure provides an opportunity both for me to learn and for you to teach," he says. Therefore, I would very much like to know whether you think *phantasms* <note>original: "phantasmata"; referring to ghosts or supernatural apparitions</note> are something real and have their own shape and some divine power, or if they take on an empty and shifting image from our fear. As for me, I am led to believe they exist primarily by what I hear happened to *Curtius Rufus* <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus (died c. 53 AD), a Roman politician and historian who rose from humble beginnings to become the Proconsul of Africa</note>. 

While still of humble and obscure status, he had attached himself as a companion to the man then holding the governorship of *Africa*. As the day was fading, he was walking in a portico; the figure of a woman, larger and more beautiful than a human, appeared to him in his terror. She declared herself to be *Africa*, a herald of future events. She told him he would go to *Rome* and hold high offices, and would even return to the same province with supreme command, and would die there. Everything came to pass. Moreover, it is told that as he was approaching *Carthage* <note>The ancient city in North Africa, which had been rebuilt as a Roman provincial capital</note> and disembarking from his ship (evidently when he was going to take up his governorship), the same figure met him on the shore. He himself, certainly seized by illness, interpreting the future by the past and adversity by his successes, cast away all hope of recovery, even though none of his household despaired of his life. Now, is not the following story even more terrifying and no less wondrous? I shall set it forth just as I received it.

<margin>The Athenian Ghost.</margin>
There was in *Athens* a spacious and roomy hou-

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter of Pliny the Younger to Sura. Having recounted the omen of Curtius Rufus, Pliny now describes a famous haunting in Athens involving the philosopher Athenodorus. This narrative serves as his primary evidence for the physical reality of ghosts.</meta>

## PART ONE

...the following story is even more terrifying and no less remarkable, and I shall relate it to you just as I received it.

There was in **Athens** <note>The center of Greek philosophy and culture, then under Roman rule</note> a house that was large and spacious, but it had an infamous reputation and was considered a place of death. In the silence of the night, the sound of iron could be heard, and if you listened quite closely, the rattling of chains <note>original: "strepitus vinculorum"</note> would echo, first from a distance and then from very nearby. 

Soon, an apparition would appear: an old man, wasted away by starvation and filth, with a long, flowing beard and bristling hair. On his legs he wore shackles, and on his wrists he carried chains, which he would shake as he moved. The inhabitants suffered through grim and terrible nights, driven from their sleep by fear. This sleeplessness led to illness and, as their dread intensified, even to death. For even when the phantom was absent, the memory of that terrible image remained before their eyes, and their fear persisted long after the sight had vanished.

Consequently, the house was abandoned and condemned to silence, left entirely to the ghost. However, a notice remained posted, in case someone—unaware of the great evil within—might wish to purchase or rent it.

<column-break/>

The philosopher **Athenodorus** <note>Athenodorus Cananites (c. 74 BC – 7 AD), a Stoic philosopher known for his bravery and for being a teacher to the Emperor Augustus</note> came to Athens. He read the advertisement, and when he heard the price—which was suspiciously low—he inquired further. Upon learning the whole truth, he was not deterred; rather, he was even more encouraged to rent it. 

As evening approached, he ordered a bed to be prepared for him in the front section of the house. He called for his writing tablets, a stylus, and a lamp. He dismissed all his servants to the inner parts of the house and applied his mind, eyes, and hand to his writing, lest an unoccupied mind should imagine hearing sounds or conjure up empty fears.

At first, there was only the silence of the night. Then, the clashing of iron and the rattling of chains began. He did not lift his eyes or set down his pen; instead, he steeled his spirit and closed his ears. The noise grew louder and came closer; now it was heard at the threshold, and now it was inside the room. He looked back and saw the figure, exactly as it had been described to him. It stood there, beckoning with its finger as if calling him. 

Athenodorus, in response, signaled with his hand that the ghost should wait a moment, and he turned back to his tablets. The ghost then began to rattle its chains over the philosopher’s very head as he wrote. He looked back again to see it beckoning as before; without further delay, he took up his lamp and followed. 

The apparition moved with a slow step, as if weighed down by the heavy chains. After it turned into the courtyard of the house, it suddenly vanished, leaving its companion behind. Now alone, Athenodorus plucked some grass and leaves to mark the exact spot. The next day, he went to the magistrates and advised them to have that spot excavated. 

They found bones <note>original: "ossa"</note> entwined and bound in chains; the body, rotted by time and the earth, had left only these remains, stripped and corroded by the iron. These were gathered and buried publicly with the proper rites. After the ghost was correctly laid to rest, the house was troubled by it no more.

<summary>Pliny recounts the famous tale of the philosopher Athenodorus, who successfully investigated a haunted house in Athens and laid a restless, chained spirit to rest through a proper burial.</summary>
<keywords>Athenodorus, Athens, chains, haunting, burial rites, ghosts, Stoic philosophy, Pliny the Younger</keywords>

# ON GHOSTS.

->...[the house was] infamous and pestilential: through the silence<-

# PART ONE

the chains were moving; he did not raise his eyes, nor did he set aside his pen <note>original: "stylum," the pointed tool used for writing on wax tablets</note>, but he steadied his mind and closed off his ears. Then the crashing noise <term>fragor: a loud, clashing din or uproar</term> grew louder; it drew near, and was heard now at the threshold, and now as if within the room. He looks back, sees, and recognizes the phantom <term>effigiem: a ghostly image or likeness</term> that had been described to him. It was standing there, beckoning with its finger, as if calling him. 

He, in response, signaled with his hand for it to wait a little, and again applied himself to his wax tablets and pen; the ghost rattled its chains <term>catenis: iron shackles or bonds</term> over the head of the man as he wrote. He looks back again and sees it beckoning just as before. Without delay, he picks up his light and follows. It walked with a slow step, as if weighed down by its shackles <term>vincula: chains or fetters</term>. After it turned into the courtyard of the house, it suddenly vanished and abandoned its companion. 

Left alone, he placed some plucked grass and leaves on the spot as a sign. The next day, he went to the Magistrates <term>magistratus: civil authorities or public officials</term> and advised them to order that the place be dug up. They found bones <term>ossa: human skeletal remains</term> entwined and entangled in chains, which the body—decayed by time and the earth—had left bare and corroded by the iron. Once gathered, these remains were publicly buried; after the spirit was properly laid to rest, the house was free <term>manibus caruit: literally "was without its spirits," meaning the haunting ceased</term> of the ghost. And while I believe these things on the word of those who swear by them, I can affirm the following story to others from my own experience.

I have a freedman <term>libertus: a formerly enslaved person who has been granted legal freedom</term> who is not uneducated. His younger brother was resting with him in the same bed. This brother seemed to see...

# ON SPECTERS

...[saw] someone sitting on his bed, moving shears <note>original: "cultros," literally knives or blades used for cutting hair</note> to his head, and even cutting the hair from the very crown of his head. When it became light, he himself was found shorn around the crown, and the hair was found lying there. A small amount of time passed, and again another event similar to the first gave it credit. A boy was sleeping with several others in the slave-school <term>pædagogio: a place where young slaves were raised and educated</term>; two figures came through the windows (so he tells it) in white tunics, and they sheared him as he lay there, then returned the way they had come. The day showed this boy also shorn, and his hair scattered around.

Nothing notable followed, except perhaps that I would not have been an accused man <note>Pliny refers to the danger of being executed or exiled during the reign of terror</note> if ***Domitian***, under whom these things happened, had lived longer. For in his desk, an indictment <term>libellus: a formal written petition or legal accusation</term> against me, given by Carus <note>Mettius Carus was a notorious informer who grew wealthy by accusing citizens of treason</note>, was found. From this it can be conjectured—since it is the custom for those accused of crimes to let their hair grow long <note>Roman defendants often went unshorn and wore mourning clothes to elicit pity from judges</note>—that the cut hair of my servants was a sign that the danger hanging over me had been cut away.

Therefore, I ask that you apply your great learning to this. The matter is worthy of your long and deep consideration; I indeed am unworthy to have you share the abundance of your knowledge with me. You may even argue on both sides of the issue, as is your custom; however, please argue more strongly on one side, lest you leave me suspended and in uncertainty, since the reason for my consulting [you...]

After a few days had passed, the **Specter** returned, threatening the farmer that it would also kill his second son unless he buried the bones. The farmer, having learned his lesson through the loss he suffered and seeing his second son fall sick, reported the matter to **Theodorus**. He set out with him to the estate and, in the place where the farmer had unearthed the urn, they made a grave once more and buried the urn with the <unclear>bones</unclear>. <page-num>69</page-num>

### On Specters.

...into its place, the farmer's son immediately recovered.

**Dio** <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian who wrote a massive history of Rome in Greek</note> writes that **Trajan** <note>Roman Emperor known for his military conquests</note> was led by a **Specter** to a safer place, out of the house in **Antioch** where he was staying during an earthquake.

**Julius Capitolinus** <note>One of the authors of the *Historia Augusta*, a collection of imperial biographies</note>, who described the lives of several **Roman Emperors**, relates that **Pertinax** <note>Emperor for only three months in 193 AD</note>, three days before he was stabbed to death, saw a shadow in the water of his pool. This shadow threatened him with a drawn sword and greatly disturbed him.

**Flavius Vopiscus** <note>Another biographer from the *Historia Augusta*</note> writes that for **Tacitus** <note>Marcus Claudius Tacitus, Emperor from 275–276 AD</note>, all the omens were of death: his father’s tomb opened itself when the doors were broken apart, and the shadow of his mother appeared during the day to both **Tacitus** and **Florianus** <note>Tacitus's half-brother</note>, looking just as she did when she was alive.

**Ammianus Marcellinus** <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian</note>, in his twenty-first book, "On the signs of the death of **Constantius**" <note>Constantius II, the son of Constantine the Great</note>, reports that the Emperor was terrified by apparitions <note>original: "imaginibus"</note> during the night.

The same author, in book twenty-five, writes regarding **Julian** <note>Julian the Apostate, the last pagan Emperor</note> that shortly before his death, while he was writing "under the skins" <note>original: "sub pellibus" — a phrase meaning he was writing in his military tent while on campaign</note> in imitation of **Julius Caesar**, he saw the figure of the public genius <term>genius publici: a guardian spirit representing the state or the people</term> (who was usually depicted with the horn of **Amalthea** <note>The "cornucopia" or horn of plenty, named after the nymph who nursed the infant Jupiter</note>). This spirit appeared more dejected than when it had first appeared to him as he was rising to his peak, and it was now seen departing from him.

## CHAPTER XIII.

->That Specters exist is proved from the history of the early Church and the writings of the Holy Fathers.<-

If we examine the histories of the Church, several instances of this kind come to our attention. **Sozomen** <note>Salamanes Hermias Sozomenus (c. 400–450 AD), a historian of the early Christian Church</note> writes in his *Ecclesiastical History*, book 6, chapter 28, concerning **Apelles**...

...about a blacksmith (whose name was famous throughout **Egypt** <note>original: "Ægyptum"</note> because of the gift for performing miracles with which he was endowed) who, while he was busying himself with his craft at night, was tempted in his chastity by the **specter** <note>original: "Spectrum"</note> of a demon in the form of a finely dressed woman. He snatched the iron on which he was working from the fire and burned the face of the demon, which immediately fled, shrieking and wailing.

Later, in **Book 7, Chapter 23** <note>Referring to Sozomen’s *Ecclesiastical History*, written in the 5th century</note>, writing about the riot <note>original: "seditione"</note> that was stirred up in **Antioch** <note>An ancient Greek city, now in modern-day Turkey</note> because of the excessive tax which the **Emperor Theodosius** <note>Theodosius I, or Theodosius the Great (reigned 379–395 AD)</note> had commanded the city to pay during a time of war. The people, offended by this, cast down the statues of the Emperor and his wife, dragging them with a rope and shouting insults. On the night before the riot broke out at dawn, he says, it is reported that a **specter** of a woman was seen, immense in size and horrific in appearance, running through the streets of the city on high and lashing the air with a terrifyingly sounding whip. Just as those who serve at such public games are accustomed to incite beasts to anger, so it appears that a certain hostile demonic spirit <note>original: "infestus quidam genius dæmonum"</note> stirred up that riot through its snares.

**Theodore the Reader** <note>Theodorus Lector, a 6th-century historian in Constantinople</note> writes in his collections from the first book of *Ecclesiastical History* that **Gennadius**, the **Patriarch of Constantinople** <note>Gennadius I, who served as Patriarch from 458 to 471 AD</note>, went down to the altar at night for the sake of prayer. There, a vision appeared to him, or a certain **Specter** <note>original: "Spectrum"</note> of a hideous appearance. When Gennadius rebuked it, he heard a voice crying out: as long as the Patriarch lived, the specter would cease its activities, but after his death, it would lay waste to the Church. Upon hearing this, he prayed for the safety of the Church, and shortly thereafter departed from among the living.

<margin>Nicepho-</margin>
In the works of **Gregorius Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century Byzantine historian known for his "Ecclesiastical History"</note>, who described church affairs at great length, and **Abdias** regarding the lives of the Apostles <note>Referring to the "Apostolic History" of Pseudo-Abdias, a collection of legendary biographies of the Apostles</note>, many accounts exist concerning visions, dreams, and the miracles of saints, as well as **Specters**. Indeed, it seems to learned men that these authors described such supernatural events more diligently than other matters which might have been more relevant to their purpose or could have been studied by readers with greater benefit.

<margin>Chronicles of Monks</margin>
To anyone turning through the histories written in later times, especially those composed by **Monks**, innumerable examples of this kind occur. However, I cannot hide the fact that many things are described by them in such a way that readers are, not without reason, suspicious of their claims.

**Ludovicus Vives** <note>Juan Luis Vives (1492–1540), a Spanish humanist who was critical of medieval legends</note>, **Beatus Rhenanus** <note>A German humanist and historian (1485–1547)</note>, and other learned men of our own age, in de-

<margin>The Fathers</margin> In the writings of the ancient **Fathers** <note>The influential early Christian theologians and bishops of the first few centuries</note>, passages concerning those apparitions, or **Specters** <note>original: "spectris"</note>, also occur here and there. **Saint Ambrose** <note>Aurelius Ambrosius (c. 340–397 AD), the Bishop of Milan and a key figure in the early Church</note> writes in his 90th sermon about the noble virgin **Agnes**, who was crowned with martyrdom for the Christian religion. When her parents kept watch at her tomb, they saw in the dead of night a choir of virgins clothed in golden **cyclades** <note>original: "cycladibus"; a type of expensive, circular outer robe or mantle worn by Roman women of high status</note>, among whom their daughter Agnes was similarly dressed. She commanded the other virgins to stand still for a short while; then, turning to her parents, she told them not to mourn her as if she were dead, but rather that they should congratulate her for having obtained eternal life from God. As soon as she had said this, she immediately vanished.

Concerning **Saint Gregory** <note>Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), a Pope and theologian whose *Dialogues* are a famous collection of miracle stories and accounts of the supernatural</note>, who recounts many things of this kind in his *Dialogues*, I shall speak in his proper place further below.

In almost all the ***Lives of the Fathers*** <note>original: "vitis Patrum"; a famous collection of biographies of the early Christian hermits and monks</note>, readers encounter many things concerning these matters, which should not all be rejected as fables and fabrications; for some were described by great and serious men. <margin>The martyr Basiliscus appears to Chrysostom.</margin>
In the life of **John Chrysostom** <note>c. 347–407 AD; the Archbishop of Constantinople known as "Golden-Mouthed" for his eloquence</note>, it is read that **Basiliscus**, the Bishop of the city of Comana (who had been crowned with martyrdom <note>A common phrase meaning he was executed for his faith</note> along with Lucian the Presbyter of Antioch under the Emperor **Maximian**), appeared to the holy Chrysostom when he had already been driven into exile, and said: "Brother John, be of good and brave heart, for tomorrow we shall be together." He had previously appeared to the priest of that temple and said: "Prepare a place for brother John, for he is coming soon." The outcome later proved that these things were true.

## CHAPTER XIV.

->*In the books of the Monks, many ridiculous and mythical apparitions are contained.*<-

We spoke a little while ago concerning the *Chronicles of the Monks*. But as it pertains to the *Legends of the Saints* <note>original: "legendas sanctorum"; likely a reference to the *Legenda Aurea* or "Golden Legend," a popular medieval collection of saintly biographies</note>, as they call them, the *Storehouses of Examples* <note>original: "promptuaria exemplorum"; these were handbooks used by medieval preachers to find illustrative stories or "exempla" for their sermons</note>, and the *Lives of the Fathers*, in which many apparitions of spirits are contained, we cannot grant much credit to them. For most things that appear in them are hardly plausible; perhaps they wished to instill religious awe and fear in people through those fabricated stories. But now is not the place to discuss these matters.

The same can be said of many superstitious Catholic writers <note>original: "Pontificiis scriptoribus"; literally "Pontificial writers," referring to those aligned with the authority of the Pope</note> who, following these earlier models, wrote most of their accounts based on the reports of others. However, lest anyone think I write these things out of an impulse of envy or hatred, I will provide a single history—or rather, a fable—out of so many, so that you may have an example of what I have just said.

**Peter Damian** <note>c. 1007–1072 AD; a monk and cardinal known for his asceticism and his writings on miracles and ecclesiastical reform</note>, who at the beginning of his mon-

<header>PART ONE</header>

...was of the Benedictine order, and later Bishop of Ostia <note>original: "Hostiensis"; a prestigious bishopric near Rome often held by high-ranking cardinals</note>, whose authority in the Papacy is great on account of his reputation for learning and piety. He relates that a <margin>Saint Severinus, Bishop of Cologne.</margin> certain cleric of Cologne, while crossing a river on horseback at a certain time, saw Saint Severinus, the Bishop of Cologne (who had died not long before and, having been laid in his tomb, had become famous through many miracles) in that very spot.

This figure seized the bridle of the cleric’s horse so that he could proceed no further. *The cleric was struck with amazement and asked why such a great man was detained in that place. The Bishop requested that the cleric offer him his hand, so that he might feel how he was faring. And when he had pressed the cleric's hand into the water, in an instant—because of the immense heat—the flesh dissolved and melted away, so that only the bare bones appeared. The cleric said to him: "Since you are so renowned in the Church, how can it be that you are so tormented?" Severinus replied: "For this reason alone: because I did not complete the canonical hours <note>The "Divine Office" or set times of daily prayer required of the clergy, such as Matins, Lauds, and Vespers</note> at their proper, distinct intervals of time; for I was in the Emperor's court, occupied with his councils. Having recited them all in a heap in the morning, I was entangled in other business for the whole day. Therefore, I bear the punishment of this burning heat. But let us both implore the help of Almighty God to restore your hand." This was done immediately. And*

<keywords>Saint Severinus of Cologne, Peter Damian, purgatory, canonical hours, miracles, apparitions, ecclesiastical duty, Bishop of Ostia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of the apparition of Saint Severinus, Bishop of Cologne, as recorded by Peter Damian. On the previous page, Severinus explained that he was suffering in purgatorial fire because he prioritized the Emperor's administrative business over his scheduled daily prayers (the canonical hours). This page concludes his request for help and transitions into a theological critique of the contemporary clergy.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>On GHOSTS.</header>

[Severinus] said to the Cleric: *"Go, my son, entreat the brothers of our church, and the other Clerics, to pour out prayers for me, and distribute alms to the needy, and persist in the offerings of sacrifices <note>original: "sacrificiorumque votis"; in this context, it refers to the celebration of the Mass offered for the souls in purgatory</note>. For once these things are performed, I shall soon be freed from those torments; and I shall be joined to the side of the choirs of blessed citizens <note>original: "beatorum civium choris"; a poetic way of describing the saints and angels in heaven</note> who are waiting for me."*

From this story, they have argued in this way: if that good and holy Bishop—who was so overwhelmed by a mass of the Emperor's business regarding the utility of the State <note>original: "Reipublicæ"; referring here to the public affairs of the Empire</note> that he did not complete his assigned duty at the appointed hours—was so miserably tortured <note>original: "excarnificatus"; literally "fleshed" or "stripped of flesh," referring back to the previous page where the cleric's hand melted to the bone upon touching the spirit's water</note>, what will happen to those who are hindered by no necessary business, yet coldly perform the <term>canonical hours|The "Divine Office," a specific cycle of daily prayers (like Matins or Vespers) required of clergy at set times</term> all at once, or omit them entirely, so that they might indulge in their own pleasures and vain pursuits? In this argument, no mention is made of the omission of things commanded of us by God. But if the <term>Pontifical Bishops|High-ranking officials of the Roman Catholic Church</term> believe this story to be true, I ask them to consider how they will excuse themselves at God’s judgment, given that they allow themselves to be created Bishops of Churches for which they take no account at all. Instead, they involve themselves only in secular business; or if they do handle Church matters, all their counsels tend toward this [end]...

## CHAPTER XV.

### *It is proven by other reliable writers,*
### *that Specters sometimes appear.*

As for other distinguished writers, they also make mention of ghosts <note>original: "spectrorum"</note> that appear from time to time. **Alexander ab Alexandro of Naples** <note>original: "Alexander ab Alexandro Neapolitanus" (1461–1523), a celebrated Neapolitan lawyer and humanist</note>, **a famous jurist, in Book 2 of *Genial Days* <note>original: "Genialium dierum"; a widely read work of the Renaissance that blended legal knowledge with folklore and ancient customs</note>, chapter 9, writes about a certain associate of his—a man of no small reliability—who took care of a friend's funeral. When he was returning to Rome from there, as night approached, he turned aside to an inn near the road and gave himself over to rest. While he was staying alone and was still awake, suddenly the image of his recently deceased friend approached him. The figure was extremely pale and gaunt, with almost the same facial expression as when he had left him while he was sick. When he had gazed upon him, and...**

### *The Visitation of the Spirit*

This **engraving**, rendered in a style typical of the **17th or 18th century**, illustrates the unsettling encounter described by the Neapolitan humanist **Alexander ab Alexandro**. The scene captures the moment a traveler, resting at an inn, is suddenly confronted by the apparition of a recently departed friend. 

The artist uses heavy cross-hatching to emphasize the gloom of the bedchamber, contrasted against the **light-colored tunic** of the visitor, which suggests a spectral or startling presence. The **canopy bed** <note>A "lit à baldaquin" or tester bed, signifying a person of some status, even while traveling</note> and the **nightcap** worn by the figure in the bed ground the supernatural event in the mundane reality of an evening’s rest interrupted.

<margin>This illustration provides a visual witness to the "reliable writers" cited in the text who attest to the reality of ghosts.</margin>

As noted in the preceding text, the figure of the friend appeared "pale and gaunt," exactly as he had looked in the final days of his illness. The **dynamic pose** of the figure on the left conveys the urgency of a spirit returning to the world of the living to address a former companion.

*Girolamo Cardano of Milan*, <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a celebrated Italian polymath, physician, and mathematician, known for his work on algebra and his encyclopedic interest in the natural and supernatural worlds.</note> a distinguished philosopher and physician, recounts many such things in his books *On Subtlety* and *On the Variety of Things* <note>original: *de Subtilitate* and *varietate rerum*</note>. Those who wish to know about them should consult those books; for I am striving for brevity.

*Olaus Magnus, Archbishop of Uppsala*, <note>Olaus Magnus (1490–1557) was a Swedish clergyman and historian; his description of the Nordic countries was the most influential source on the region for early modern Europeans.</note> in his history *On the Northern Peoples* <note>original: *gentibus Septentrionalibus*</note>, Book 2, Chapter 3, relates that in Iceland, specters appear in the form of people known to the inhabitants. The locals offer their right hands to them before they have heard anything regarding the death of those whose form these spirits have assumed; the trickery is not understood until the figures vanish.

I wanted to present these examples from ancient and more recent historians and other writers so that it may be understood that specters do appear from time to time.

...sexes <note>Completing the phrase "both sexes" from the previous page.</note>, many of whom are still living, while some have departed from this life, have affirmed and still affirm that they have sometimes at night, and sometimes during the day, seen and heard **Specters**. Someone goes to bed and settles down for rest, and something is there, plucking at the blanket or pulling it off; sometimes it sits upon him or even settles itself into the bed; at other times it paces about the bedchamber.

Men have been seen walking on foot, or on horseback, sometimes in a fiery form—individuals known to many, whether they died long ago or recently. It has even happened that certain men who were killed in battles, or taken by a natural death, have called out to their kin and were recognized by their voice.

However, it can be understood from this that it was not always priests <term>sacrificos: literally "sacrificers," a term frequently used by 16th-century writers to refer to Catholic priests, often with a critical or Protestant lens</term> or other bold and impious men who falsely claimed to be souls (as was stated above regarding many cases). # PART ONE

...and overturned things, but afterwards did not replace them in their proper spot, and that they violently disturbed people by making a commotion. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page regarding spirits who cause chaos in the home.</note>

At night in monasteries and solitary places, a noise is sometimes heard, just as if coopers <term>vietores: craftsmen who make and repair wooden casks and barrels</term> were binding and packing wine vessels, or as if other craftsmen were practicing their trades, even though it is certain that everyone has gone to bed and given themselves over to sleep.

<margin>Those building houses hear spirits at night.</margin>
When houses are being built, neighbors hear carpenters <term>fabros lignarios</term>, stonemasons <term>cementarios</term>, and other artisans handling everything just as if they were intent on their daily labor. This omen is received with applause as a lucky sign.

There are those who think this happens by a natural reason <note>original: *Phyſica ratione*; the author is referring to a psychological or "physical" explanation rather than a supernatural one</note>: that what we have heard during the day, we think we also hear at night. I leave this question to be discussed by more learned men.

<margin>There are demons in the mines.</margin>
Miners <term>Metallici: specifically those who work with metals or in mines</term> testify that in some mines, specters <term>Spectra</term> or spirits are seen who are dressed in the manner of other miners. These wander about in the shafts and tunnels <note>original: *cuniculis*</note> and seem to exert themselves in every kind of labor—although they actually do nothing—digging veins of ore, gathering what has been dug up, pouring it into vessels, and turning the machine... <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the next page.</note>

They are said to harm the miners <term>metallicos: specifically those who work in mines or with metals</term> very rarely, unless they are provoked by them with mocking laughter or curses; then they pelt them with gravel, or harm them in other ways. A pious and learned man wrote to me that in *Davos* <note>original: *Davofii*; a high-altitude town in the Swiss Alps</note> in the *Rhaetian Alps*, there is a silver mine <term>argentifodina</term> into which the Consul of that place (they call him the *Landammann* <term>Londamanum: the chief magistrate of a Swiss canton</term>), *Peter Buol*, an excellent man, invested expenses in former years, and from which he gathered no small wealth. 

In that mine, there was a spirit or mountain demon <note>original: *daemon montanus*; a common folkloric figure in mining regions, often known in German as a "Bergmännlein" or "Kobold"</note> who, when the miners were pouring the excavated ores into vessels—usually on Fridays—appeared to be very busy, pouring the metals from one vessel into another at his own whim. The Consul did not take this poorly; whenever he wished to descend into or ascend from the mine, fortifying himself with the sign of the cross, he was never harmed. 

It happened, however, that when that spirit was being very troublesome one day, a certain miner, taking this poorly, attacked him with insults and ordered him to go to the devil <note>original: *abire in malam rem*; literally "to go to a bad end," a common Latin idiom for a curse</note>, adding threats and execrations. That spirit, seizing the worker’s head, twisted it so much that his face was turned toward his back; yet he was not killed, but for a long time afterward [he lived with his head] twisted... <note>The sentence concludes on the following page.</note>

...[twisted it, and] surviving with a twisted or turned neck, he was well known to many who are still living, and only a few years ago he departed from the living <note>meaning he died recently</note>.

*Georgius Agricola* <note>1494–1555; a German scholar known as the "Father of Mineralogy," whose work *De Re Metallica* is a foundational text of mining science</note>, whose very learned books on metallic matters are publicly available, distinguishes two types of those demons who frequent certain mines at the end of his little book *On Subterranean Creatures* <note>original: *de animantibus subterraneis*</note>. For he says that some are fierce and terrible in appearance, who are generally hostile and harmful to miners <term>metallicis: those who work in mines</term>. 

Such a one was a certain *Annebergius* <note>a spirit named after the mining town of Annaberg in Saxony</note> who killed more than twelve workers with his breath in a cave called the *Rose Garland* <note>original: *Corona rosacea*; likely a specific mine shaft name</note>. Indeed, he emitted this breath from an open mouth, for he appeared in the likeness of a horse. 

Of such a kind was also the *Snebergius* <note>a spirit named after Schneeberg, another famous mining town</note>, dressed in a black hood, who in the *Georgian* mine lifted a worker from the ground and placed him in a high spot of a certain very large cavity (which was formerly rich in silver), not without bruising the man's body.

Next, he says some are mild, whom some of the *Germans*—and even the *Greeks*—call *Kobolds* <term>Cobalos: derived from the Greek *kobalos* (knave or goblin), these are the "Kobolds" of German folklore</term>, because they are imitators of men. For they laugh as if leaping with joy, and they seem to do many things, when in fact they do nothing...

<meta>catchword: ...at all.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes an anecdote about a miner left with a permanent neck injury by a spirit, then cites Georgius Agricola's classification of mine demons into two types: the violent and the "Kobolds" who mimic human activity.</summary>

# ON SPECTERS.
<sig>F</sig>

...[they] call them; by others they are named little mountain men <note>original: *virunculos montanos*</note>, signifying the small stature in which they are mostly seen. Moreover, they appear as old men and are dressed in the manner of miners <term>metallicorum: workers in metal mines</term>, that is, wearing a tunic with bands and leather hanging around their loins. These do not cause harm to the miners unless provoked, but rather they imitate them. He writes that they are not unlike those whom the Germans call *Gutelos* <note>likely "Gütel," a type of helpful household or mining spirit</note>, because they seem to be friends to the human race; for they care for the pack animals and perform other tasks. Likewise, they are similar to those called *Trulli* <note>Trolls</note>, who are said to have been in service—assuming the false appearance of either a woman or a man—among other nations, but especially among the *Swedes* <note>original: *Sujonas*; an ancient North Germanic tribe</note>.

Certain things about these *Mining Specters* can also be read in *Olaus Magnus’s* <note>1490–1557; a Swedish writer and archbishop famous for his maps and descriptions of the Nordic world</note> *History of the Northern Peoples* <note>original: *De gentibus Septentrionalibus*</note>, Chapter 10, Book 6.

<margin>Men see these during rest, and when they fall into a trance <note>original: *ecstasin*</note>.</margin>
Those who sail on the Ocean report that in certain places inhabited by *Cannibals* <note>original: *Antropophagi*</note>, many *Specters* dwell, and they afflict those peoples with many evils.

*Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> writes wonderful things in his book *On Divination* <note>original: *de divinatione*</note>. Likewise others. *Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 CE), an influential early Christian theologian</note> himself also recounts here and there in his books that certain deceased individuals warned others about many matters, revealed secret and even future things to them, showed remedies to the sick, and performed other deeds of this kind.

*Avenzoar Albumaron, an Arabic physician* <note>Ibn Zuhr (1094–1162), a famous physician and surgeon from Al-Andalus</note>, writes that he received the best remedy for his own ailing eye from a recently deceased doctor during sleep: as witnessed by *Marsilio Ficino* <note>1433–1499; an Italian scholar and priest who led the revival of Neoplatonism</note> in *On the Immortality of Souls* <note>original: *de immortalitate animorum*</note>, Book 16, Chapter 5.

Holy Scripture also teaches us that God revealed many things to humans through dreams. In *Matthew* chapters 1 and 2, he writes that an Angel appeared several times in dreams to *Joseph, the foster father of Christ our Savior*, and commanded him to be on guard against the traps that were being prepared for *Christ*.

Concerning *Saint Peter* we read in the *Acts of the Apostles*, Chapter 10, that he fell into a trance <term>ecstasin: a state of being "outside oneself" or in a spiritual vision</term>, and saw heaven opened, and a certain vessel—like a great linen sheet tied at its four corners—descending to him, being let down from heaven to the earth. In it were all the four-footed animals of the earth and wild beasts

## CHAPTER XVII.

*Certain omens and portents precede the death of men, battles, and significant changes: sudden crashes or other things of that kind are heard.*

<header>P A R T   O N E</header>

...were accustomed <note>original: *solebant*</note> to do when they were in good health; indeed, the sick themselves often hear these things and immediately sense what is about to happen. Sometimes before they depart from this life, sometimes just after their passing, or even much later, their likenesses or other human forms are seen. It also happens quite often that when our loved ones are about to die, even if they are separated from us by many miles, certain crashes and loud noises <note>original: *fragores & strepitus*</note> are heard.

We sometimes think the house is going to collapse, or that something heavy is falling through the whole house and making a confused sound; months later, we finally realize that these things happened at the very hour when our kin departed from this life. There are families in which no one dies without first observing certain omens <term>praesagia: signs or forewarnings of a future event</term> and preceding signs, such as hearing doors or windows being opened and closed again, something climbing the stairs, wandering through the house, or other occurrences of this kind.

However, it should not be hidden <note>original: *non est dissimulandum*</note> at this point that many superstitious people convince themselves that this or that relative or friend will die shortly. Having believed it was signaled to them by some sign, the reality eventually shows it was <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "vanam" implies "it was a vain or empty premonition."</note>

<header>CONCERNING SPECTERS</header>

...a vain and foolish persuasion. Cardano <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a famous Italian polymath. His work *De subtilitate rerum* and *De varietate rerum* discussed natural wonders and the supernatural.</note> writes in his books *On the Variety of Things* about a certain noble family of Parma, in which, whenever someone is about to die, a certain little old woman is seen beneath the hearth. On one occasion, she appeared when a girl of that family was lying ill; therefore, they despaired for her life, but she was restored from her sickness. Meanwhile, another member of that family who had been in good health suddenly passed away.

I once knew a certain Parish Priest, an honest and pious man, who, while the plague was raging, knew beforehand if anyone in his parish was about to die from the pestilence. For at night, he heard a sound above his bed as if someone were dropping a sack full of grain from their shoulders onto the floorboards. Upon hearing this, he would say, "Once again, someone is bidding me farewell." After it grew light, he would ask who had died that night or who had been seized by the plague, so that he might comfort and encourage them, as befits a good shepherd.

It has also often been observed in Council Chambers <note>original: *Curiis*; referring to the meeting places of high-ranking officials or senators.</note> that if some Senator was near death, a crash was heard near his seat, or some other sign of future death was overheard. The same happens near the pews in churches and in other places where men often <note>The catchword "ver" suggests the next page continues with *versantur*, meaning "are accustomed to spend time" or "frequent."</note>

In monasteries <note>original: *coenobiis*; refers to communal religious houses for monks or nuns.</note>, monks, servants, and others have fallen into fatal illnesses: at night, many have heard their own coffins <note>original: *loculum*; literally a "little place," here meaning a casket or coffin.</note> being prepared for them, in the exact same manner as they were later built by the joiners <note>original: *arculariis*; craftsmen who made chests and coffins.</note>.

In villages, when someone is about to die, in the evening or at night in the cemeteries, people are sometimes heard digging a grave with shovels; on the following day, the grave is dug in the very same way that those witnesses had heard it previously.

Sometimes at night, by the light of the moon, figures have been seen leading a funeral procession with great ceremony according to the custom of the people, or standing before a house as if someone were about to be carried out for burial. Many believe they see their own image, or, as they say, their own soul <note>This refers to the "fetch" or "doppelgänger," a spirit double of a living person.</note>; and many are convinced that unless those who have seen themselves in this way die shortly thereafter, they are destined to live a very long time. But these things are superstitious: let every person prepare himself as if he were to die tomorrow, lest by being complacent <note>original: *securus*; in this context, meaning "careless" or "overly confident" regarding one's spiritual state.</note> he bring misfortune upon himself.

Executioners <term>Carnifices: state officials responsible for carrying out corporal and capital punishment</term> report that they often know whether someone is to be handed over to them for execution: for their swords are said to move of their own accord. Concerning those unhappy men who lay hands upon themselves <note>A historical euphemism for suicide, which was often viewed with a mixture of horror and supernatural dread.</note>, marvelous things happen. Sometimes their corpses had to be carried away over a long journey to be thrown into a flowing stream while enclosed in a leather sack <term>culeo: a sack traditionally used in Roman law for the execution of those who killed their parents, here used for disposing of those who committed suicide</term>. When placed on a wagon or cart, the horses could barely pull them down the hills; yet on the ascent, the horses did not have to labor at all, as the cart was carried along at a rapid pace by its own momentum.

When changes of empires, wars, seditions, and other most difficult times are imminent, marvelous things contrary to the course of nature generally happen in the air, on the earth, and among living creatures. In the second Punic War, the standard-bearer of the first *hastati* <note>The *hastati* were a class of infantry in the early Roman Republic's army, typically younger men who formed the first battle line.</note> could not move the standard from its place; nor did several more soldiers accomplish anything when they approached to help. Josephus records in his books on the *Jewish War* <note>original: "de bello Judaico"; written by Flavius Josephus, a 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian who witnessed the Roman siege of Jerusalem</note> how many wondrous signs preceded the destruction of the city of Jerusalem. On the day of Pentecost <note>A Jewish harvest festival, also known as Shavuot</note>, when the priests were entering the temple to perform their divine duties, they heard a rustling sound and then a voice saying: "Let us depart from here" <note>original Latin: "Migremus hinc"; a famous omen suggesting the divine presence was leaving the Temple</note>. He recounts other signs of this kind as well, which it is not necessary to repeat in this place. On that same night in which *Leo of Constantinople* <note>Leo V, the Armenian, assassinated in 820 AD</note> was murdered in the temple, sailors at sea heard a voice in the air saying: "that Leo has died here" <note>original Greek: "ὅτι ὁ Λέων τῇ δε ἀποπέφανται"</note>.
