# Of Specters.

...spent the night, and that in the dead of night spirits <note>original: "lemures"; in Roman mythology, these were the restless or malevolent ghosts of the dead</note> disturbed the army with a horrible clamor and tumult. That they were indeed spirits was apparent from the fact that they vanished like smoke. 

The same author writes in chapter 26 that in the year of our Lord 1280, while the Parish Priest <note>original: "plebanus"; a term for a priest serving a local community or "plebs"</note> (as they call him) of the church of *Zurich* <note>original: "Tigurinae"</note> was preaching to the people at the tombs or the sepulcher of the Martyrs *Felix* and *Regula* <note>The patron saints of Zurich, legendary members of the Theban Legion who were executed in the 3rd century</note>, who are the patrons of that place, a stone was violently shattered without anyone moving it. It let out a horrible sound like thunder, so that it struck terror into all the people no differently than if the vault <note>original: "testudo"; referring to the arched ceiling or dome of the church</note> of the temple had collapsed. In that same year, on the 3rd day of *October*, a very large part of the town of *Zurich* was destroyed by fire; and uprisings also broke out among the citizens because of Church censures and the "Imperial Ban" <note>original: "banum imperialem"; a form of outlawry or state-sponsored shunning in the Holy Roman Empire</note>, as it is called. 

In the year of our Lord 1440, on the 12th day of the month of *December*, during the dedication of the said temple, a similar crash was heard in the dead of night. Soon indeed there followed a civil war, which the *people of Zurich* waged against the rest of the *Swiss* <note>original: "Helvetios"</note> for seven and more years with varying fortune <note>This refers to the Old Zurich War (1440–1450), a conflict over the succession to the Count of Toggenburg</note>.

### OF SPECTERS.

...that they were seeing a **specter** <note>original: "spectrum"</note>. From this we conclude that it was not unknown to them that specters appeared to men in the waters and on the land. Likewise, when the **Lord** <note>Jesus Christ</note>, having returned to life, appeared to his disciples and wished to make them certain of his true **resurrection**, they thought they were seeing a specter; on this occasion, the Lord does not deny that spirits and specters exist and are sometimes seen, but rather confirms it, distinguishing between himself and spirits or specters <note>This refers to Luke 24:37–39, where Jesus says, "A spirit hath not flesh and bones, as ye see me have."</note>. ## CHAPTER XIX.

->*To whom, when, where, and how Specters appear, and what they achieve.*<-

From all these examples, we clearly understand that many marvelous things are presented to people, and that sometimes <term>Specters</term> <note>original: "Spectra"; here referring to a wide range of supernatural sightings, including ghosts and phantoms</note> are seen and heard. This occurs not only to children, women, those exhausted by illness, the delirious, or otherwise simple-minded people—as some have believed, according to the testimony of *Plutarch* in his *Life of Dion*—but also to the brave and to those who are in full command of their minds.

It cannot be denied, however, that these things happen more frequently to some than to others. This is the case for those such as travelers, watchmen, hunters, carriage drivers, and sailors, who spend their time not only during the day but also at night on the roads, upon the waters, and in the forests, mountains, and valleys. You will find some individuals who, throughout their entire lives, have never seen or heard anything of this sort; on the contrary, there are others who have seen and heard many things. In the same way, there are people who very rarely run into snakes, while there are others who, by contrast, frequently encounter many of them during a journey. It is commonly said that to those...

<margin>Some see and hear things, while others do not.</margin>
It also often happens that someone sees or hears something very clearly, yet another who stands beside him or accompanies him sees nothing and hears nothing.

In the *history of Elisha* <note>original: "historia Helisæi"; referring to the biblical account in 2 Kings 6:17</note>, we read that he himself saw chariots of fire and horsemen on the mountain, while his servant <note>original: "puerum"; literally "boy," but used here for his attendant</note> saw nothing until the prophet prayed that, for his confirmation and consolation, the Lord might deign to open his eyes so that he too might be able to see this great miracle. Likewise, in the *Acts of the Apostles, chapter 9*, it is read that Christ struck *Paul* down before *Damascus* and spoke to him, and his companions heard a voice.
<margin>The question is resolved.</margin>
Later, in *chapter 22*, *Paul* himself—while in the barracks at *Jerusalem* in the presence of *Lysias* <note>Claudius Lysias, the Roman commander</note> and telling the whole people what had happened—says that they did not hear the voice of the one speaking with him. These accounts do not contradict each other. For the meaning is that they indeed heard a voice or sound, but they did not understand what the Lord spoke to him.

<margin>The <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "dæmonium"; in the Socratic context, this refers to a "daimon," a divine inner voice or guiding spirit rather than an evil demon</note> of Socrates.</margin>
*Plato* writes in the *Theages* <note>a dialogue attributed to Plato concerning the nature of wisdom</note> that *Socrates* had a familiar spirit by which he was usually warned to desist from acting whenever what he was about to do was not going to turn out successfully. He did not see this spirit himself, and others did not hear it. It is sometimes said that children see certain things,

<margin>At what time specters may appear.</margin>
As for the time when Specters appear, we read in histories that after the fateful thousand years <note>A reference to the millennium described in Revelation 20, which many medieval and Reformation-era writers believed had ended, signaling a period of increased demonic activity.</note>—during which *Saint John* in the *Book of Revelation* <note>original: "Apocalypsi"</note> predicted that *Satan* was to be released from his chains; that is, that errors, superstitions, and crimes would spread throughout the world—many Specters appeared everywhere. For people attributed almost more authority to them than to the *Scriptures* themselves. A spirit appeared or was heard, which said that if this or that were established—for instance, a vowed pilgrimage, or a chapel—it would be a form of worship most pleasing to God. Bishops and parish priests did not weigh whether those things agreed with the Holy Scriptures or not, etc. Specters appeared in the past and still appear today by day and night, but especially at night: and before the dead of night, during the first sleep. Likewise, they appear on Fridays, Saturdays, and on fast days, so that they may confirm superstitions. We should not wonder, however, that these things are heard more often at night than by day. *For he by whose authority these things are mostly done is called the "Prince of Darkness" in the Holy Scriptures, and he flees from the light of the Divine Word.*

And although indeed in all places these things are hea-

# Concerning Specters. 127

...deceased, sometimes in the form of a man unknown to us.

I heard from a grave and prudent man, a governor of the *Zurich* <note>original: "Tigurinae"; the Latin name for Zurich, Switzerland.</note> district, who asserted that while traveling through pastures with his servant in the early morning during summer, he saw someone very well known to him committing a wicked act with a mare <note>original: "equae sese nefariè commisceret"; a reference to bestiality, which was a capital crime in the early modern period.</note>: stunned, he turned back on his journey, knocked on the doors of the man he thought he had seen, and discovered for certain that the individual had never left his bedroom. And unless he had investigated everything diligently, a good and honest man would have been thrown into prison and subjected to tortures, etc.

I recount this story for this reason: so that judges may be cautious in cases of this kind. For the Devil often lays traps for the innocent in this way. *Cunigunde*, the wife of *Emperor Henry II*, was suspected of adultery, and rumors were spread that she was too familiar with a certain noble youth in the court. For a diabolical appearance <note>original: "species"; here meaning a ghostly or illusory image.</note> of that youth was often seen emerging from the Empress’s <note>original: "Augustae"; a title for the Empress.</note> bedroom. She later declared her innocence by walking over glowing plowshares <note>A form of "trial by ordeal" where a person walked over hot iron to prove their innocence through divine intervention.</note> (as was customary in those times) without any injury to her feet. *Albert Krantz, book four, chapter five of the Metropolis.* <note>Albert Krantz (1448–1517) was a German historian; "Metropolis" is his history of the ecclesiastical history of Saxony.</note>

->### PART ONE<-

It is read that many spirits appeared to hermits and monks in the clothing and form of women, who enticed them to sexual matters <note>original: "res venereas"; a reference to Venus, the goddess of love, used here to mean lustful or carnal acts.</note>. <margin>*Dances of Spirits*</margin>
<margin>*Pans. Fauns. Satyrs.*</margin>
*Olaus Magnus writes in Book 3, Chapter 11, On the Northern Peoples*, that even in our own age, certain monsters, or spirits assuming some form, in many places of the Northern regions—especially at night—lead dances to the harmony of various kinds of musical instruments. The inhabitants call this the dance of the Elves <note>original: "choream Elvarum"; likely referring to the Scandinavian folk belief in the "elf-dance" or "älvlek".</note>. Certain things regarding this matter are also read in *Saxo Grammaticus in the History of the Danes*. Not dissimilar to these are the reports that *Pomponius Mela* gives in his *third book in the description of Ethiopia*, that beyond *Mount Atlas in Mauritania*, lights were very often seen at night, and the clashing of cymbals and the songs of flutes were heard; yet no one was found there during the day. *Solinus in chapters 38 and 44* writes that on this very mountain, Aegipanes <term>Aegipanes: ancient mythological creatures, often depicted with the legs of a goat and the body of a man, similar to Pan.</term> hold their dances everywhere; *Pliny* also mentions these in *Book 5, chapter 1*. They perceive these to be Pans, Fauns, and Satyrs, about whom many accounts survive among the ancients.

*Saint Jerome writes in the Life of Paul the Hermit*, that a Hippocentaur <term>Hippocentaur: a specific type of centaur with the body of a horse and the torso of a man.</term> appeared to *Antony*...

In a rocky valley, he says, he saw a little man <note>original: "homunculum"; a diminutive form of man, often used in folklore to describe spirits or sprites.</note> of no great size, with a hooked nose and a forehead roughened with horns, whose lower body ended in goat's feet. Undaunted even by this Spectacle, *Anthony* seized the shield of Faith and the breastplate of Hope, like a good warrior. Nevertheless, the mentioned animal was offering him palm fruits as provisions for the journey, like hostages of peace. Recognizing this, *Anthony* checked his step, and asking who he might be, received this answer from him:

> I am a mortal, and one of the inhabitants of the Wilderness, whom the pagans—deluded by various errors—worship, calling us Fauns, Satyrs, and Incubi. <term>Incubi: in this context, the speaker refers to woodland spirits; traditionally, these were spirits thought to haunt or lie upon sleepers.</term> I fulfill an embassy for my flock: We pray that you would entreat the common God on our behalf, whom we have learned has come for the salvation of the world, etc.

At this point, we must not pass over in silence that memorable story which *Plutarch* recounts in his little book *On the Failure of Oracles* <note>original: "de defectu oraculorum"; a famous work by Plutarch discussing why the ancient Greek oracles had ceased to speak.</note>, as translated by that most learned man *Adrianus Turnebus*, in these words:

Regarding the death of demons, I received a certain narration from a man neither foolish nor vain: for it was from *Aemilianus the Rhetorician*, from whom some of you also [heard] this...

### ON SPECTERS.

...heard; *Epitherfes* was the father, my fellow countryman and a professor of *Grammar* <term>Grammar: in this period, the study of grammar encompassed not just syntax but the broad study of classical literature and philology.</term>: he used to relate that once, while intending to travel to *Italy*, he had boarded a ship which carried not only a great weight of merchandise but also a large crowd of passengers. Towards evening, near the *Echinades* islands, the wind died down completely. While the ship drifted on the open sea and was finally carried to the islands of *Paxi*, many were still awake, and many were drinking together after dinner. Suddenly, a voice was heard from *Paxi* calling out for someone named *Thamus*, so loudly that they were filled with wonder. Now, *Thamus* was an *Egyptian* pilot, but his name was unknown to many who were on the ship. Although he was called twice, he remained silent; however, the third time he was called, he answered. Then the voice, with an even greater exertion of sound, commanded that when he had been carried as far as *Palodes*, he should *announce that the great Pan is dead* <note>original: "Pana magnum mortuum esse nunciaret"; this is one of the most famous legends of antiquity. Early Christian scholars, such as Eusebius, argued that this event coincided with the crucifixion of Christ, signaling the death of the old pagan gods and their oracles.</note>. Upon hearing this, *Epitherfes* said that they were all struck with amazement and terror. As they deliberated whether the command ought to be carried out or not, *Thamus* decided that if a wind were blowing, they should sail past in silence; but if there were calm and tranquility from the winds in that place, he would proclaim what he had heard. Therefore, when they arrived at *Palodes* and found that no breeze...

Because so many people had been present, he related that the news of the event spread very quickly in **Rome**, and **Thamus** was summoned by **Tiberius Caesar**. **Tiberius** gave such weight to this matter that he questioned and inquired as to who that **Pan** might be. The learned men, of whom he had many around him, judged that he was the **Pan** born of **Mercury** and **Penelope**, and so forth.

## Of Specters.

...[it was] told that it was announced that our Lord and God had suffered. For ***Pan*** signifies "all" <note>The Greek word πᾶν (pan) translates to 'everything' or 'the whole'. In this Christian interpretation, the death of the pagan god Pan at the time of the Crucifixion symbolizes the suffering of the Lord of all nature.</note>: and thus the Lord of the whole and entire nature had suffered.

He also adds that ***Theodosius*** <note>Likely Macrobius (Macrobius Ambrosius Theodosius), a 5th-century Roman writer whose work *Saturnalia* discusses the nature of gods and the origins of myths.</note> says that the ***Arcadians*** <note>Inhabitants of Arcadia, a region of Greece traditionally associated with the god Pan and pastoral life.</note> worship this God, calling him **the lord of matter** <note>original Greek: τὸν τῆς ὕλης κύριον</note>, intending to signify not merely the lord of ***forests***, but the ***ruler of the entire material substance***. *The power of this nature composes the essence of all bodies, whether they be divine or earthly. Although he refers this to the Sun, if one pays close attention, the mysteries reach deeper, etc.*

<margin>Diabolical hunts</margin>
To this topic belong the reports concerning the **hunt of demons** <note>This refers to the "Wild Hunt," a widespread folklore motif involving a phantasmal group of hunters or spirits traveling across the sky or ground, often seen as an omen of catastrophe.</note>, as well as the **dances of the dead**, which are not all of a single kind. I have heard of certain people who claimed to have seen them with their own eyes. No one can list all the forms in which spirits have appeared. For indeed the **evil spirit** <term>cacodæmon: derived from the Greek *kakodaimon*, meaning an evil spirit or malevolent entity, as opposed to a *agathodaimon* or good spirit.</term>, who is usually the author of these things, can transform himself into all shapes, just as the poets tell stories of **Proteus** <note>Proteus was a prophetic sea-god in Greek mythology who could change his shape at will to escape those seeking his knowledge.</note>.

I recall hearing such a story from a pious and learned man named *Johannes Willing* <note>Johannes Willing (1525–1572) was a prominent Reformed theologian and preacher who served in various German territories.</note>, a long-time friend of mine. He told of a certain man in the county of *Hanau* <note>A territory within the Holy Roman Empire, located in present-day Hesse, Germany.</note> who, just a few years ago, encountered a certain *Specter* <term>Specter: from the Latin 'spectrum', referring to a ghostly apparition or a demonic vision intended to deceive or terrify.</term> at night. The man was so transformed by the experience that when he returned home, even his own daughters did not recognize him.

*Specters* frequently disturb people’s sleep; they even cause many to be forced to abandon their homes, such that they cannot even rent them out to others. Sometimes they overturn objects, shake people, or pelt them with stones, thereby damaging both their property and their physical health. Occasionally, if God permits, they even take their lives. It often happens that those who have seen or heard *Specters*, or have been "blasted" by their breath <note>The Latin 'afflati sunt' refers to the belief that the "breath" or influence of a spirit could cause physical illness or stroke.</note>, suffer from a swelling of the mouth and the entire face. They may also be seized by a sudden madness—a fact well known through experience.

I also recall an incident where certain individuals, believing they saw armed men coming to capture them, attempted to lay hands upon themselves <note>A common euphemism for suicide or self-harm.</note>—a deception that can be brought about by the trickery of *Satan*. *Specters* also occasionally harass livestock in the pastures during the night.

This concludes the first part of this little treatise, in which I hope I have made it clear

# OF SPECTERS.

...and to have proven that, although there are many who falsely persuade themselves that they have seen *Specters* <term>Specter: from the Latin 'spectrum', referring to a ghostly apparition or a spirit that manifests to the senses.</term>, or have mistaken one thing for another, *Specters* nonetheless exist, and other wondrous things also happen from time to time.

I have added details regarding those to whom they especially appear, as well as where, when, how, or in what forms they manifest, and what they sometimes achieve or bring about.

He who dares to utterly deny these manifold and consistent testimonies of the *ancients and the moderns* <note>original: "veterum & recentiorum". The author refers to both classical Greek and Roman writers and contemporary 16th-century witnesses.</note> seems, in my judgment, unworthy of our belief when he affirms anything at all. For just as it is a sign of great fickleness <note>The Latin 'levitas' implies a lack of intellectual gravity or a tendency to be easily swayed by rumors.</note> to immediately believe all those who claim to have seen *Specters*, so on the other hand, it is the greatest insolence if one rashly and shamelessly rejects all those things affirmed by so many trustworthy *historians*, *Church Fathers* <note>original: "Patribus". These are the influential Christian theologians of the first several centuries whose writings carry great weight in doctrine.</note>, and other serious men of great authority.

### CONCERNING SPECTERS.

of the dead, or not; what the ancients thought of them, and what the *Holy Scriptures* <note>original: "Sacræ litteræ"</note> teach us about these things.
<margin>Plato</margin> Plato <note>The famous Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BC) who discussed the fate of the soul in his dialogues, particularly the 'Phaedo'.</note> thinks that excellent and heroic souls, being purer, escape upward; others, who were lazy and contaminated by the pleasures of the body, wander the earth near tombs <note>original: "sepulchra"</note>, and he judges these to be the *Specters* <note>original: "Spectra"</note> which are often seen.
Other pagan <note>original: "ethnici"</note> writers also say they are moved to think that the souls of men survive after death because it is quite certain that many *Specters* wander about everywhere, and are often heard and seen; they often even converse with men, for they believe most of them to be souls. That most ancient writer *Tertullian* <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage.</note> in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "de Anima"</note>, writes that the wise men of the pagans <note>original: "gentilium"</note> who determined the soul to be immortal (for some, like the *Epicureans* <note>Followers of the philosopher Epicurus, who held that the soul was composed of atoms and perished at the same time as the body.</note>, thought that souls died along with the bodies) taught that the souls of wise men, if they depart from the body, have their seats in the heights, while the souls of others are cast down to the underworld.
To this, the pagans believe that for souls, unless the bodies from which they were separated by death are properly buried <note>original: "legitimè sepeliantur"; referring to the performance of necessary funeral rites.</note>, they must wander before they find rest.

For this reason, it was considered a horrible thing to cast someone aside unburied: a sentiment that can be seen everywhere among the Poets. *Hector, in the works of Homer, begs Achilles not to throw his corpse to the dogs and birds, but to hand it over to his elderly father Priam and his mother Hecuba for burial.* <note>Referencing the 'Iliad', where the Trojan prince Hector pleads with his killer, Achilles, to respect his remains.</note> *Patroclus appears to Achilles in a dream and asks that he perform the proper funeral rites for him; otherwise, he cannot approach the gates of the underworld, as the spirits of those already buried drive him far away,* <note>Patroclus, Achilles' companion, represents the Greek belief that the soul cannot find rest until the body is honored.</note> which example Tertullian <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155–220 AD), an early Christian author who often critiqued pagan customs while utilizing their literature.</note> cites, while simultaneously refuting this empty opinion of the pagans. *Palinurus asks Aeneas* in Virgil's writings, *that he throw earth upon him, which is to say, that he erect a cenotaph for him:* <note>In the 'Aeneid', the pilot Palinurus begs Aeneas for a symbolic burial so his soul may find peace.</note> for this was the name they gave to tombs in which someone was not actually interred, yet which were built in their honor. Virgil writes of the *shade of Deiphobus being carried across*, for whom *Aeneas* had built a cenotaph; for they say that even an empty and symbolic burial <note>original: "inanem & imaginariam sepulturam"</note> is effective. ### ON SPECTERS

...had been extinguished by death; an opinion which **Tertullian** <note>original: "Tertullianus." Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155–220 AD) was a prolific early Christian author who argued against many pagan superstitions.</note> also attacks. **Plato** writes in Book 9 of *The Laws*:
> the souls of those who have been killed pursue their assassins to harm them:

unless you understand this metaphorically, it is likewise to be rejected.

<margin>The faith of the Jews.</margin>
Among the Jews, the universal faith was that the souls of the dead either rest—namely, if they died in the faith of the **promised Messiah**—or are damned, if they departed from the living in their sins without repentance; however, they do not return to these lands. For **Job** says in chapter 7:
> Just as a cloud fades and passes away, so he who descends to the underworld shall not ascend, nor shall he return to his house, etc. <note>Job 7:9–10. The author uses this to show that the Hebrew Bible views death as a one-way journey, contradicting the idea of restless ghosts.</note>

If you should say that this was merely Job's personal view, it is written of **David**, that when he was in the greatest dangers and death was visible before his eyes, he prayed in **Psalm 31**:
> Into your hands, Lord, I commend my spirit.

**Ecclesiastes**, chapter 12:
> The spirit shall return to God who gave it.

In the **Book of Wisdom** (which is attributed by the ancients to **Philo** <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BC – 50 AD). Although the Book of Wisdom is now considered part of the Biblical Apocrypha, historical scholars often debated its authorship.</note>), chapter 3, it is written:
> The souls of the righteous are in the hand of God, and no torment shall touch them.

On the other hand, the souls of the wicked descend into hell. In **Psalm 49**, it is written concerning the wealthy, who on account of their riches [deviated] <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword suggests the following page continues describing how they "defied" or "fell away" from God.</note> from God and his commands...

The author uses this to emphasize that the dead are confined to a specific place and do not wander.</note>, etc. If the **Jews** had believed that souls were tortured in purgatory, they would undoubtedly have had at least one kind of sacrifice for redeeming souls, or at least for mitigating their tortures, among the many types of sacrifices they offered for the sins of the living. On the contrary, in **2 Samuel** 12, **David** says of his young son that he cannot recall him; he says that he himself will go to the boy, but the boy will not return to him. **Sirach** <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon.</note>, chapter 38, says: *From death there is no return.* Regarding the appearance of **Samuel** <note>This refers to the "Witch of Endor" narrative in 1 Samuel 28, where a medium appears to summon the spirit of the prophet Samuel. The author is referencing a Rabbinic interpretation of 1 Kings 22:21, where a "spirit" stands before God and offers to deceive the wicked King Ahab.</note>.

# CHAPTER II.

## *The doctrine of the Papists regarding souls and their appearance.*

In the past few centuries, the **Papists** <note>A term used by Reformation-era writers to refer to members of the Roman Catholic Church.</note> have publicly taught and written that the ghosts <note>original: *Spectra*</note> which are seen and heard are either good or bad angels, or the souls of those who dwell in eternal happiness, in purgatory, or in the place of the damned. They claim that most of these are actually souls seeking help and liberation from living people. Furthermore, so that this doctrine of theirs and the entire matter may be understood more clearly, we must examine it a little more fully from their own books.

*Jacobus de Clusa* <note>James of Clusa (c. 1381–1465), also known as Jacobus de Paradiso, was a famous Carthusian monk and professor of theology whose writings on the soul were influential.</note>, a Carthusian and doctor of theology, wrote a book concerning the appearance of souls separated from their bodies; it was printed in Burgdorf...

<header>PART TWO</header>
<page-num>141</page-num>

...dorf <note>completing the word "Burgdorf" from the previous page</note>, in a town under the jurisdiction of **Bern**, in the year of **Christ's** birth 1475; a book which contains many superstitious things.

<margin>The four receptacles of souls.</margin>
The Papal doctors, in their commentaries on the fourth book of the *Sentences* <note>original: *Sententiarum*. This refers to the *Sentences* of Peter Lombard, the standard theological textbook used in universities during the Middle Ages.</note>, establish four receptacles or places into which souls separated from their bodies are carried. They say that three of these receptacles are eternal, while one lasts only for a defined period of time.

<margin>The empyrean heaven.</margin>
The first receptacle, or the first place, is the empyrean heaven <note>The highest part of the heavens, believed by medieval scholars to be a place of pure light and fire (from the Greek *pyr*) where God and the blessed reside.</note>, which is the seat of the blessed; in the Holy Scriptures, this is called by another name: *paradise*. The second place is hell <note>original: *infernus*</note> beneath the earth, the dwelling place of demons, unbelievers, adulterers, and wicked Christians who depart from this life in mortal sin without repentance. The third is the *limbo of children* <note>original: *limbus puerorum*</note>, for the children of both the faithful and the unfaithful who depart from this world without the sacrament of baptism. They say that these children will live there forever without sensible pain <note>meaning physical or sensory suffering</note>, yet they are to be deprived of the "beatific vision" or the face of God. Therefore, they claim that the bodies of these infants should not be buried in consecrated cemeteries after death.

<margin>Purgatory.</margin>
The fourth place is *Purgatory*, which they believe is prepared for those who have departed from this life without mortal sins; or, if they have committed mortal sins, they have nevertheless [done] peni- <note>The sentence continues on the next page with "penance."</note>

<summary>This page outlines the medieval Catholic framework of the afterlife, categorizing the four "receptacles" for souls: Heaven, Hell, the Limbo of Children, and Purgatory. It specifically notes the theological status of unbaptized infants and the criteria for entry into each spiritual realm.</summary>

The author is referring to Roman Catholic theologians or "doctors of the Church."</note> relate marvelous things. Some say that ***purgatory*** is also beneath the earth, just like hell. For indeed, they say that in this or that mountain, river, or valley, this or that soul has been seen in the place where it committed a sin; and they claim these are "particular purgatories" assigned to them by God for a special reason before the Day of Judgment. After that day, all ***purgatories***, both the general one and the particular ones, shall cease to exist.

Some say that the punishment of ***purgatory*** is the same as the punishment of ***hell***; differing only in this: that the former has a set end, while the latter does not. They even claim it would be easier to bear every punishment of this world—everything that all people have suffered from the time of ***Adam*** until the day of the final judgment—than to endure even the slightest of those [purgatorial] punishments for a single day. Likewise, they say that our earthly fire, if it were compared to the ***fire of purgatory***, is like a...

***Richard of Middleton*** <note>original: *Richardus de media villa*. A 13th-century Franciscan theologian known for his commentaries.</note>, a Franciscan, writes in his commentary on the *Sentences* that he believes souls are carried by good angels to the places of torment; however, these angels do not personally punish them, because the souls are destined to be their fellow citizens in heaven. Nevertheless, it is possible that demons are present and take pleasure in the souls' sufferings. I thought these details about purgatory should be presented at somewhat greater length so that you might see how even the doctors themselves disagree on a doctrine of such great importance—a doctrine through which they have despoiled people of their wealth <note>This is a critique of the sale of indulgences and paid masses for the dead.</note> and cast them into such great sorrow.

In what manner people ought to behave when spirits appear.

an investigation of these apparitions ought to be preceded by fasts and prayers: it is expressed in *chapters 10 and 11 of Daniel* that *Daniel* did this. Likewise, the confession of sins and the celebration of Masses should precede it. Furthermore, one should not immediately grant belief when some sign is heard, but one must wait until we hear it three times, just as is read concerning the boy *Samuel* in *1 Samuel, chapter 3*. Having entered the place, they must sprinkle it with holy water and incense it; they also believe that a holy stole <note>A vestment consisting of a narrow strip of fabric worn around the neck by priests, symbolizing their sacramental authority.</note> would not be useless. ...that spirit will [not] harm a person in their physical body. For if such a spirit were to harm someone, it would make itself suspect that it was not good.

<margin>By what signs good spirits may be distinguished from evil ones.</margin>
Catholic writers <note>original: *Pontificii scriptores*. Literally "Pontifical writers," referring to Roman Catholic theologians and demonologists.</note> further provide four ways by which good spirits can be distinguished from evil ones. First, they say that if it is a good spirit, it will indeed bring some fear with it initially, but it immediately lifts people up and comforts them. In this way, Gabriel comforted the Blessed Virgin <note>original: *divam virginem*. A common Latin title for Mary at the time of the Annunciation.</note>, who was troubled by his greeting. They also offer other examples. Second, a spirit can be recognized by its visible appearance <note>term: *appearance* (species). In the context of the supernatural, this refers to the outward form or shape assumed by a spiritual being to make itself visible to human eyes.</note>. If they appear in the form of a lion, a bear, a dog, a toad, a serpent, a cat, or a black shadow, it is easily concluded that the spirit is evil. Good spirits appear in the form of a dove, a man, a lamb, or in the brightness of the sun and light.

One must also consider whether the voice that is heard is sweet, humble, modest, and sorrowful, or if it is terrifying and rebuking; for that is how they speak.

Third, one must observe whether the spirit says anything that conflicts with the doctrine of the Apostles and the doctors approved by the Church; likewise with the faith, good morals, and the rites of the Church according to Canon Law <note>term: *Canon Law* (jura canonica). The internal legal system and regulations of the Catholic Church.</note> or the coun-

If these were not the souls of the dead imploring help and assistance, but *diabolical specters* <note>original: *Spectra diabolica*</note>, they would not command people to pray, fast, and give alms; for devils hate all these things, just as they hate other good works.

## CHAPTER III.

### *What followed from that doctrine of the Papists <note>original: "Papistarum." A term used by Protestant reformers to refer to members of the Roman Catholic Church who followed the authority of the Pope.</note> regarding the appearance of souls.*

By these means, it was brought about that it was commonly believed that those things which were seen and heard were the souls of the dead, and that whatever they said was to be believed without hesitation. And so the true and simple doctrine of calling upon God *through Christ alone*, *of trust in the merit and redemption of Christ*, and *of the true works of Christian charity*, was more and more weakened and suppressed. Gradually, as the *Holy Scriptures* were abandoned and rejected, the traditions and mandates of men began to be held in great authority—indeed, in greater authority than the *Word of God* itself. It was regarded as the greatest sin if anyone dared to violate the mandates of men. Purgatory <term>purgatory (purgatorium): in Catholic tradition, a place or state of suffering inhabited by the souls of sinners who are expiating their sins before going to heaven.</term> relies especially upon these appearances of spirits as its foundation. From the speeches of these spirits, the Papal teachers taught that men [obtain eternal] <note>The text breaks here; "eternal" (æternam) is the catchword for the following page.</note> salvation through their own merits and the merits of others

H
<meta>catchword: eternal-</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the Catholic Church's reliance on ghost stories to support the doctrine of Purgatory, arguing that such beliefs led believers away from the Bible and the sole merit of Christ. It reflects the Protestant view that traditions based on "apparitions" undermined the authority of the Word of God.</summary>
<keywords>purgatory, Protestant Reformation, apparitions, salvation by faith, authority of scripture, merit, ghosts</keywords>

to obtain eternal [life]: a belief that rendered them complacent and lazy. For if anyone believes that he can hire men for a price to do this or that for the liberation of the soul of the deceased, he postpones the amendment of his own life, or neglects it entirely. Therefore, the same thing happened to them as happened to the *five foolish virgins* <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25, where those who were unprepared were locked out of the wedding feast.</note>, of whom we read in the *Gospel of Matthew, chapter 25.* Through these apparitions, the following things gradually gained great authority, despite the fact that they could not be proven from the *Holy Scriptures*: Masses, images, satisfactions <term>satisfactions: penances or works performed to make amends for sins</term>, pilgrimages for the sake of religion, relics of saints, monastic vows, holy days <note>original: "feriae"</note>, auricular confession <term>auricular confession: the practice of confessing sins privately into the ear of a priest</term>, and other forms of worship and ceremonies. In short, all things which could not be justified by the Bible. Matters were brought to such a point that many pious and simple people deprived themselves <note>original: "genium suum fraudarint," a classical idiom meaning to skimp on one's own needs or pleasures</note> so that they might have the means to feed idle monks and priests, and to offer gifts to images. They founded chapels, altars, monasteries, perpetual lamps, anniversaries, fraternities, and other such things, so that they might redeem their loved ones from the torments of purgatory. For those nocturnal ghosts <note>original: "lemures." In Roman mythology, these were restless or malevolent spirits of the dead.</note> commanded them to do these things; sometimes, wills and testaments had to be changed at the urging of these spirits.

155
<center>ON GHOSTS.</center>

<margin>They amass wealth.</margin>
They have greatly increased the size of parishes, colleges, and monasteries through annual revenues: they have acquired for themselves the finest estates, vineyards, fields, meadows, game preserves, fishponds, serfs <note>original: "mancipia"</note>, legal jurisdictions, great lordships, and the "power of the sword" <term>power of the sword: the high legal authority to execute capital punishment</term>. For after people firmly believed that the souls of the dead could return, most did whatever those spirits demanded.

However, so that it may be more clearly understood how much value people placed on those visions and others of this kind—and how they instituted new forms of worship because of them—I will recount one or two historical accounts.

*Martin the Pole <note>original: "Martinus Polonus," a 13th-century Dominican chronicler</note>, Archbishop of Cosenza and penitentiary to the High Pontiff, writes in his Chronicles that Clement IV canonized Saint Hedwig <note>original: "Eduergia," referring to St. Hedwig of Silesia</note> at Viterbo. She was a widow and Duchess of Poland of remarkable holiness. Among other memorable events, when her canonization had been delayed for several years, she herself appeared in a vision to the advocate <note>original: "procuratori"</note> in the Roman court while he was handling this very business. Seeing him in a state of sadness, she assured him of the success of the case and even informed him of the exact day it would be completed. Canonization is called "apotheosis" <note>original: "apotheosis," the ancient Greek process of deifying a human</note> by the heathens, from whom the practice takes its origin.*

<margin>The Feast of All Saints.</margin>
*Johannes Trithemius <note>original: "Ioannes Trittenhemius," a famous German Benedictine abbot and historian</note>, Abbot of Span-*

156
<center>PART TWO</center>

Sigebert of Gembloux <note>original: "Sigebertus Gemblacensis," an influential 11th-century monk and historian whose "Chronica" was widely read in the Middle Ages.</note>, a man of great authority, writes in his Chronicles that the commemoration of all the faithful souls <note>This refers to All Souls' Day, observed on November 2nd.</note> was established to be celebrated because a certain monk, returning from *Jerusalem*, was received as a guest by a hermit in *Sicily* near Mount *Etna*, which spews flames. There, he learned from the hermit that many souls of the deceased were tortured in those flames, but were being freed from them through the prayers of the faithful—a fact the hermit asserted had been confirmed to him by the testimony of spirits. 

Regarding this matter, *Polydore Vergil* also writes in *On the Inventors of Things* <note>original: "De inventoribus rerum," a famous Renaissance encyclopedia of "firsts" or origins of customs and technologies.</note>, book 6, chapter 9. You will find in *Peter de Natalibus* <note>A 14th-century bishop known for his massive "Catalogus Sanctorum" or Catalog of Saints.</note>, book 10, chapter 1, that the Feast of All Saints has the same origin. From this, you may conclude that these festivals were instituted based on the reports of appearing spirits. 

A very similar story is found in the writings of *John of Damascus*, who writes about *Macarius* <note>Likely St. Macarius of Alexandria or Macarius the Great, famous "Desert Fathers" of the 4th century.</note>: when he was pouring out prayers for the deceased according to his custom, he desired to know whether they were of any benefit to them and whether they received any comfort from them. God, wishing to reveal this to his servant, breathed the word of truth into a skull that had dried up. For the skull broke out into these words: "When you pray for the dead, we feel a small measure of comfort <note>original: "consolatiunculam," a diminutive expressing a slight or meager relief.</note>."

### ON SPECTERS.

<margin>The Origin of the Carthusians</margin>
The Carthusian order <note>A Catholic religious order of enclosed monastics, known for their commitment to silence, solitude, and strict asceticism.</note>, which is commonly held to be the most holy and severe, had its beginning in the *University of Paris* <note>original: "Academia Parisiensi"</note> for a very similar reason (concerning which the monks of this family themselves published a specific book). This took place around the year 1080, as *Polydore Vergil* <note>An Italian humanist and priest who wrote a famous history of "firsts" or origins.</note> testifies. A certain very celebrated Doctor, famous for his erudition and the integrity of his life, departed from the living. When he was about to be buried in a certain church, he cried out in a horrible voice: "By the just judgment of God, I have been accused" <note>original: "Justo Dei judicio accusatus sum"</note>. Therefore, because of the many people flocking from all sides to this spectacle, they left the bier in the church for three days. On the second day, he cried out: "By the just judgment of God, I have been judged" <note>original: "Justo Dei judicio judicatus sum"</note>. On the third day: "By the just judgment of God, I have been condemned" <note>original: "Justo Dei judicio condemnatus sum"</note>. Some writers, such as *Vincent of Beauvais* <note>original: "Vincentius Bellovacensis," a medieval Dominican scholar and encyclopedist.</note>, add—perhaps of their own accord—that he raised himself up from the bier three times. Since no one would have ever suspected that this famous and distinguished man had perished eternally, everyone was struck with amazement.

<margin>Souls are gathered either to the heavens or to the underworld</margin>
Consequently, *Bruno*, a master of theology and a native of Cologne, immediately abandoned all his possessions and, accompanied by six pious men, withdrew to a deserted place which was called *Chartreuse* <note>original: "Carthusia"</note>, in the diocese of *Grenoble* <note>original: "Gratianopolitana"</note>, where [he founded] the first monastery...

### C H A P T E R IV.

->*Testimonies of the Divine Word <note>The "Divine Word" refers to the Holy Scriptures or the Bible.</note> showing that neither*<-
->*the souls of the faithful nor the unfaithful,*<-
->*after they have been separated from their bodies,*<-

That neither the souls of the faithful nor the unfaithful wander in these lands after they have departed from their bodies can be understood from the following arguments. For it is certain that men are either endowed with true faith, or are devoid of faith, when they must depart from this life. Those who depart from here in true faith, their souls immediately attain eternal life; those who migrate from here without faith, immediately become sharers of eternal damnation.

Souls do not vanish, nor do they perish with the body, as the Epicureans <note>Followers of the ancient philosopher Epicurus; in the early modern period, "Epicurean" was often a slur for those who denied the immortality of the soul or the afterlife.</note> believe, nor are they everywhere as some confusedly claim. ## PART TWO

He <note>Referring to Tertullian, the author mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> says that evil spirits use this trick of pretending to be the souls of the dead, etc.

Regarding the fact that the *underworld* <note>original: "inferi"</note> is open to no soul (namely, in a way that allows them to exit), he claims that the Lord sufficiently decreed this in that argument of the resting *Poor Man* and the groaning *Rich Man*, speaking through the person of *Abraham*. <note>This refers to the biblical parable of Lazarus and the Rich Man in Luke 16, where Abraham states that a great chasm prevents anyone from crossing between the afterlife and the living.</note> He declares that a messenger cannot be sent back to renounce the arrangement of hell, etc. And although some of his errors were noted by the ancients, no one has ever criticized this particular opinion of his.

<margin>Athanasius</margin>
**Saint Athanasius** <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria and a major figure in early Christian theology.</note> in his *Book of Questions, question 13*, answers why God did not permit the soul of any of those who have departed from the living to return to us and narrate the state of things among those in the underworld, and what the conditions are there. He says: "Many errors would arise among us from that. For many demons could take on human forms and be transformed into the shapes of deceased men, and claim that they themselves had risen from the dead; and from there, they could spread many fabricated things and false doctrines about what happens there, doing so to seduce and destroy us." I pray you, weigh these words carefully.

<margin>Chrysostom</margin>

### ON SPECTERS,

...according to *Matthew* <note>The author is referring to Saint John Chrysostom's Homilies on the Gospel of Matthew, specifically discussing the miracle of the Gadarene demoniacs.</note>: He raises that question: why were those possessed by demons dwelling in the tombs? Specifically, it was so they might confirm that false opinion that the souls of those who have died a violent death are transformed into demons and serve sorcerers and soothsayers <note>original: "aruspicibus," ancient Roman practitioners who interpreted the will of the gods from the entrails of animals; here used generally for practitioners of the occult.</note>. The devil introduced this belief in order to undermine the glory of the martyrs, and so that sorcerers might kill people whose assistance—even for evil ends—they might use. He declares these ideas to be far from the truth. For he proves through the *Scriptures* that the spirits of the holy ones are not under the power of the devil, nor do they wander about. Furthermore, if it were permitted for them to wander wherever they wished, they would return to their own bodies. In addition to this, if they obeyed their own killers, those killers would receive a reward for their crime. Also, by the laws of nature <note>original: "Physicis rationibus"</note>, it is impossible for the body of a human to be turned into the body of another; therefore, neither can the spirit of a human be turned into a devil.

Among other things, which pertains specifically to our current investigation, he says: If a cry is heard saying, "*I am the soul of such-a-one*" <note>original: "Anima talis ego sum"</note>, this speech is the result of diabolical fraud and deception. For it is not the soul of the deceased person speaking, but a demon, who fabricates these things to deceive those listening. Soon, [he addresses] these words of old women <note>The author is likely referring to "old wives' tales" or common superstitions held by the uneducated.</note>,

Therefore, the soul cannot wander here among us once it has departed from the body.

He adds that it can be proven from many passages of *Scripture* that the souls of righteous men do not wander here after death. For *Stephen* said, *Receive my spirit* <note>Acts 7:59, the final words of the first Christian martyr.</note>; and *Paul* desired to be released—or to depart from here—and to be with Christ.

For if the souls of men could dwell here, he would have come himself as he desired, and would have made his kin certain regarding the torments of hell. From which passage of *Scripture* this also evidently appears,

## CHAPTER VI.

### *The arguments of those who claim the souls of the dead appear are refuted. First, a response is provided to the claim that God is omnipotent and therefore can act contrary to the usual order of things.*

<margin>Arguments of those who say souls return.</margin>
At the outset, our adversaries <note>The author is likely responding to Roman Catholic or traditionalist views that supported the existence of ghosts as souls from Purgatory.</note> raise an objection against us. They admit that by the "common law" <term>lege communi: the standard natural or divine order</term>, the souls of the faithful remain in heaven and those of the ungodly remain in hell <note>original: "inferno"</note> until the final day, and do not wander about. However, they argue that God can grant a special dispensation so that they might occasionally appear in order to instruct and warn living people. 

They argue that in this way, Samuel appeared to King Saul after his death <note>A reference to 1 Samuel 28, a central text in Renaissance debates over whether the "ghost" was really Samuel or a demonic illusion.</note>; that Moses, who had departed from this life several centuries earlier, and likewise Elijah, who had been carried away in a fiery chariot, appeared to Christ our Savior and his three chosen disciples on Mount Tabor <note>The Transfiguration of Jesus, where he appeared in glory alongside the two prophets.</note>; that Lazarus of Bethany also returned to the lands of the living from his rest; and that many others were raised from the dead by Christ, his Apostles, and the Prophets.

-> # SECOND PART <-

They also bring forward this point: that the *Apostles of Christ* believed a spirit or soul appeared to them, whether it was that of *Christ* (as some of the *Church Fathers* <note>The influential theologians of the early Christian church.</note> maintain) or some other person.

In addition to these, they cite passages from the ancient *Fathers* regarding this matter, the decrees of church councils, and the common rumors concerning those who have returned from the dead. To all these arguments, with *God* as my guide, I shall respond in order and briefly.

<margin>That souls are recalled against the common law by the omnipotence of God so that they may instruct men, etc.</margin>
As to the first objection raised—that all things are possible for *God*—we do not deny this in any way. *God* could indeed lead souls out of heaven or hell <note>original: "inferno"</note> and use their efforts to teach, comfort, warn, or rebuke men. However, because no passage and no example exists in the *Holy Scriptures* showing that any souls returned from the dead to teach or warn others in this way, or that the faithful learned or inquired anything from the souls of the dead, we cannot grant them this argument. One should not conclude just anything based solely on the omnipotence of *God*. In the schools <note>A reference to Scholastic philosophy in universities.</note> they say: *the leap from "it is possible" to "it is actually so" is not a valid conclusion* <note>original: "à poſſe ad eſſe non valere conſequutionem." This is a standard rule of logic stating that just because something can happen doesn't mean it does happen.</note>. For *God* does not act against his own word and against himself. It would have to be proven that he willed that a soul...

<meta>This page continues the systematic rebuttal of the argument that God’s omnipotence implies that the souls of the dead return to earth. # ON SPECTERS #

...souls would return to these lands. In this same way, the holy fathers <note>The influential early theologians of the Christian Church whose writings established orthodoxy.</note> also speak regarding omnipotence.

<margin>How one should speak of the omnipotence of God</margin>

*Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD).</note> says in his work Against Praxeas:* I have clearly perceived that nothing is difficult for God. However, we could imagine anything we like about God as if he had done it, simply because he was able to do it. But just because he *can* do all things, we should not believe he *has* done them; rather, we must inquire whether he actually did them, etc. <note>original: "Non autem quia omnia poteſt facere ideo cre- dendum eſt illum feciſſe, ſed an fecerit inquirendum."</note>

*St. Ambrose <note>The Bishop of Milan (c. 339–397 AD) and a major figure in Latin Christianity.</note> writes to Chromatius, in the 6th book of his letters, epistle 37:* What then is impossible for him? Not that which is difficult for his power, but that which is contrary to his nature. It is impossible, he says, for him to lie. That "impossible" is not a matter of weakness, but of power and majesty: because truth does not admit a lie, nor does the power of God admit the error of inconstancy <note>original: "levitatis errorem" - literally an error of lightness or fickleness.</note>; read what follows.

*St. Jerome <note>A priest and theologian (c. 342–420 AD) best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> in his letter to Eustochium on the guarding of virginity, says:* I shall speak boldly: though God can do all things, he cannot restore a virgin after her fall. <note>Jerome argues that while God can forgive the sin, the physical and historical reality of the act cannot be "undone" even by divine power.</note>

*Augustine <note>Bishop of Hippo (354–430 AD); his "City of God" is one of the most influential works of Western philosophy.</note> in The City of God, book 5, chapter 10:* God is called omnipotent by doing what he wills, not by doing what he does not will. Likewise: The power of God is not diminished when it is said that he cannot die or be deceived.

## PART TWO ##

Soon after, he says: "For this reason he cannot do certain things, because he is omnipotent," etc. <note>The author likely continues a thought from St. Jerome or another Church Father mentioned on the previous page, arguing that God's power is defined by His nature—He cannot lie or act against His own goodness.</note> Theodoretus <note>Theodoret of Cyrrhus (c. 393–457 AD), a prominent theologian and bishop from Syria known for his biblical commentaries.</note> also says that it should not be stated without qualification that all things are possible for God. For whoever says this in an absolute sense implies that all things—both those that are good and those that are contrary to good—are possible at the same time, etc. 

Therefore, the argument they make is weak: "God is able to send the souls of men to warn and teach others; therefore, the spirits who appear seeking help are souls that have returned from heaven or hell." In the meantime, we do not deny the power of God (though some impudently slander us by claiming we do); however, we do not want His power to be used as a "hiding place for errors" <note>original: "errorum esse latibulum" — a metaphor meaning the author refuses to let "God's power" be a catch-all excuse for justifying superstitious beliefs about ghosts.</note>.

One must not learn from the dead. Deuteronomy 18.

Let there not be found among you anyone who makes his son or daughter pass through the fire, nor a diviner who predicts the future by divination, nor an astrologer <term>mathematicus</term> <note>In the medieval and early modern period, a "mathematician" often referred to someone who practiced judicial astrology or numerology to predict the future.</note>, nor a sorcerer, nor a worker of harmful magic, nor a fabricator of charms, nor anyone who consults a spirit of divination <note>original: "Pythonem" — a reference to the Pythoness of Delphi, used here to mean a medium or someone possessed by a familiar spirit.</note>, nor a wizard, nor anyone who raises the dead. For the Lord abominates anyone who does these things, and because of these abominations, the Lord your God will drive them out before you. You shall, therefore, be blameless before the Lord your God.*

Isaiah chapter 8: *“If they say to you, 'Consult the mediums <note>original: "Pythonicis" — a term derived from the Pythian oracle of Delphi, used here for those who claim to channel spirits.</note> and the diviners, who whisper and mutter.' Furthermore, more will follow on this matter in its proper place below.

## CHAPTER VII.

### That the true *Samuel* did not appear to the *Medium* at *Endor*.

<margin>They claim Samuel returned.</margin>
Regarding the examples brought forward by those who believe that the souls of the dead return to the living on earth, I will first address the appearance of *Samuel*, a matter that is still much debated today. I intend to prove with solid arguments, I hope, that the true *Samuel* did not return in either soul or body—neither his body, which perhaps had already turned to dust in the earth, nor his soul—but rather that a *demonic specter* <note>original: "Spectrum diabolicum"</note> was summoned by the *medium* <note>original: "Pythonissa" — a term for a woman possessed by a spirit of divination, named after the Pythian oracle of Delphi.</note>.

First, the author of the Book of *Samuel* states that *Saul* consulted the Lord, but the Lord did not answer him—not through dreams, nor by *Urim* <term>Urim: sacred lots used by high priests in ancient Israel to determine the divine will</term>, nor through the Prophets. If God disdained to answer the man He had rejected through living prophets or other ordinary and established means, it is not difficult to imagine that He would be even less willing to recall a prophet from the dead to answer him. This is especially true since the Law of God <note>original: "Lex Dei"</note>...

<header>ON SPECTERS.</header>

<margin>Deuteronomy 18.</margin>
...sternly prohibited <note>original: "minaciter prohibuerit"</note>, as we said a little earlier, that we should learn from the dead or seek the truth from them. Nor does He want anyone to devote themselves to necromancy <note>original: "Necromantiae" — the practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future.</note> or other diabolical arts of that kind.

Secondly, if the true *Samuel* returned, it must necessarily have happened either by the will of God or by the operation and effect of the magical art. But God did not want him to return: for He does not want the dead to be consulted, and He condemns necromancy by His own word. It should not be granted that the Spirit of God acted contrary to this, or permitted the saints to do so, or assisted those who acted against His command.

Furthermore, we cannot say that these things were done by the force and efficacy of the magical art. For the evil spirit has no command or power over the souls of the faithful, which are in the hand of God and in the **bosom of Abraham** <note>original: "sinu Abrahae" — a traditional biblical expression for the place of rest where the righteous await the afterlife.</note>, such that he could lead them out whenever he wishes. He does not even have command over a foul and stinking pig. That words recited by magicians or others in a peculiar manner, or characters <term>characters: occult symbols or sigils believed to hold power</term>, possess a secret power is a pagan <note>original: "ethnica"</note> superstition. For the pagans believed that with prescribed words they could lead down or draw out *Jupiter* from the sky; hence they also named him *Elicius* <note>original: "Elicium" — derived from the Latin "elicere," meaning to draw out or entice. # ON SPECTERS.
### I 4

Fifthly, if it had been the true Samuel, without doubt he would have exhorted Saul to repentance, commanded him to wait for divine help and to trust in God, or would have at least brought some consolation and given him counsel to fight more courageously against the enemies of God's people. For although Prophets rebuke men, they nevertheless console and lift them up again. But because *this* Samuel instills in Saul a hatred of God, saying that God is hostile to him and has deserted him, it must not be believed that he was the true Samuel, but an impersonated <note>original: "personatum" — literally "masked," suggesting a theatrical role or a disguise</note> Samuel.

<margin>Testimony of the Fathers</margin>
Sixthly, the ancient Fathers of the Church also write that the true Samuel was not seen.

<margin>Tertullian</margin>
Tertullian, in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "De anima"</note>, says that the devil mimicked the soul of Samuel. "Far be it," he says, "that we should believe the soul of any saint, much less a Prophet, could be extracted by a demon; for we are taught that Satan himself transforms into an angel of light, much less a man of light <note>The author contrasts the biblical "angel of light" with a "man of light" to show that if a demon can fake a celestial being, it can easily fake a human soul</note>, and will even assert himself to be God, and produce more portentous signs to overthrow, if possible, the elect," etc.

<margin>Augustine</margin>
St. <note>original: "D." for Divus, a title often used for "Saint" in early modern Latin</note> Augustine writes in a similar way concerning this apparition. In the second book of his work addressed to Simplician, Bishop of Milan...

## PART TWO

In the third question to Januarius <note>original: "Ian. quæstio. 3." — This likely refers to St. Augustine’s *To Simplician* (Ad Simplicianum), as he addresses these questions there; "Ian." may be a printer's error or shorthand for a specific edition or recipient</note>, he concedes it could have happened that the spirit of the holy Prophet, through some dispensation of the divine will—not against his will, nor dominated or subjugated by magical power, but willing and obeying the hidden dispensation of God—consented to show himself to the King’s sight, returned from his resting place, and spoke. However, he does not hide that the readier solution is for us to say that the spirit of Samuel was not truly stirred from his rest, but was some phantom and imaginary illusion created by the machinations of the devil. He explains that *Scripture* calls this figure by the name "**Samuel**" because it is customary to call images by the names of the things they represent. For who is there, he asks, who would hesitate to call a painted man "a man"? Indeed, when we look at individual paintings, we apply their proper names without hesitation. For example, when looking at a wooden panel or a wall, we say, "That is **Cicero**," "that is **Sallust**," "that is **Achilles**," "that is **Hector**," "this is the river **Simois** <note>A river near Troy, famous in classical literature</note>," or "that is **Rome**," even though they are nothing but painted images. Since this is the case, he says, it is no wonder that *scripture* says **Samuel** was seen, even if perhaps an image of Samuel [was what appeared].

<summary>This page presents St. Augustine's argument that the figure appearing to Saul was likely a demonic illusion rather than the actual soul of Samuel, using the analogy of how we name people in paintings to explain why the Bible uses Samuel's name.</summary>

...was seen to have appeared, by the machination of him who transforms himself into an angel of light, and his ministers as ministers of justice, and so forth. <note>original: "transfigurat se in angelum lucis" — A reference to 2 Corinthians 11:14-15, where the Apostle Paul warns that Satan disguises himself as a holy messenger to deceive the faithful.</note>

*In his book **On the Eight Questions of Dulcitius** <note>original: "De octo Dulcitii quæstionibus" — A work by St. Augustine written around 422 AD to answer theological queries from a friend</note>, in the sixth question, he repeats all these things in exactly the same words. In his book **On Care to be Taken for the Dead** <note>original: "De cura pro mortuis gerenda"</note>, he writes that some of the deceased are sent to the living, just as, conversely, **Paul** was caught up from the living into paradise. He adds the example of the deceased **Samuel**, who predicted the future to **Saul**. He further adds that this passage can be understood in another way, and that some of the faithful have felt it was not Samuel himself, but some spirit suited to such evil works who fashioned himself into the likeness of the prophet. In other places, as we shall see later, he asserts that there is a trope <note><term>trope</term>: A figure of speech where a word or name is used in a non-literal sense, such as calling a statue by the name of the person it depicts.</note> in these words: for the name of the person represented is attributed to the image. Thus, it was not Samuel, but a **diabolical specter**, etc.*

Other Church Fathers write nothing, as far as I know, specifically about this story, but they teach in general in various places that good spirits are not called back to the earth by magical arts. I will speak of **Justin** <note>Justin Martyr, an early Christian apologist</note> and **Gregory** <note>Likely Gregory the Great, whose "Dialogues" were the primary source for medieval ghost stories</note> shortly.

§. c. And it is no wonder, for it is written that it was not Samuel but an evil demon <note>original: "cacodæmonem" — A Greek-derived term for a malevolent spirit or "bad demon."</note> who appeared. ## CHAPTER VIII.

->*The arguments of those who maintain that the true Samuel appeared are refuted.*<-

Now hereafter we shall also refute the arguments of those who believe that the true *Samuel* appeared to the <term>Pythoness</term> <note>original: "Pythonissæ" — Derived from the Pythia of Delphi, this term was commonly used in Latin Bibles to describe the medium or witch of Endor.</note>. For he who correctly refutes the arguments of his adversaries is believed to confirm his own cause by one and the same effort.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 46.</margin>
The chief argument used by those who oppose our opinion is drawn from the 46th chapter of <term>Ecclesiasticus</term> <note>A book of the Bible (also called Sirach) which, in the Latin Vulgate tradition, contains a passage explicitly stating that Samuel prophesied after his death.</note>, where you will find these words: > *Samuel, before his death, bore witness before God and his Anointed <note>original: "Uncto" — referring to King Saul, the "Lord's Anointed."</note> that he had accepted neither money from any mortal, nor so much as a pair of shoes; nor indeed was there anyone who accused him. Even after his death, he prophesied and showed the king his end. He lifted up his voice from the earth, prophesying that the sins of the people should be blotted out.*

This testimony caused no small amount of difficulty <note>original: "nonnihil negoti fecit" — literally "made not a little business," meaning it was a significant theological challenge.</note> for Saint Augustine and other Holy Fathers. For if the devil, and not *Samuel*,

At this point, I could respond that this book is not counted among the canonical books of the *Old Testament*, and that controversial dogmas are not to be proven by its authority (a fact which even *St. Augustine* admits in his book *On the Care to be Taken for the Dead* <note>original: "D. Next, they <note>Referring to the author's theological opponents.</note> say that he who appeared to *Saul* is several times clearly and expressly called *Samuel*. They argue that it would be unworthy and would constitute an insult to such a great prophet if his name were attributed to the devil. If it had not been *Samuel*, they say, but an evil spirit, *Scripture* would have indicated it by at least a single word.

To this argument, I first respond: It is common even in our daily speech to use a <margin>Metonymy</margin> <term>metonymy</term> <note>A figure of speech where a thing is called by the name of something related to it; here, calling a spirit by the name of the person it represents.</note> where we call an image by the name...

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 188 regarding the use of "metonymy"—a figure of speech where a thing is called by the name of something else it is associated with. The author is explaining why the Bible might call a demonic apparition "Samuel" without it actually being the soul of the dead prophet.</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>->ON SPECTERS<-</header>

than of the person whom it represents. Thus, we call weapons or insignias by the name of the Lord to whom they belong. You say: "This is *Julius Caesar*, *Nero*, or *Saint Peter*, *Saint Paul*"; or, "Here you see *Zurich* <note>original: "Tigurum"</note>, *Strasbourg* <note>original: "Argentoratum"</note>, the *Duke of Zähringen*," even though these are only their images or their arms and insignias. In a comedy or tragedy, you name this man *Saul*, that one *Samuel*, and a third *David*, because they take on the persona of those people or represent them. *Virgil*, in the first book of the *Aeneid*, says:

> *They marvel at the gifts of Aeneas; they marvel at Iulus.* <note>original: "Mirantur dona Æneæ, mirantur Iulum"</note>

Yet it was not actually *Iulus* or *Ascanius* <note>Iulus and Ascanius are two names for the son of Aeneas in Roman mythology.</note>, but *Cupid*, who was pretending to be *Iulus*, or who took on his form—his gait, face, gestures, and voice—so that he might more easily breathe the poison of love into the unsuspecting and incautious Queen <note>Referring to Queen Dido of Carthage.</note>.

*Saint Augustine*, in the second book of *On the Wonders of Scripture* <note>original: "de Mirabilibus Scripturæ" — a work historically attributed to Augustine, though now often attributed to an anonymous 7th-century Irish author known as Pseudo-Augustine.</note>, chapter 9, says that divine Scripture sometimes assigns the names of real things to mere likenesses of those things. He brings forward this very example: that the <term>unclean spirit</term> <note>A common biblical term for a demon.</note> is called *Samuel* because he falsely claimed to be him. This is no deception on *Samuel’s* part, nor does it result in his disgrace; for who would say it is shameful for an honest man if some

...if a scoundrel calls himself by another's name and pretends to be that person? The Devil himself lies by claiming to be God. False prophets have claimed that they were true seers and servants of God—indeed, even the Son of God, the Messiah, and so on. Truly, the fact that Scripture does not say so much as a single word <note>original: "ne verbulo quidem"</note> about it not being the real *Samuel* but a *Specter* <note>original: "Spectrum"</note> should be understood as follows: everyone ought to have known from the Law that magic and the summoning of the dead are displeasing to God. *Saul* himself—certainly with *Samuel* as his advisor and driver—had previously removed from their midst as many magicians as he could find. *God* is not accustomed to explaining figurative <term>expressions</term> <note>original: "tropicas loquutiones" — phrases using "tropes" or figures of speech where words are used in a non-literal way.</note> in this manner, for most of them are easily understood by those who pay attention. It would be superfluous if someone were to say, "This is Peter," [and then add] "This is an image of Peter," which we commonly call by the name of *Peter* through a <term>trope</term> <note>A literary device where a word is used in a sense other than its literal one; here, specifically metonymy (naming the image after the subject).</note>.

Furthermore, the *Holy Scriptures* speak of things more according to the opinion and judgment of men than according to the actual existence of the thing. Thus, *Jesus* is called the son of *Joseph*, and Joseph is called his father, even though our Savior was born of a chaste virgin without the work of a man; nevertheless, many of the *Jews* were of the opinion that he was the son of *Joseph*. In the first chapter of the first [letter] to the Corinthians, the Gospe- <note>The text breaks here; the original Latin "Euange-" is completed by the catchword "lium" on the following page to form "Euangelium" (Gospel).</note>

...[the Gos]pel <note>original: "lium" — likely the conclusion of *Evangelium* from the previous page.</note> is called foolishness, because men held the highest wisdom in the place of foolishness. *Scripture calls those "gods" who were anything but [gods]* (1 Corinthians 10), for the reason that the Gentiles believed they were gods and worshipped them as gods. In the same way, *Scripture calls the devil "Samuel,"* because *Saul* thought that he was *Samuel*.

Furthermore, they argue that he predicted future events to *Saul*: *that the army would be defeated in battle by the Philistines* <note>original: "Palæstinis" — referring to the Philistines of the Old Testament.</note>, *and that he himself, along with his sons, would be killed.* All these things truly came to pass. However, the devil can neither know nor predict the future, which is placed in the hand of **God** alone; rather, as *Christ says in John [8:44]*... he is *a liar and the father of lies.* To this, it is not difficult to respond. The devil knew how affairs were arranged among the *Jews* and the *Philistines*: he knew the secret plans and the military preparations of both sides. He perceived that the *Israelites* were poorly equipped and utterly dismayed. # PART TWO

*Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose views on ghosts and demons were central to Western thought.</note> in several places calls it into doubt whether it was the true *Samuel* who appeared; however, in other places he approves the opinion of those who deny it was *Samuel*, affirming instead that it is a figurative way of speaking <note>original: "tropicam loquutionem" — a rhetorical term suggesting the biblical text uses a figure of speech rather than a literal description of the prophet’s return.</note>.

<margin>Justin</margin>
*Justin Martyr*, who is one of the most ancient of the Fathers, writes in his *Dialogue with Trypho the Jew* that the soul of *Samuel* was called up at the command of *Saul* by a medium <note>original: "ventriloqua" — literally "one who speaks from the belly." In the early modern period, this was the standard term for the Witch of Endor, based on the idea that a spirit spoke through her body.</note>. Truly, no one should wonder at this, for the same author immediately adds: that it seems to him that all the souls of the Prophets and the righteous were subject to such powers, as is believed to have been the case with the medium. No one among the other Church Fathers will grant him this point. Others among the Greeks, who from their youth devoted their labor to philosophy rather than to the *Sacred Scriptures* and were only later converted to the Christian faith, held certain opinions of their own which do not agree with the *Word of God*. Therefore, it should not seem strange to anyone that *Justin* also was mistaken <note>original: "hallucinatum esse" — the author uses a strong word here, implying Justin was "wandering in his mind" or deluded on this specific theological point.</note> in certain matters.
