PART TWO

<margin>According to the words of *St. Augustine*, Question 9, chapter "Do Not" <note>original: "Noli"</note>.</margin> No one among the *Fathers* <note>The "Church Fathers" were the influential Christian theologians and scholars of the first several centuries of the Church.</note> is to be believed unless he has confirmed his claims from the *Sacred Scriptures*. Many people nowadays pluck only errors from their writings and reject those things which they prove through the testimony of the *Sacred Scriptures*. They are no different than those sons who imitate their good parents only in their bad deeds; for even good men have their own blemishes <note>original: "naevos" — literally "moles" or "birthmarks," used here to mean minor flaws or errors.</note>.

## CHAPTER IX.
### *Whether the devil can appear to anyone in the likeness of a faithful man.*

You ask whether the devil can represent the appearance of some holy man who has departed from the living. There is no doubt about this. For *St. Paul, in 2 Corinthians chapter 11*, testifies that *Satan transforms himself into an angel of light.* Satan, by his very nature, exists as a spirit. He is called an "angel" <note>The word "angel" (from the Greek 'angelos') literally means "messenger."</note> because God sends him to carry out a task. An evil angel is sent out in the *first book of Kings* to be a lying spirit in the mouth of the *False Prophets of Ahab* <note>Referencing 1 Kings 22, where a spirit is permitted by God to deceive King Ahab through his prophets to lead him to his downfall.</note>, for the...

### *Justin Martyr, Second Apology for the Christians* <note>Justin Martyr (c. 100 – 165 AD) was an early Christian apologist who sought to defend Christian philosophy against Roman and Jewish critics.</note>

*I shall speak the truth*, he says. Long ago, evil spirits deceived women and children through *specters*, and terrified men with omens, so that they might wring from the simple-minded through fear what they could not achieve through reason. These people, unaware that these illusions were the tricks of evil demons, called them gods in a general sense, assigning distinct names to each as it pleased them, etc. Later in the same apology, he urges the *Pagans* <note>original: "Ethnicos"</note> not to deny that human souls possess sensation after death; at the very least, they should place their faith in their own necromancers, who say they call forth souls. Likewise, they should believe those who claim to be troubled by the spirits of the dead, whom the common people call the "possessed" <note>original: "arreptitios," referring to those seized or snatched by a spirit.</note> and the "frenzied."

*Plutarch writes in the "Life of Cimon" (as translated by Joachim Camerarius <note>Joachim Camerarius (1500–1574) was a prominent German classical scholar who translated various Greek works into Latin.</note> in the preface to Plutarch’s booklets "On the Oracles Which Have Ceased" and "On the Figure ‘E’ Dedicated at Delphi") that after Pausanias had captured Byzantium <note>Pausanias was a Spartan general during the Greco-Persian Wars; Byzantium is modern-day Istanbul.</note>, he summoned a young woman of noble birth named Cleonice for a shameful purpose. ## SECOND PART

...she accidentally knocked over the bronze lampstand. Startled from his sleep by the noise and fearing that an assassin had broken in to kill him, Pausanias seized his dagger and struck the figure in the darkness. It was only when a light was brought that he realized he had mortally wounded the girl Cleonice.

After this tragedy, Pausanias found no peace. Her ghost <gloss>the shadow or spirit of the deceased</gloss> haunted his dreams every night, appearing to him and reciting a grim verse: "Approach your justice; the price of wicked deeds is a heavy fate." Driven nearly mad by the haunting, he traveled to the Oracle of the Dead <term>Psychomanteion: A sacred site, often near a cave or spring, believed to be an entrance to the underworld where the spirits of the dead could be summoned.</term> at Heraclea. There, through secret sacrifices and prayers, he called forth the soul of Cleonice. She appeared to him and told him that he would soon be delivered from his troubles upon his return to Sparta—a veiled prophecy of his own coming death.

The background provides a glimpse into the life of a commander, showing the faint outlines of military tents and a rugged, hilly terrain. Furthermore, regarding the primary question: suppose (for this cannot be proven from the *Scriptures*) that *God himself* permitted *Samuel* <note>Samuel was a biblical judge and prophet. In the early times, God often sent his angels to men in visible form, but now you do not hear of many being sent to people; nor is such a thing necessary. In the times of the *Apostles*, astounding miracles were performed, but later, for specific reasons, they ceased. For whatever is necessary for our salvation is contained within the *Word of God*. Let it suffice to have noted these points concerning Samuel.

## CHAPTER X.

**Moses and Elijah appeared to Christ our Lord on the mountain: many were resurrected in soul and body: therefore souls can also return to these lands.**

<margin>III. Moses and Elijah appeared to Christ.</margin>
It is likewise objected against us that Moses and Elijah <note>Moses and Elijah represent the Law and the Prophets of the Old Testament. Luke explains what matters they discussed with him—namely, his departure, which is to say, his suffering on the cross. Therefore, they <note>That is, the opponents who believe in ghosts.</note> conclude that the souls of the dead are able to return to the earth and present themselves to be seen by men. We have already granted previously that God *is able* to send souls to the earth; however, whether this is his *will*, and especially whether it is necessary in these present times, is something that requires proof. Moses and Elijah appeared only to three, and not to all of the Apostles; they did not converse with those three Apostles; they did not bring new <term>dogmata: established religious doctrines or teachings</term>, nor did they command them to...

It was of the greatest importance that those who were to be witnesses of *Christ* should understand perfectly that the Law and the Prophets bear witness to *Christ* our Savior, showing that he was to die for the world and return at the end of the ages, that he would raise the bodies of the dead, glorify them, and lead them with himself into eternal glory. For this reason, therefore, *God* willed that those two excellent prophets <note>Moses, representing the Law, and Elijah, representing the Prophets.</note> should also be seen by them.

This is, as it were, a <term>pledge: a "pignus" or legal guarantee</term> of our own true resurrection which will occur on the final day. Just like the others whom *our savior*, the *Apostles*, and the *Prophets* of ancient times raised from the dead. Nowhere do you read that *Lazarus* or the others said where they had been in the meantime, or what the state is in the next world. For these things are to be learned and held from the Word of God, not from the dead.

On the day of Christ’s resurrection, many rose and appeared to many people.

The same can be said concerning the account found in Matthew 27: that as Christ the Savior was dying on the cross, the tombs were opened, and later, on the day of the resurrection, the dead rose with the Lord and appeared to many in Jerusalem. It was not only the souls of the dead that were seen, warning the living and commanding them to do certain things for the benefit of the deceased—namely, that they should pray for them, or undertake pilgrimages to the saints <note>original: "ad divos"; this refers to the practice of visiting shrines or holy sites dedicated to saints.</note>, and so forth—but they returned to the earth in both soul and body. For God wished to testify that, through His own death, He had conquered and disarmed death for His faithful, and that on the final day their bodies and souls would be reunited, and they would live with God forever. As to who those holy people were who rose, and whether they remained for a while in this present life and died a second time, or if they ascended into the heavens with Christ, consult *Augustine’s 99th letter to Evodius* and *Book 3 of the Miracles of Scripture, chapter 1*.

She, being more cautious than was necessary, hid the deposited item under the earth and, shortly thereafter, ended her life. I will not deny that such things occur. Since the case could be neither proved nor disproved by standard evidence, both men were ordered to go to the tomb of *Felix the martyr*, so that the judgment might be decided by a miracle. They would not have been sent there unless other hidden matters <note>original: "arcana aliqua"</note> had been uncovered by this method in the past. It could be argued that it was good angels, or perhaps even evil ones, who appeared in such instances.

### CHAPTER XI.

*Whether the holy Apostles believed they were seeing a soul, when Christ appeared to them unexpectedly after his resurrection.*

<margin>VI. Christ's disciples think they see a spirit.</margin>
In the Gospel of Luke, chapter 24, we read that the two disciples who had returned from Emmaus <note>original: "Emaunta"</note> to Jerusalem recounted to the Apostles that they had seen Christ restored to life. While they were speaking, the Lord stood in their midst and said to them: "Peace be with you." But they, terrified and struck with fear, supposed they were seeing a spirit <note>original: "spiritum"</note>, and so forth. From these events, some people attempt to prove that the Apostles believed that spirits or souls wander about and present themselves to be seen by human beings. For they thought that they saw the spirit of Christ—as some of the ancient authors interpret it—or perhaps the spirit of someone else.

One can respond to this argument in two ways. First, if they truly believed they were seeing a soul, they were not thinking correctly; rather, they erred alongside the common people <note>original: "cum vulgo"</note> no less than when they believed Christ was going to establish an external and earthly kingdom,

in which they themselves were to be leaders. Furthermore, it is possible that they thought they were seeing either a malevolent or a benevolent angel.

<margin>Various kinds of spirits.</margin>
For there is not just one kind of spirit. There is the Spirit who created all things: namely God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. Then there are the created spirits, namely good and evil angels; likewise, the souls of human beings, which are either within the human body or, having been separated from the body by death, dwell in eternal life or eternal damnation. For regarding the state of souls in *purgatory* <note>original: "purgatorio"; a place of cleansing for souls before entering heaven</note>, in which they are prepared for the heavenly journey, or regarding the *limbo of infants* <note>original: "limbo puerorum"; a place for the souls of those who died without baptism</note>, nothing exists in the *Sacred Scriptures* <note>original: "Sacris literis"</note>.

*It is clear from the Sacred Scriptures that God appeared to the holy Patriarchs, Prophets, Kings, and others in various visions and forms, and that He revealed Himself to them and spoke to them. Jacob saw a ladder placed upon the earth reaching to heaven, upon which God leaned. Isaiah saw the Lord sitting upon a high throne. Daniel saw an ancient man sitting, and the Son coming to Him and receiving all power.* <note>These references describe famous biblical "theophanies" or appearances of God to figures like Jacob (Genesis 28), Isaiah (Isaiah 6), and Daniel (Daniel 7).</note>

**That He appeared to the Patriarchs in angelic form.**

*The Holy Spirit* was seen in the *form of a dove* when *John the Baptist* baptized our Savior in the *Jordan*. The *Sacred Scriptures* testify throughout that good *angels* <note>original: "angelos"; messengers of God</note> frequently appeared to the ministers of God.

History and daily experience testify that evil spirits are often seen, and even to this day they present themselves to enchanters and sorcerers in various forms; likewise to other men, both pious and impious.

But concerning souls, it is not read that they appeared in this manner. <note>The author makes a theological distinction here: while angels and demons (spirits) frequently appear in the Bible, he claims there is no scriptural precedent for the "souls" (ghosts) of the dead appearing in the same way.</note> From these facts it can easily be gathered that when the *Apostles* thought they saw a *spirit* <note>original: "spiritum"</note>, they did not believe they saw a *soul* <note>original: "animam"; the specific life-force or ghost of a deceased human</note>. Could they not have thought they were seeing an *evil spirit*? Or rather that they were seeing a *good spirit or angel*? For it can be shown by many examples that even the faithful were troubled and terrified at the appearance of good angels.

*In Daniel chapters 8 and 10, it is read that the Prophet fell ill at the sight of Angels. The Virgin Mary herself* was terrified when she saw the archangel *Gabriel*, as was *Zechariah the priest*, and many others. <note>These references (Daniel 8:27, Luke 1:12, Luke 1:29) illustrate the "fear of the Lord" or the overwhelming nature of divine encounters, suggesting that the Apostles' fear did not necessarily mean they thought they were seeing a "ghost" in the modern sense.</note>

### ON GHOSTS ###
K 7
<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

<margin>Good and evil spirits</margin>
The **Gentiles** <note>original: "Gentiles"; a term used here to refer to the ancient Greeks and Romans</note> also believed, as is understood from their own writings, that every single human being has both a good and an evil spirit <note>original: "genium"</note>. The good spirit incites men toward virtues and defends them, while the evil one truly seeks to harm men wherever it can and urges them toward wickedness.

If our ancestors said of someone who was traveling abroad, or who had departed from this life, when they heard or saw something: "It is his Spirit"; it is possible that they did not mean his soul <note>original: "animam"</note>, but rather his angel. But if they did believe they were souls—as was believed by everyone in later times—when spirits presented themselves to be seen now in this place, then in another, they were in error, just as they were in many other matters. For souls are immediately gathered into either eternal joys or eternal damnation <note>The author is arguing for the Protestant view that souls go directly to Heaven or Hell, rather than lingering on Earth or in Purgatory</note>.

If the teachers <note>likely referring to the clergy or "Doctors of the Church"</note> had performed their duty and had rightly instructed the common people committed to their care concerning this and other points of Christian doctrine, or at least had not forbidden them from reading the sacred books <note>A common Reformation-era critique of the Catholic Church’s restriction on laypeople reading the Bible in their own language</note>, they would have held the correct view on this matter and others which are disputed today.

<note>Vocabulary: Gentiles (Heathen/Pagans), spirits (genii), Spirit (Spiritus), soul (anima), Christian doctrine (doctrina Christiana), eternal joys (aeterna gaudia), eternal damnation (aeterna damnatio)</note>

<summary>The author compares the classical belief in personal "genii" to Christian concepts of angels and souls, arguing that sightings of the deceased are often misinterpreted angels, as human souls depart immediately to their final destination.</summary>
<keywords>guardian angels, genii, Greco-Roman belief, state of the soul, afterlife, religious education, Reformation polemic</keywords>

# PART TWO

## CHAPTER XII.

### *Concerning the holy Fathers, Councils, Popes, and the common people, who say that souls appear.*

<margin>The Holy Fathers say that souls appear.</margin>
The authority of the holy Fathers <term>Patres; refers to the "Church Fathers," the influential Christian theologians and bishops of the first few centuries</term> is raised as an objection against us. They point specifically to what **Saint Ambrose** writes concerning **Saint Agnes**, and **Saint Augustine** concerning **Saint Felix**, both of whom we mentioned earlier. **Abdias** <note>Referring to the *History of the Apostolic Contest*, a collection of legends attributed to a legendary Bishop of Babylon named Abdias</note>, in his *Life of the Apostles*, writes that **Thomas** appeared and preached after his death. **Saint Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), whose *Dialogues* are famous for their many accounts of miracles and visions</note>, in his *Dialogues*, writes various and marvelous things; among many other examples, he recounts stories of the dead who appeared and asked for help, and of certain saints—even the **Apostles** themselves—who visited those lying on their deathbeds shortly before they passed away. There are other stories of this kind which those who wish to do so may read for themselves. It is even said that **Jerome** appeared to **Saint Augustine**.

At this point, I do not wish to accuse the holy Fathers of falsehood <note>original: "vanitatis"; literally 'vanity' or 'emptiness,' here implying they were being deceptive or unreliable</note>. However, it should be understood that they do not necessarily mean they believed those who appeared were truly the souls of the dead; rather, they are speaking in the common manner <note>original: "vulgari more"; the author suggests the Fathers were using popular, non-technical language rather than making a strict theological statement about the nature of the soul</note>.

<meta>catchword: Which</meta>

<summary>The author addresses historical accounts from the Church Fathers, such as Augustine and Gregory the Great, which seem to support the reality of ghosts. He argues that these respected leaders were merely using popular figures of speech rather than confirming that souls actually leave the afterlife.</summary>

<keywords>Church Fathers, Saint Augustine, Gregory the Great, Saint Ambrose, apparitions, souls, Thomas the Apostle, Dialogues, theology, deathbeds</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of historical authorities who claimed that souls return to earth. Having previously listed the names of various Church Fathers, the author now focuses specifically on the reliability of Pope Gregory the Great’s famous accounts of miracles and visions.</meta>

->ON SPECTERS<-

<margin>The Dialogues of Gregory the Great</margin>

As for what concerns the *Dialogues* of **Saint Gregory**, I cannot hide—something many others have observed before me—that they contain many things that are not at all plausible, but are more like old wives' tales <note>original: "fabulis anilibus"; a common term used by early modern scholars to describe superstitious or unrefined folklore</note>. It is not that the holy Father wrote those things out of malice, but because he was overly credulous <note>original: "nimis fuit credulus"</note> and included many things in his books based on the reports of others rather than because he possessed certain and verified knowledge of them.

Even today, there are many pious and honorable men who struggle with this fault: they believe too quickly and allow themselves to be led by others. They judge others by their own standards; it would shame them to state anything false, and so they believe others are similarly inclined—others who then take advantage of their simplicity and goodness. Because of this credulity, they sometimes run into great dangers.

Furthermore, in the time of **Gregory**, much importance began to be placed on such apparitions and visions. In those very times, true and simple doctrine had already begun to be seriously undermined. It matters a great deal what era a person happens to live in. He himself admitted the state of his own times. Therefore, one should have adhered more closely to the *sacred scriptures* <note>original: "sacris literis"</note>, and nothing should have been accepted that is not in agreement with them.

Some people, making excuses for him, say that...

<sig>K 7</sig>
<meta>catchword: and therefore [ideq]</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the reliability of Pope Gregory the Great's accounts of spirits, suggesting they are based on hearsay rather than verified truth. He argues that even virtuous men can be misled by their own goodness and that the theological corruption of Gregory’s era led to an over-reliance on visions instead of Scripture.</summary>
<keywords>Gregory the Great, Dialogues, credulity, visions, sacred scriptures, church history, superstition, doctrine</keywords>

### PART TWO

...that he filled his dialogues with miracles and wonders so that the stubborn and hard hearts of the **Lombards** <note>original: "Lomgobardorum"; a Germanic people who ruled Italy and were often seen as "barbarians" or heretics by the Roman Church during Gregory's time</note> would be softened by those examples and embrace the true religion which they had so greatly attacked. <margin>Councils approve of the appearances of souls.</margin>
Some press us with the authority of the Church Councils, which have approved certain appearances of souls and the books written about them; they have permitted some to be read for the edification of the simple-minded, while completely rejecting other books and apparitions.

It is said of the **Council of Constance** <note>original: "concilio Constantiensi"; an influential 15th-century council (1414–1418) that addressed the Western Schism and church reform</note> that it approved this apparition: A certain Dean, after he had resigned from his position, went into the wilderness to perform penance. After his death, he appeared to his Bishop and indicated to him that in the very hour he died, thirty thousand men departed from the living. Among all those people, only his soul and that of **Saint Bernard** <note>likely St. Bernard of Clairvaux, a highly influential 12th-century mystic and monk</note> became partakers of eternal salvation; three descended into purgatory, and the rest into eternal damnation, etc.

...[they claim that the Councils] cannot err, because they are guided by the Holy Spirit. They also point out that the Lord himself says in **Matthew 24**: *“In the last times, signs and wonders shall occur, so that if it were possible, even the elect would be led astray.”* <note>original: "Noviſſimis temporibus fient ſigna & prodigia, ut ſi fieri poſſet, etiam electi ſeducerentur"; quoting Matthew 24:24</note> Therefore, they conclude that these things which are said concerning those apparitions must be believed. Tell me, I ask, who gathered in the ancient councils? Was it not the **Holy Fathers**? Yet it is well known that they disagreed among themselves on many points, and showed this through their conflicting writings. Indeed, they sometimes even contradict themselves, and therefore they did not always think correctly. They themselves frequently refer us back to the **Word of God** as the most certain norm and rule of faith. 

Examples are not lacking which can show that the ancient Councils erred in certain dogmas. The **Council of Ariminum** <note>original: "Ariminenſe concilium"; held in 359 AD, this council is famous for temporarily adopting an Arian creed, which denied that Christ was of the same substance as God the Father</note> approved the **Arian** <note>original: "Arrianorum"; a theological movement that held that Jesus was a created being rather than co-eternal with God</note> dogma. The **Second Council of Ephesus** <note>held in 449 AD, later condemned as the "Robber Council" for its support of the Monophysite heresy</note> subscribed to the views of **Eutyches** <note>a monk who taught that Christ had only one divine nature, rather than being both fully human and fully divine</note>. In the **Council of Carthage**, which **Cyprian** <note>St. Cyprian (c. 210–258 AD), Bishop of Carthage, who famously disagreed with the Pope over the rebaptism of heretics</note> assembled, a pronouncement was even made against the Scriptures.

### SECOND PART

...was, etc. What shall we say happened in subsequent times? It is well known from history who summoned and presided over the councils during several centuries, and what matters were primarily discussed; and what usually followed soon after: namely, <term>deadly wars and slaughter</term> <note>original: "feralia bella & cædes"; the author is likely referring to the civil and international conflicts triggered by the decrees of religious councils, such as the crusades against heretics</note>. If those ancient councils could err, to whom will it seem strange that those held in the intervening time have also erred? But as for those apparitions—omitting all other things to deal only with these—tell me, I pray: who at the councils could say whether this or that apparition was true or a fabrication? Certainly, no council can make it so that the lies being spread begin to be true, even if they themselves affirm them to be true.

<margin>The Popes approve the apparitions of souls.</margin>
It is no less foolish to say that the **Pope** (who wishes to be held superior to all councils) has confirmed this or that miracle which they say occurred in some **monastery** <note>original: "cœnobio"; a communal dwelling for monks</note>. ...wants to establish new pilgrimages and <term>schemes to catch money</term> <note>original: "pecuniæ aucupia"; literally "bird-snaring for money," a metaphor for deceptive ways to profit from the faithful</note> through rumors or letters, it is immediately believed without contradiction. <margin>Many say that souls have appeared to them.</margin>
As for other people—the <term>laity</term> <note>laypeople, or those who are not members of the clergy</note> as they call them, and the common folk—who say that they have seen someone after their death, heard them, recognized them, or spoken with them: I readily grant that they did indeed see and hear something, and were entirely certain that these were souls and that they spoke with them. However, it does not follow that they were actually souls; much less does it follow that a <term>deceased</term> <note>original: "defunctus"</note> person appeared to them in both body and soul. For only on the final day of judgment will souls return to their bodies. Souls are spirits; and spirits are invisible unless they take on some outward forms, which is why they cannot be seen in their natural state. For even if souls, like good and bad angels, [clothe] themselves in certain forms...

## ON SPECTERS.
<meta>catchword: to complain (queri)</meta>

That souls do not return or appear before the final day <note>Referring to the Day of Judgment at the end of the world.</note>, some might perhaps not unwillingly concede; but they <note>Referring to the Catholic theologians whose views the author is refuting.</note> invent a third place, namely *purgatory*, from which souls return to the lands of the living. For they argue that the final sentence has not yet been passed against those souls; therefore, they can still be relieved by assistance <note>Such as prayers, fasts, or financial offerings made by the living on behalf of the dead.</note>, and they even seek such help, and for this reason they present themselves to be seen by men. 

But previously, from *divine oracles* <note>original: "divinis oraculis"; here meaning the Holy Scriptures.</note> and the *writings of the Church Fathers*, we have abundantly and clearly proven that the souls of the faithful are saved and those of the unfaithful are damned without any intervening delay; and therefore, there is no purgatory. 

Against these points, they oppose various arguments, among which one is common indeed, but seems to be their primary point: they say that no one is saved who has not been purified of all their sins, yet sin clings to us even until the grave. 

If we say that purity is not located in our own works or in the sufferings that we endure, but rather that God makes us righteous through faith in *His Son Christ Jesus, who is our only redemption, righteousness, satisfaction <term>Satisfaction</term> <note>In a legal or theological sense, this refers to the payment of a debt or the fulfilling of a penalty due for sin.</note>, and the price paid for our sins*, they answer that *faith in us is imperfect*, and that even the best of people, when they must depart from this life, complain *about the weakness of their faith*...

<summary>This page refutes the doctrine of purgatory, arguing that souls go directly to heaven or hell upon death rather than returning as ghosts to seek aid. The author counters the idea that human works or suffering are needed for purification, asserting instead that righteousness comes solely through faith in Jesus Christ.</summary>

### PART TWO

...to complain <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of why some might argue for an intermediate state.</note>. Therefore, they claim that God does not lead such men directly into heaven; and because they are not entirely devoid of faith, He does not immediately thrust them down to the underworld <note>original: "inferos"; referring here to Hell.</note>. They argue, then, that there is an intermediate place, which is called **Purgatory Fire** <note>original: "Ignis purgatorius"</note>, in which souls are purified from the imperfection that remained at death, and from which they are liberated through the merits of the living and through generous **indulgences** <term>Indulgences</term> <note>In the Roman Catholic tradition of the time, these were grants by the Church to reduce the temporal punishment required for sins already forgiven.</note>. 

But is this not to attribute to our own pains and to an external fire that which ought to be attributed solely to the suffering of Christ? Does not Christ teach us that if we feel a weakness of faith within ourselves, we should cry out with the *Apostles*, ***Lord, increase our faith***? <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 17:5.</note> Does God disdain to hear the prayers of His faithful in the agony of death? Christ says, ***He who is bathed has no need except to wash his feet, but is clean all over*** <note>original: "Qui lotus est, non opus habet nisi ut pedes lavet, sed est mundus totus"; a reference to the Gospel of John 13:10.</note>: ***Not because of the dignity of our faith, but out of pure grace He wills to save us.*** 

This grace is indeed communicated to us through faith. It is as if some wealthy person were to distribute food and drink to others for free, and some received it in a wooden vessel, some in earthenware, some in silver, or even in gold; or, if some Prince were to distribute a gold coin to every individual, and some received it with a weak hand, while others <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

What else follows from this, then, but that purgatory either does not exist, or it is entirely empty?"

Having heard these things, the **Inquisitors**, who were well aware that such things were commonly taught by their own men, were stunned. Having consulted with one another, they rebuked him for busying himself <note>The original text includes a typographical repetition: "oc-cucuparetur" instead of "occuparetur" (to be occupied or busy with something).</note> with questions that did not concern him; they ordered him to leave such matters to the **Theologians** and to mind his own business.

### ON APPARITIONS

There was among us an honest and serious man who, before the light of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Euangelii lux"; likely a reference to the Reformation, which proponents often called the "return of the Gospel light"</note> emerged, used to pose this dilemma: *"Either the **Roman Pontiff** <note>The Pope</note> has the power to summon souls from the torments of **purgatory** <note>original: "purgatorii"</note>, or he does not. If he has that power, and does not use it except when money is provided, he cannot escape the charge of cruelty and greed. But if he truly does not have it, then it is a great crime indeed to rob so many widows and orphans on this account, and to boast insolently of a power he does not possess."* If, however, there is no purgatory (as is clearly gathered from the **Holy Scriptures** <note>original: "Scripturis sacris"</note>), then souls certainly cannot return from there, nor present themselves to be seen by others.

As for the fourth place, namely the **Limbo of children** <term>Limbo of children</term> <note>original: "Limbum puerorum"; in medieval theology, a place on the edge of Hell for those who died with original sin but no personal guilt</note> (in which innocent children, as they call them, are said to dwell), the **Papists** <note>A 16th-century term for Roman Catholics, used here by the Protestant author</note> will hardly dare to affirm that they return and appear to men to beg for help. For they teach that since these children died without **baptism** <note>original: "baptismo"</note>, they must forever lack the **sight of God** <note>original: "conspectu Dei"; also known as the Beatific Vision</note>; for this reason, they are not to be buried with other Christians in the same cemetery.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### *What those things are that are seen and*
### *heard: and first, indeed,*
### *that sometimes good angels appear.*

You say: I do not yet clearly and explicitly understand what those things are of which (as was said above) historians, the holy Fathers, and others make mention. For example: that the holy Apostles, Bishops, Martyrs, **Confessors** <note>In the early church, a 'Confessor' was someone who suffered for their Christian faith but was not killed, as opposed to a 'Martyr.'</note>, virgins, and others who died many years ago, have sometimes appeared to certain people when they were at the point of death; that they warned them, answered questions posed to them, and commanded them to do this or that. Likewise, you say that sometimes something is seen and heard which not only claims to be this or that **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note>, but adds how it might be helped; afterward, it returns and gives thanks to those from whom it received the benefit. Or, that a deceased husband approached his widowed wife at night; and that rarely does anything memorable happen that is not signaled beforehand by certain signs.

<note>The author is listing common reports of supernatural encounters—ranging from visions of saints to ghosts of family members—which he intends to explain through a theological lens, likely arguing that these are often angels or spirits rather than the actual souls of the deceased.</note>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>PART TWO</header>

I hear, you say, and I understand that those things are not the souls <note>original: "animas"</note> that dwell in their appointed places: what then are those things? To put it briefly: if it is not a vain delusion born from the weakness of the senses, fear, or other things of that sort, or human trickery, or a natural phenomenon—about which we noted many things in the first part—it is either a good or an evil angel, or else a warning sent from God. We will discuss this matter hereafter in order and more fully.

<margin>Angels appear. Matthew 18.</margin>
The Lord testifies in the Gospel that good children have their own angels. In Matthew 18, it is read that the Lord said: *“See that you do not despise one of these little ones. For I tell you that their angels in heaven always see the face of my heavenly Father.”* These words should not be understood as if they are never sent down to earth. Rather, the Lord speaks in a human manner. For just as ministers stand before their masters to carry out their commands, so angels are ready to serve God. Isaiah 63 says: *“The angel of his presence”* <note>original: "Angelus faciei ejus"</note>—that is, the one who stands in his sight—*“saved them.”* Furthermore, those who constantly stand by their masters are pleasing to them and are privy to their secrets. He sent others who reported the same thing; finally, he went himself, saw that it was indeed the case, and abandoned his undertakings. And so, in a wonderful way, God defended the city and church of *Constantinople* against the plots of that tyrant.

When *St. Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD) was one of the most influential theologians in Western Christianity.</note> writes in his book *On the Care to be Taken for the Dead, chapter 10*, that the dead have appeared to the living in dreams, or in any other way, and indicated to those who did not know where their unburied bodies lay, asking them to bury them: he judges that these are the operations of angels through the dispensation of divine providence, which makes good use of both the good and the bad according to the inscrutable depth of its judgments. He does not say that the souls themselves appear in dreams, but rather their likenesses. He adds that if the souls of the dead were involved in the affairs of the living, and if we ourselves could speak with them in dreams whenever we wished, his own mother—who followed him by land and sea so that she might live with him—would never have left him for a single night. Such was her natural affection <note>original: στοργή (*storgē*), a Greek term referring specifically to the instinctive love between parents and children</note> toward her son.

That not all those things are done by Satan, however, can be understood from this: It happens that someone is stabbed by another with whom he never had any enmity, but with whom he was always on familiar terms; someone perishes in the waters, falls from a height, or is otherwise miserably extinguished; an evil spirit cannot foreknow these things (for there are no natural [causes] for those things...

Meanwhile, the governor came to them, and they reported to him what had happened. They had scarcely finished speaking when the knife fell a third time, heard even by the governor, who previously had somewhat doubted the truth of the matter. Therefore, taking this as an opportunity, he urged them that—since a great wedding was to be celebrated in that place a few days later—they should all strive for harmony and moderation, lest that wedding be turned into a funeral by brawls and slaughter. He himself set out on his journey, and after a day or two, having finished his business, into the fortress...*

Indeed, I conclude that the Devil mocks humans even with unusual occurrences for this reason: that if some are seized by a grave illness, of whose recovery not only doctors but even the sick themselves despair, a noise is heard at night as if someone were constructing a coffin <note>original: "pheretrum," a bier or coffin</note>, or committing a dead man to burial. What **Pliny** <note>original: "Plin."; Pliny the Elder was a Roman author whose "Natural History" was the standard scientific text for centuries, though it contained many myths</note> writes—that the vulture is of such keenness <note>original: "sagacitatis," referring here to a prophetic or sharp-scented instinct</note> that it flies to a place where there will be corpses two or three days in advance—is entirely mythical. In addition to these things, by the Lord's permission, if wars and rebellions are imminent, he can move artillery <term>tormenta bellica: literally "war engines," referring to cannons or siege equipment</term> and weapons of every kind in armories, and stir up a noise, and create in the air the shouting and roaring of a great army, the beating of drums, and other things of that kind, which historians all testify with one voice have often occurred.

### CHAPTER XVII.

*It is not difficult for the devil to appear in various forms and to perform miraculous feats.*

Furthermore, it is evident that it is not difficult for the devil to appear in various guises—not only as living men, but also as the dead (a matter I also discussed earlier when dealing with the appearance of Samuel <note>The author refers to the biblical story in 1 Samuel 28, where the Witch of Endor appears to summon the spirit of the deceased prophet Samuel to speak with King Saul</note>). Moreover, and even more commonly, he can appear as four-footed animals, birds, and so on—such as in the form of a black dog, a horse, or an owl—and perform incredible things. For through his long and varied experience, he is able to possess knowledge of the power and effects of natural things, such as herbs, stones, and the like, and through these he can work wonders.

Furthermore, he is a subtle and nimble spirit <term>spiritus: in this context, a non-physical being capable of moving with immense speed and manipulating the physical world</term> who can skillfully manipulate objects, a fact that is of no small importance in any undertaking. By his speed and his knowledge of the natural world, he can deceive the sight and the other human senses; he can hide the things placed right before us and substitute others in their stead. ### Concerning Specters

...was most learned in the magical art, and through it performed many wonders. Whenever he wished, he transformed himself into a wolf <note>this refers to lycanthropy, a common theme in medieval magic trials</note>, or into the form of any other beast, so that he was entirely unseen by anyone, and he was able to and did perform other marvelous things, by which he moved men to great amazement.

Next, he writes that in the year 876, there was a certain Jew named Sedechias <note>a legendary physician often associated with the court of Louis the Pious</note>, formerly the ***Physician and doctor of Emperor Louis***, who, being instructed in the magical disciplines, performed many miracles and stupendous illusions <term>ludibria: deceptive spectacles or "mockeries" of the senses</term> publicly before princes and various others. He would cut off men's heads, hands, or feet, which, placed before them in a basin with blood flowing out, he presented for all to see; these he immediately restored to the places from which they appeared to have been severed, without any harm to the men. The Duke said to him, "Who are you?" To which the spirit replied, "Do not ask who I am, for it is irrelevant to the matter; but mount this horse which I present to you, and I will lead you back to *Austria*, freed from here safe and sound."

Upon hearing these things, horror seized the Duke—who was otherwise a very bold man—and when he protected himself with the sign of the holy cross, the spirit vanished along with the black horse he had presented, and returned empty-handed to the one who sent him. After being rebuked by the magician for not bringing the captive, the spirit narrated the sequence of what had occurred. Eventually, once Duke *Frederick* was liberated from custody <note>Frederick was eventually released in 1325 after the Treaty of Trausnitz</note>, he confessed on a specific day that this very event had happened to him while he was in captivity.

This history also exists in the annals of the *Swiss* <note>original: "Helvetiorum"</note> and others.

## CHAPTER XVIII.

*The devils sometimes command men to do what is good, and to avoid the opposite: they also sometimes tell the truth, and why.*

If those spirits seeking help are not souls but devils, many ask: why, then, do they persuade men toward good things, urge them toward virtues, and call them away from crimes? For they say: "Judge justly," "guard yourselves against theft and robbery," "restore ill-gotten wealth to its rightful owners," "beware of false oaths <note>original: "dejerationibus"—referring to perjury or swearing falsely in God's name</note>, gluttony, and drunkenness, hatred and envy, lies and deceit," "pray fervently," "attend church assemblies," and so on. It does not please the devil if we do good or avoid the opposite; he would endure nothing more painfully than if we were to shape our lives according to the prescription of the Word of **God**. Therefore, [some argue] they are not devils who command us to do what is good and flee what is evil.

->PART SECOND<-

Furthermore, these spirits speak the truth, whereas the devil is a liar, and is called the "father of lies" by Christ <note>original: "à Christo pater mendaciorum appellatur"—a reference to John 8:44</note>: therefore, it should not be said that these **Apparitions** <term>Spectra: visual manifestations of spirits or ghosts</term> are diabolical.

I respond to this argument in this way: the devil does these things for his own benefit. If he presented himself to be seen as he truly is by nature, he would accomplish very little. What he does, he does to this end: to win trust for his words, so that he may foist other matters upon men and lead them into various errors and plunge them into ruin. His goal is that, having neglected the Word of **God**, men might instead lend their ears to those spirits. 

Did not the servants of the impure spirit—namely, the **False Prophets**—of old come clothed in sheepskins <note>A reference to the biblical warning in Matthew 7:15 about wolves in sheep's clothing</note> and pretend that they were devoted to the welfare of the common people? Yet all the while they sought something else: specifically, that having gained great authority, they might rob others and fill their own purses with gold and silver. 

Do not all **heretics** in this day and age say that they are sent by **God**, that one must beware of crimes, and that one must strive for virtue? Have you never heard of how **robbers**, when encountering travelers on the road, have conversed with them about leading a proper life, or about the punishments of the wicked and the rewards of the good, so that...

## SECOND PART

Therefore, having recognized these things, let us not believe the demon at all. On the contrary, even if he speaks some truth, let us flee from him and turn away from him. For sound and life-giving teachings <note>original: "dogmata salutiferaque"</note> can be learned with precision not from demons, but from *Holy Scripture*.

In order that you may know it is impossible for a soul that has departed the body to come under the tyranny of demons, hear what Paul says: 

> *For he who dies is justified from sin* <note>original: "justificatus est à peccato"—a citation of Romans 6:7</note>; *that is, he no longer sins.* 

For if the devil cannot inflict violence upon a soul while it remains in the body, it is evident that he cannot do so once it has departed, etc.

From all these points, it is clear what those things truly are which are seen and heard <note>The author is referring to "specters" or apparitions, arguing they are demonic deceptions rather than actual returning souls</note>.

...why He permits other astonishing and miraculous things to happen. Then, in what manner people ought to behave themselves, when anything of this kind is presented to them.

<margin>God permits these things for the good of the faithful.</margin>
God allows spirits to appear to His chosen ones for their good, but to the reprobate <term>reprobate: those who are rejected by God and left to their own sin and punishment</term> for their punishment: and just as all things work together for the good of the faithful, so do these things as well. For if they are good spirits who are seen by the pious, and they warn and defend them: from this they recognize God’s care, providence, and fatherly affection toward them. But if they are evil spirits, as they usually are, the faithful are stirred to true repentance. They guard themselves diligently throughout their whole life, lest the enemy of the human race <note>original: "hostis humani generis" — a traditional theological title for the Devil</note>, who is intent on every opportunity and constantly lies in wait for them, should plunge them into wickedness and seize a greater power for harming and vexing them. God also exercises and tests their faith and patience in this manner, so that they may persist in His *Word*: and that they admit nothing which conflicts with it, however plausible an appearance it may have: nor should they act in any way against the *Word*, even if those spirits do not immediately cease to trouble them. *God* furthermore permits, for this reason also, by

[God permits] spirits to trouble His own people, so that they may be more humble.

In the twelfth chapter of the Second Epistle to the Corinthians, Paul says, *“Lest I should be exalted above measure by the abundance of the revelations, there was given to me a thorn in the flesh, a messenger of Satan, to buffet me—lest, I say, I should be exalted above measure. Concerning this thing, I pleaded with the Lord three times that it might depart from me: and He said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you, for my strength is made perfect in weakness.’”* <note>original: "stimulus per carnem" — traditionally translated as "a thorn in the flesh," this refers to a physical or spiritual trial intended to keep the Apostle humble.</note> Unless God closed off our path with certain obstacles, we would not know ourselves, nor would we understand what things we truly need: we would not pray so fervently that God might preserve us from evil, strengthen our faith, grant us patience, and so on. Nor would we be moved by the misfortunes of others who are troubled by spirits <note>original: "Spectris" — refers to ghosts, phantoms, or supernatural sightings</note>: we would say they do not know what they are talking about, and that many imagine their own terrors. Furthermore, if others hear that even the godly are tried by spirits, they are more patient in their own illnesses and other hardships, thinking their own evils to be small if they are compared with others: for nothing is more terrible than if someone is tormented by the devil.

->### ON SPECTERS ###<-

From these words, we see not only that God permits those scoundrels <note>original: "nebulones" — literally "worthless fellows" or "rascals," here referring to false prophets or deceptive spirits</note> to perform wondrous things, but also for what purpose He permits this. *In the 3rd chapter of John, Christ our Savior says:*

> *This is the condemnation: that Light has come into the world, and men loved darkness more than light, for their deeds were evil. For everyone who does evil hates the light; nor does he come to the light, lest his deeds be exposed, etc.*

By these words, our Savior shows why the world is condemned: specifically, because it does not receive the light of the Divine Word—that is, Christ Himself, who is the light of the world and who is presented to us in the Word. Instead, the world closes its eyes to the clear light and prefers darkness—meaning various errors, superstitions, and crimes—above the Word of God. If God condemns and casts away the ungrateful world for these reasons, is it any wonder if He vexes them with specters <note>original: "Spectris" — refers to ghosts or frightening apparitions</note> and various apparitions? *In the 5th chapter of John, Christ says:*

<header>PART THREE</header>

**[...comes] in his own name, you will receive him.** Christ was concerned for their salvation and safety: they refused to acknowledge this but instead rejected him. Therefore, it was God’s just judgment that they should receive others who served their own interests and utility—such as *Theudas*, *Judas the Galilean* <note>Theudas and Judas the Galilean were historical figures mentioned in the Book of Acts (5:36-37) as leaders of failed uprisings who gathered many followers before being killed.</note>, and many other false teachers and seditious deceivers. For this reason, if any disdain to hear Christ and his ministers, it happens by God’s just judgment that they give their ears to *Specters* <note>original: "Spectris" — ghosts or supernatural apparitions</note> and other things of that kind. In *2 Thessalonians 2*, *Paul writes concerning the Antichrist, that he will exercise great tyranny in the Church of God.* He shows against whom, and why God will permit him to do this, saying:

> *Toward those who perish, because they did not receive the love of the truth, so that they might be saved. And for this reason, God will send them a powerful delusion* <note>original: "efficaciam illusionis" — literally the 'efficacy of illusion' or a 'working of error'</note>, *so that they believe a lie, that all may be judged who did not believe the truth, but approved of injustice.*

In *2 Timothy 4*, Paul adjures his disciple <note>Timothy</note> to be diligent and assiduous in his preaching. He gives this reason:

> *For the time will come when they will not endure sound doctrine: but according to their own lusts, they will heap up for themselves teachers, because they have itching ears* <note>original: "pruriunt aures" — a metaphor for a restless desire for novelty or hearing only what is pleasing</note>: *and they will turn their ears away from the truth...*

There are also other causes for which these occurrences are rarer. If someone deceives you once, twice, or a third time, afterwards you open your eyes; you see what he is doing and what he is plotting. In the same way, since we have so often been deceived by fabricated visions, we do not easily believe it if we are told that some soul or spirit has appeared. The fisherman, as the proverb goes, is wise after being struck <note>original: "piscator ictus sapit." This Latin proverb refers to learning a lesson through painful experience—like a fisherman who becomes more cautious after being stung by a fish.</note>. To this we may add that since few people now fear **Specters** for themselves, people could easily be found who would want to seek them out, see them, and indeed even touch them—this is well known. Therefore, no one easily puts on a mask <note>original: "personam," referring to a theatrical mask or a disguise used to impersonate a ghost.</note> or otherwise lies about being a soul. They will laugh at your foolishness if you thereafter attempt to terrify them in such a way.

## CHAPTER III.

### *Why God allows unusual crashes and disordered noises to be heard before significant changes and other events.*

The fact that certain things precede the death of men, as well as significant changes and destructions—such as marvelous crashes <term>fragores</term> and disordered rumblings <term>inconditi fremitus</term>—is done for the good of the faithful and as a punishment for the unfaithful. For by these things **God** shows that nothing happens by chance, but that life, death, prosperity, and adversity are placed in God's hand. Things are by no means as...

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page's chapter heading, which asks why God allows strange noises to be heard before major life events. The author is refuting the idea that these are random occurrences.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>

# PART THREE

<meta>catchword: things</meta>

The Epicureans <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Epicurus, who in this period were often stereotyped as believing God was indifferent to human affairs.</note> say, namely, that *God* does not care whether someone dies or is born, whether one fares well or poorly, or whether commonwealths flourish or perish. *Christ* himself teaches us that not even a small sparrow falls to the earth apart from the will of *God*. *Solomon* and *Daniel* say that *God* holds the heart of the *King* in his hand, and that he appoints and deposes *Kings*. Therefore, if we hear those crashes and other things of that kind, they should encourage us rather than terrify us. Furthermore, *God* warns us by these things so that we are not sluggish and complacent, for in every age he has stirred up his people not only with words, but also with wonderful events. The *Gentiles* <note>Non-Christian or pagan peoples of antiquity.</note> also regarded those miraculous things as warnings from their own gods, as can be seen throughout their histories. Although it is likely that many such things are brought about by an evil spirit <term>cacodaemon</term> <note>original: "cacodaemone." A Greek-derived term for a malevolent spirit or demon, often used in the Renaissance to distinguish between purely "evil" entities and more neutral spirits.</note>, we nevertheless see in these matters the fatherly care and benevolence of *God*, the best and greatest, toward us, and his defense against the snares of the devil. For even if the devil is restless and prepared at every opportunity to destroy us, he cannot harm us while the Lord keeps watch for us and protects us. The impious, who despise the preaching of the word of *God*, [are moved] by these...

### CHAPTER IV.

->*How those to whom good or evil spirits appear, or to whom other wonderful things are presented, ought to conduct themselves: and in the first place, how the Gentiles and Jews conducted themselves in such cases.*<-

So that we may more rightly understand how it is fitting for us to behave if something appears, whether it be good or evil, we will first indicate how the Gentiles <note>The "Gentiles" here refers to the ancient Greeks, Romans, and other non-Christian "pagan" peoples.</note> and Jews behaved in these cases in former times.

<margin>What the Gentiles accepted.</margin>
Among the erring Gentiles, those spirits not only said they were souls, but also indicated what would be appropriate and beneficial to do for their sake: namely, that the *Feralia* <note>An ancient Roman festival held in February to honor the spirits of the deceased (the Manes).</note> and *Parentalia* <note>A nine-day festival in ancient Rome honoring deceased ancestors, involving offerings at their tombs.</note> should be celebrated...

<header>Part Three</header>

...[that gifts] should be given, their bodies buried, temples built, festivals performed, and other things of that kind.

*Suetonius* <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the twelve Caesars.</note> writes that the corpse of the Emperor *Caligula* was secretly carried into the *Lamian Gardens* <note>A set of private gardens on the Esquiline Hill in Rome.</note>, partially burned on a makeshift funeral pyre, and covered with a light layer of sod; later, it was exhumed, cremated, and properly buried by his sisters after they returned from exile. Before this was done, the caretakers of the gardens were disturbed by ghosts <note>original: "umbris," literally "shadows" or "shades."</note>. In that house where he had died, no night passed without some terror until the house itself was consumed by fire. Among other writers, we also read that those who were not legitimately buried, or if the usual ceremonies had been interrupted during wartime, their ghosts appeared to their kin or to others, complaining and asking that the proper rites <note>original: "justa." In Roman Law and custom, these were the "due" or "obligatory" funeral honors.</note> and other ceremonies be performed for their sake. From this arose cenotaphs <note>Empty tombs or monuments erected in honor of a person whose remains are elsewhere or lost.</note>, the "Seventh-day" and "Thirtieth-day" rites <note>Specific memorial days following a death, common in both ancient Roman and later Christian traditions.</note>, and anniversary observances, about which many things are found among pagan <note>original: "Ethnicos."</note> writers; and in *Lilio Giraldi's* <note>Lilio Gregorio Giraldi (1479–1552), a prominent Renaissance humanist scholar.</note> *small book on burial*, several instances are mentioned from the ancient poets. Likewise in *Polydore Vergil's* <note>Polydore Vergil (c. 1470–1555), an Italian humanist priest and historian who lived in England.</note> *On the Invention of Things, book 6, chapter 10* <note>original: "de invent. rerum."</note>. He writes that certain people requested after their death that they be buried...

# ON SPECTERS.

...[how they are] produced, we explained above in the *second part, chapter 1*. *Cicero* writes in *Book 5 of On the Laws* <note>original: "de Legibus," a philosophical dialogue by Marcus Tullius Cicero regarding the origins of law and religious tradition.</note> that *Romulus*, the founder of the city of *Rome*, walking not far from the house of *Atticus* after his departure from this life, told *Proculus Julius* that he was a god and was called *Quirinus* <note>The name given to the deified Romulus as a protector of the Roman state.</note>, and he ordered a temple to be dedicated to himself in that place.

*Ovid* writes in *Book 5 of the Fasti* <note>A poetic calendar explaining Roman festivals and their origins.</note> that *Remus* appeared by night to *Faustulus* and *Acca Larentia* (Faustulus's wife and his own nurse), complaining of his wretched death, and he asked them to see to it that the day on which he was slain should be held among the festival days. <margin>Feralia.</margin> The *Romans*, as *Ovid* testifies in *Book 2 of the Fasti*, celebrated the *Feralia* in the month of February, during which the proper rites were paid to the ancestral spirits <note>original: "diis manibus," the "Divine Manes" or spirits of the dead who were honored as minor deities.</note>, and feasts were brought to the tombs by those who had the right to perform parental rites there; for this reason, the *Feralia* were so named according to *Festus* and *Varro* <note>Two significant Roman scholars of language and history.</note>.

In these sacred rites, the feasts were placed upon a flint stone, which from that—as *Servius* <note>A late 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note> says—were called *Silicernium* <note>A funeral feast or a sacrifice offered to the dead.</note>. By this word, some mean to signify a funeral banquet that is provided for the elderly. *Donatus* <note>A celebrated Roman grammarian and teacher of rhetoric.</note> says that the *Silicernium* is the supper brought to the ancestral spirits, because they see it in silence <note>original: "silentes cernant," an etymological pun on the word Silicernium.</note>—that is, the shadows possess it—or because those who bring it see it, but do not taste it,

### PART THREE

...and so forth. They were also called the *Parentalia* <margin>Parentalia.</margin>, sacred rites in which feasts prepared for parents or ancestors were carried to the tombs. Although they believed that the spirits of the dead <note>original: "manes," the ancestral spirits honored by the Romans as minor deities.</note> could be appeased with small gifts—such as milk, wine, and other offerings mentioned in the works of *Ovid*—they also sacrificed animal victims. Because of this, some believe the *Feralia* <note>The public festival concluding the Parentalia.</note> were named from the "striking" <note>original: "feriendis," suggesting a linguistic link between *Feralia* and *ferire*, to strike or slaughter.</note> of cattle. To these sacrifices, they added prayers and lit lamps. 

When the Romans, hindered by wars, once interrupted the observance of the *Parentalia*, they believed the spirits grew angry. They thought that because of this neglect, great storms and a plague arose, and the shadows of the deceased, having left their graves, wandered with mournful cries around the tombs, as well as through the city streets and the fields. This festival lasted for eleven days, during which time wives lived apart from their husbands, those of marriageable age did not contract new marriages, and the statues of the gods were veiled. When the shadows of the departed came to the feasts, they wandered about the tombs and fed upon the *funeral meal* <note>original: "filicernio," a specific type of meal eaten at the grave site, previously identified as being laid upon a stone.</note>.

<margin>Lemuria.</margin> In the *month of May*, a nighttime festival was celebrated which they initially called the *Remuria*, but later named the *Lemuria*. This festival does not differ much from the *Feralia*, as both were established to appease the *Lemures* <note>The restless or malevolent spirits of the dead, as opposed to the more benevolent "manes."</note>. Regarding their origin and rituals, see *Ovid*...

# ON SPECTERS.

In Book 5 of the *Fasti* <note>original: "Fast.", Ovid's poetic calendar of Roman festivals and customs.</note>, it is written that he who was to appease the shadows would rise at the dead of night. He walked with bare feet and washed himself with spring water. He would throw black beans—which he had first turned over in his mouth—behind his back, and as he did so, he said that he was redeeming <note>Buying back or protecting his soul and his household.</note> himself with them. He would then clash bronze vessels together and pray for the shadows to depart. If they performed this nine times, they believed they had completed the sacred rites, which were celebrated for three days. The sacrifices made for those in the underworld are called *Inferiae* <note>original: "Inferias," offerings such as wine, milk, or blood dedicated to the spirits of the deceased.</note>.

In the works of *Lucan* <note>A Roman poet; the reference is likely to his epic poem *Pharsalia*, which features scenes of necromancy.</note>, we read about the rituals used to appease the shadows of *Sylla* and *Marius* <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla and Gaius Marius, rival Roman generals whose ghosts were said to haunt the city during the civil wars.</note>. We mentioned previously that the philosopher *Athenodorus* ordered that the bones found unearthed in the entrance of a house in *Athens* should be given a lawful burial, and so forth.

<margin>What the *Jews* did.</margin>
The ancient *Jews* had a clear command from God not to be moved by the miracles of false prophets; and He expressly forbade them from consulting the dead. At the beginning of his reign, while he was still devoted to piety, *Saul* destroyed magicians and sorceresses. As to how the *Jews* of our own time behave when *Specters* <note>Apparitions or ghosts.</note> appear, I do not remember ever hearing or reading; however, I have no doubt that they are superstitious in this matter, just as they are in other things.

# CHAPTER V.

### *How Christians, to whom Specters present themselves, ought to conduct themselves; and first, they must be of strong heart and firm in faith.*

Indeed, how Christians should conduct themselves in such a case is most fully shown to us—as are all other matters pertaining to our salvation—in the *Holy Scriptures* <note>original: "sacris Literis"</note>. Specifically, we must first be brave, fearless, and steadfast in the true faith. For if they are good angels who reveal themselves, they are sent to us by *God* for our benefit; if they are evil, they cannot—however much they may wish—inflict harm upon us without *God’s* permission. If the experience is nothing more than a vain delusion or an empty phantom <term>simulachrum: an illusory image or a mere likeness of a thing without substance</term>, it is surely foolish to be afraid. 

It is natural, of course, that we should shudder when these things are presented to us. For even holy men in the *Old and New Testaments* trembled at the encounter and sight of good angels; nevertheless, one’s courage must be gathered again. When the disciples of *Christ* saw the *Lord* walking upon the waters, and [approaching] <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "pro-" likely begins "propinquantem" (approaching).</note> the ship...

### CHAPTER VI

->*Those who are troubled by specters <term>spectris: ghosts or supernatural apparitions</term> should especially persist in prayer, fasting, sobriety, watching, and the pursuit of a pious life.*<-

And since good angels appear more rarely to people in our age (for the condition of people under the New Testament <note>original: "novo Testamento"</note> is far different from those under the Old Testament <note>original: "veteri"</note>, to whom God frequently sent His angels), and evil spirits appear more frequently when we go to bed or when we rise, we ought all the more diligently to commend ourselves to God.

<margin>Intercessions.</margin>
It is also useful to seek the prayers of the entire Church when we are vexed by *Lemures* <term>Lemuribus: restless or mischievous spirits of the dead</term> or *Specters*. For we know that the prayers of the Church are useful and effective in this and other cases, and the faithful have always sought them in their hardships. *God* also wills that in this life the faithful support one another with their prayers. However, as for the idea that the Saints pray for us after they have departed from here, or that we ought to seek their prayers, no commandment of God or example exists in *Holy Scripture* <note>original: "sacris literis"</note>.

The *Apostles* also teach that the snares of the devil must be resisted in this way. *St. Paul in Ephesians 6; Peter in the 1st Epistle, chapter 5: Be sober, he says, and watch: because our adversary the devil walks about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour. ### CHAPTER VII.

->*Spirits that appear should rightly be held suspect by us: conversations are not to be held with them; nothing is to be learned from them.*<-

All spirits or apparitions ought to be suspect to us, and not without cause. For although *God* uses the work and ministry of good angels for the salvation of His faithful, they appear more rarely in our own times. Indeed, things have been far different since the coming of *Christ* into this world <note>original: "post Christi adventum," referring to the theological belief that the physical presence of Christ and the completion of scripture changed the frequency of angelic visits</note> than they were in ancient times. Even if it seems to you that you have seen a good angel, do not believe it easily or rashly. If the event itself later shows that it was a good angel—one who warned you excellently about some matter or delivered you from great dangers—give thanks to the *Lord* that He acts so mercifully and fatherly toward you and takes care of you; devote yourself to His will. But if instead an angel is seen by you who flatters and adulates you—such as those spirits who seek help <note>original: "spiritus qui opem petunt," likely referring to ghosts claiming to be in purgatory needing prayers or masses</note>—you have already heard be-

<note>original: "verbis," continuing the sentence from the previous page</note> by their words that their reliability is in no way to be accepted. Men who flatter and fawn over us are suspicious to us; why, then, should those spirits not be suspicious to us? Do not enter into conversation with those spirits; do not ask them what should be returned, what should be done, or what the future holds. Do not ask who they are, or for what reason they show themselves to be seen and heard. For if they are good, it will be pleasing to them if you wish to hear nothing except the Word of God; but if they are evil, they will attempt to deceive you with lies. *When, in the first chapter of Matthew, the angel instructed Joseph through a vision, he immediately added the testimony of prophetic Scripture. Christ and the Apostles, however much they were endowed with the power to perform miracles, nevertheless confirmed their teachings through the Holy Scriptures.*

## CHAPTER VIII.

*Sacred Scriptural testimonies, and an example showing that faith should not be placed in apparitions of this kind, and that we ought to be cautious.*

That faith should not be immediately granted to all those things which are heard is taught not only by experience and many proverbs, but by the *Sacred Scriptures* themselves: especially in matters of our salvation, regarding which we will bring forward only certain passages and examples.

When Christ first sent out his disciples to preach the Gospel, he said to them in the 10th chapter of *Matthew*: *Be as wise as serpents, and as simple as doves:* <note>original: "Estote prudentes sicut serpentes, & simplices sicut columbæ." ## CHAPTER IX.

### *How the faithful in the early Church conducted themselves when encountering specters.*

I have already explained from the *Word of God* how godly people ought to conduct themselves at the appearance of specters <note>The author uses "Spectra" to refer to any supernatural apparition, ghost, or spirit.</note>, and the ancient *Christians* conducted themselves in this very manner. For they were brave and fearless, they strove for holiness and all good works, and they studiously guarded themselves against those things which displease *God*; moreover, they were cautious not to attribute too much importance to these *Specters*.

<margin>The ancients protected themselves with the cross.</margin>
It was a common custom, when encountering these things, to arm oneself with the sign of the cross; many people are still accustomed to doing the same today. *Tertullian* writes in his book *On the Soldier’s Crown* <note>original: "de Corona militis." A treatise by the early Christian author Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) regarding Christian conduct.</note> that the early *Christians* frequently marked their foreheads with the cross. *Saint Jerome* <note>Latin: "D. Hieronymus." A prominent 4th-century scholar and translator of the Bible.</note> urges *Demetrias* <note>A Roman noblewoman to whom Jerome wrote letters of spiritual advice.</note> to protect her forehead frequently with the seal of the cross, lest the "exterminator of Egypt" <note>A reference to the Angel of Death from the Book of Exodus; Jerome suggests the cross acts as a spiritual shield against destruction.</note> find a place within her. *Origen* also, and *Epiphanius*—

<summary>This page discusses the historical behavior of early Christians when facing supernatural apparitions, emphasizing their courage and their use of the sign of the cross as a protective measure. It cites early Church Fathers like Tertullian and Jerome to validate these practices.</summary>
<keywords>early Church, specters, sign of the cross, Tertullian, Saint Jerome, spiritual protection, holiness, apparitions</keywords>

*Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching.</note> and *Augustine* <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity.</note> write many things regarding the power of the holy cross. *Saint Athanasius* <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria and a chief defender of Christian orthodoxy.</note> writes in his *Book on the Humanity of the Word and His Bodily Advent*, leaf 67: *"Long ago,"* he says, *"demons would entangle people with empty appearances and mockeries of things, haunting springs here, rivers there, stones or woods, and thus by their illusions they drove the foolish into a stupor. But now, since the Word of God has appeared, specters of this kind and these mocking images have ceased."* <note>original: "Olim, inquit, dæmonia inani specie ludibriisque rerum intricabant homines... Nunc verò cùm apparuerit Dei verbum Spectra hujusmodi & ludibria imaginum cessaverunt."</note> On leaf 56, likewise 72, and in other places he treats the same subject.

*Lactantius* <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD), an early Christian author and advisor to the first Christian Roman emperor, Constantine I.</note> writes in Book 4, Chapter 26 of the *Divine Institutes*, and throughout the entirety of Chapter 27 regarding this matter: he says that the devil has no access to those who sign their forehead with the cross, nor is he able to harm them. He adds that Christians in ancient times used this ceremony in expelling demons and healing diseases.

<margin>*Whether the sign of the cross possesses any power.*</margin>
It was not because they attributed such great power and efficacy to the external sign of the cross (for this would be <term>superstitious</term> <note>The author is careful to clarify that the physical gesture itself has no magic; the "superstition" would be believing the shape of the cross works like a charm without faith.</note>), but to the Cross itself—that is, to the <term>merit of Christ</term>, whose dignity they recalled to mind at the same time. Regarding the holy *Apostles* and the *Apostolic Churches*, however, we do not read that they used the ceremony of the cross for expelling demo-

...eases or curing illnesses, or have used it in other ways as well. The *Jews*, who in *Egypt* marked the lintel with the blood of the lamb, were spared: not because the blood of a sheep had the power to preserve men from destruction, but because it was a type <note>original: "typus" — In theology, a 'type' is a person, event, or object in the Old Testament seen as a symbolic foreshadowing of something in the New Testament.</note> of the blood and cross of *Christ*. The *Jews* indeed did not sprinkle the blood out of their own "good intention," as they call it, but by the command of the *Lord*. The *Holy Fathers*, by that ceremony in which they signed themselves with the cross, wished to bear witness to their trust in the cross—that is, in the death of *Christ*—which drives away all evils.

<margin>
The wooden cross.
</margin>
The Devil fears neither the cross with which we sign ourselves, nor those fragments of the cross of *Christ* which are shown as relics; rather, he fears the efficacy and power of *Christ’s* death, through which he himself was conquered and overthrown. If anyone attributes too much to ceremonies, they cannot be excused from superstition, which deserves rebuke.

We read, moreover, among the *Ancient Writers*, that in the early *Church*, they used exorcisms against demons.

In addition to this, we read that the ancients drove out impure spirits from people and from those places in which, through their commotion, they struck great terror into many and harmed them. This gift lasted for a long time in the *Church* for the benefit of the faithful, but it later ceased, just as other miracles did. What *Tertullian* <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage, c. 155–220 AD.</note> writes in his *Apology* <note>original: "Apologetico" — A famous defense of Christianity against paganism.</note>, on pages 858 and 859, also relates to this.

Now we have understood well enough how the holy Fathers and ancient Christians conducted themselves when *Specters* <note>original: "Spectra" — Apparitions, ghosts, or supernatural manifestations.</note> appeared to them.
