## CHAPTER X.

### That various superstitions gradually grew, by which people sought to drive away specters.

<margin>2 Thessalonians 2; 1 John 4</margin>
Over the course of time, superstitions increased more and more. Paul complains that even in his own time the *Antichrist* had begun to work the mystery of iniquity <note>original: "mysterium iniquitatis" — a biblical concept referring to the hidden presence of lawlessness or evil in the world</note>, and that various sects had arisen. Saint John writes that in his own time many *Antichrists* had appeared. Is it any wonder, then, if later—and indeed quite early on—various errors crept into the *Church* and were subsequently multiplied?

Saint Augustine <note>A hugely influential North African theologian and bishop, 354–430 AD.</note>, in the 22nd book of *The City of God*, chapter 8, after recounting several miracles performed so that people might believe in Christ, relates the following story:
> A man of tribunitial rank <note>original: "Vir tribunitius" — referring to a high-ranking administrative or military official in the Roman Empire</note> named Hesperius, who lives among us, has an estate called Cubedi in the territory of Fussala. When he discovered that his house was suffering from the harmful power of evil spirits, causing affliction to his animals and servants, he asked our Priests, during my absence, that one of them go there, of whose...

He had hung it in his bedroom to protect himself from suffering any harm. But once his house was purged of that infestation, he began to wonder what should be done with that earth, for out of reverence he no longer wished to keep it in his bedroom, etc. 

From this it is clear that **superstition** began immediately and, as typically happens, grew with a massive increase. It is just as if someone pushes a ball of snow, or if a mass of snow begins to roll down the Alps—soon everything far and wide is filled with snow. Shortly thereafter, people also began to **pray for souls** <note>original: "orari pro animabus" — the practice of intercessory prayer for the deceased</note> and to offer sacrifices for them. This was certainly done with good intentions, as can be clearly understood from certain passages written by the **holy Fathers** <note>original: "sanctorum Patrum" — the influential early Christian theologians and bishops of the first few centuries</note>.

Later, when the **Bishops** and <term>Parish Priests</term> <note>original: "Parochi" — local clergy responsible for the care of souls in a specific district</note> not only failed to correct these old superstitions but actually increased them with what they called "good intentions," they finally grew to an immense degree. For whenever **specters** <note>original: "spectra" — apparitions or ghostly sightings</note> appeared, people did not [turn to...]

<margin>The Angelic Salutation.</margin>
Certain authors write that to drive away *Specters*, it is especially helpful if we pray the *Hail Mary* <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note>. Here it should be noted in passing that this "Angelic Salutation" is not actually a prayer, but a greeting and a historical narrative: namely, how the *Archangel Gabriel* predicted the incarnation of *Christ* to the holy Virgin. But consider, I ask you, the meaning of the words, and you will be forced to admit—whether you like it or not—that these words contain neither a petition nor an expression of thanks, which are the two essential types of prayer. When the angel approached her, he greeted her, saying *chaire* <note>original: "χαιρε" — Greek for "rejoice" or "hail"</note>, which is to say "hail" or "rejoice." (<margin>To hail is to rejoice.</margin> For the *Greek* word and the Latin *Avere* <note>the root of "Ave"</note> have the same force, according to the testimony of *Festus* <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian whose work on the Latin language was a standard reference for Renaissance scholars.</note>). ...as soon as the priest elevated the host <note>original: "hostiam"; the consecrated bread of the Eucharist</note>, as they call it, during the celebration of the Mass, the people would immediately wipe their faces with their hands. If you bring up the story from the book of *Tobit* <note>original: "Tobiæ"; a book found in the Catholic and Orthodox Bibles but regarded as apocryphal or non-canonical by Protestant Reformers</note>, where the *heart and liver of a fish* placed upon a *burning coal* drove away a *demon* by its scent, we reply first that this book is not counted among the Canonical scriptures. Furthermore, we say that the demon was driven away by the fervent prayers of *Tobit* and his wife, rather than by the smoke. Did *Christ* institute the *Holy Supper* <term>sacram Cœnam: the Eucharist or Communion service</term> for the purpose of casting out demons? Even if the evil spirit pretends to retreat because of such things, he nonetheless ensures that superstitions take deeper root in the hearts of men.

## CHAPTER XI.

->*Specters are not to be driven away with curses*<-
->*and execrations.*<-

At this point, I cannot pass over the fact that some people falsely convince themselves that *specters* <note>original: "Spectra"; apparitions or ghosts</note> are driven away with no effort by curses and execrations <note>original: "diris & execrationibus"; the use of foul language, oaths, or ritualized shouting to repel spirits</note>. Indeed, [they claim] that *specters* draw near to those who pour out prayers, and disturb and vex them even more violently. Our Lord *Jesus Christ*, who knows best how one must fight against the snares of the devil, teaches us in more than one place to pray with perseverance. In the Lord's Prayer <note>original: "precatione dominica"</note>, he commands us to pray *that we may be delivered from evil*, calling Satan "the evil one" preeminently <note>original: "Κατ' ἐξοχὴν τὸν πονηρὸν" (Kat' exochēn ton ponēron); the Greek phrase identifies Satan as the personification of evil</note>. Nothing more pleasing can happen to the devil than if someone uses curses and execrations. He pretends to be driven away by such things, but meanwhile, he invisibly insinuates himself into the company of those people. If you wish, you can drive the devil away by saying that he has no place with you, but rather in Tartarus <note>original: "Tartaro"; a term for the abyss or hell</note>: and that he has no right over those who place their trust in *Christ* alone. For in the 8th chapter of the Epistle to the Romans, it is said shortly from the begin- <meta>catchword: ini- (likely referring to "initio" or "beginning")</meta>

<summary>The author warns against using curses or verbal abuse to repel spirits, arguing that the devil only pretends to be offended by such outbursts while actually gaining ground. Instead, he advocates for persistent prayer and faith in Christ as the only effective defenses.</summary>

For with this kind of "demon," good and holy words do not accomplish as much as a good beating.

# ON SPECTERS.

...[to those] who throughout their entire lives have encountered nothing of this sort, let them give thanks to God for this great blessing. They should not be reckless or daring, nor should they seek to have such things shown to them; rather, they should intercede with the *Lord* on behalf of those who are tormented by these evils. They should not act as those who have never been seriously ill are prone to do—for such people are not moved by the diseases of others, believing them to be only mildly sick, or perhaps even faking their illness, until they themselves fall into grave and dangerous maladies. In the same way, God can place before those who have seen no **Specters** <note>original: "Spectra"</note> up until now some [apparitions] by which they may be troubled, so that thereafter they may be moved by the misfortunes of others and pray for them diligently.

### PART THREE

[which] were mentioned above, should happen, what then must be done? Too much weight should not be attributed to them; for those things sometimes—indeed, most often—occur by the **devil's cunning** <note>original: "diaboli astu"</note>, to whom it would be most pleasing if men thought about such things day and night. He wishes to place many horrific things before their eyes and minds so that they might fall into grievous illnesses and have no quiet day. When those things truly happen, they ought to warn us so that, having rejected all those things which displease God, we may consecrate ourselves entirely to Him. We should prepare ourselves so that at whatever hour He comes—as He himself teaches us—and wishes to call us out of this life, we may be ready. Likewise, let us patiently bear all the sad events that happen to us, seeing that they do not happen by chance.

...to despise those things; so on the other hand, it is destructive and harmful to fear everything. For as in other matters, so in these too, a middle course <term>modum</term> <note>The author refers to the "golden mean" or moderation—avoiding the extremes of total disbelief and total superstition.</note> must be maintained. He feels the same about other prodigies and miracles. For in the *Life of Camillus* <note>Referring to Plutarch's *Parallel Lives*. Marcus Furius Camillus was a famous Roman general and statesman.</note> it is read that when the leader Camillus had captured and leveled Veii <note>original: "Vejos"; a powerful Etruscan city that was a long-time rival of early Rome.</note>, he made a vow concerning the moving of the statue of Juno to Rome. He therefore commanded certain men to lift the statue; he offered sacrifices to the goddess and asked that she might accompany him and be favorable to the Romans, just like the other gods who dwelt in Rome. The statue replied that it wished to go. He writes that those who recorded these events also recount other prodigies of that kind: such as statues sweating, groaning, turning their faces away, or casting them down. He writes that those who lived before his time collected many things of that sort, and that he himself heard many miraculous things from those who lived in his own time, which should not be immediately despised or rejected by anyone. Nevertheless, he notes that human weakness ensures that it is not without danger to attribute either too much or too little to these things. For men do not keep to a middle way, but are either excessively superstitious, too—

### PART THREE
<note>original: "PARS TERTIA"</note>

<margin>
Nothing in excess.
<note>original: "Ne quid nimis." A famous Latin proverb traditionally attributed to the Seven Sages of Greece, advocating for moderation.</note>
</margin>
—much to attribute to those things: or utterly to reject and despise them. But it is best that we be cautious and maintain a middle way <term>modum</term> <note>The author emphasizes the "golden mean," a philosophical concept where virtue exists between two extremes.</note> in those matters. Even to the pagans <term>gentilibus</term>, many miracles and prodigies were suspect, and not without cause, as even Valerius Maximus <note>A Roman writer from the 1st century AD known for his collection of historical anecdotes used as moral examples.</note> confesses in his first book. True prodigies, however, ought to shake off our lethargy <term>veternum</term> <note>Original: "veternum," referring to a state of spiritual sluggishness or "old-age" indifference.</note>. A noble horse walks correctly; nevertheless, if he is given a signal by a rod or if spurs are applied, he becomes more eager. Likewise, as long as we live, we must walk the path leading us to the heavenly kingdom; but if we observe any signs, and great changes seem to be imminent, we ought to be more stirred up, to devote ourselves to prayers, and to apply ourselves to piety. If the pagans, whenever warnings of this kind were divinely set before them, established their own supplications to appease their gods, how much more should all Christian princes and magistrates, teachers and preachers in our times—with punishments hanging over us—be entirely devoted to this: that one and all may perform true repentance <term>pœnitentiam</term>.

<margin>
Conclusion.
</margin>
So far, I hope, we have shown abundantly what is to be thought regarding *Specters and apparitions of spirits*, and other marvelous things which have a great kinship <note>original: "cognationem"</note>—

—it seems right that they should be judged: and that for several centuries past, the <term>doctores</term> <note>The "doctors" or learned theologians of the medieval Church.</note> spoke and wrote about these things far differently than the matter truly stands. We have also assigned the causes for why that was so. The same could be shown to have happened in other chapters of Christian doctrine, and indeed, it has already been abundantly shown by many men outstanding in learning and piety in books publicly available on these subjects.

And so that I may conclude this book of mine, I would ask all those who come across it, for the glory of **God**, that if they think I depart in any matter from the rule of the word of **God**, they would deign to admonish me in a friendly and free manner. But if indeed (as I hope) they recognize this work to be consistent with the word of **God**, let them not thereafter offer themselves up to be ruled and mocked by monks and sacrificial impostors <term>sacrificis impostoribus</term> <note>A sharp Protestant critique of Catholic priests, whom the author accuses of deceiving the public through the ritual of the Mass, which he views as a "false sacrifice."</note>. Nor should they loosen the reins of their own passions so much that they reject the truth they have recognized.

### PART THREE

<note>original: "PARS TERTIA"</note>

—had perished, the *Senate and People of Rome* <note>original: "Senatus populuſque Romanus," the traditional formal name for the Roman Republic.</note> granted that they should thereafter be free and under their own law. But once the leading men had deliberated on the matter, they refused a freedom which they could not stomach <note>The Latin "concoquere" literally means to digest; the author suggests they were not ready to manage the "richness" of self-rule.</note>, and instead requested a King. The *Romans* marveled at this and gave them the power to declare whoever they wished as king. Let us not be so foolish, but let us instead embrace the freedom of our souls, which God offers us daily through His word.

Many noble nations, fighting bravely, have offered themselves up to the most certain danger of life in order to secure and defend sweet freedom—even if only an external one. How much more ought we *Christians* to fight against the tricks and snares of *Satan*, lest the freedom of our souls, which is far more precious than that other kind, be crushed by various superstitions and errors?

Those sitting in darkness seek light with the most earnest prayers. Therefore, let us not cast away the light offered to us freely by God through the Scriptures. Let us hold nothing on this earth more dear than the freedom of our soul and conscience; as *Paul* says, let us not *suppress the truth in unrighteousness.* <note>A reference to the biblical verse Romans 1:18, where the Apostle Paul warns against those who know God's truth but live contrary to it.</note>

# INDEX OF CHAPTERS
<note>original: "INDEX CAPITUM"</note>

**CHAP. XII.** Concerning the holy <term>Fathers</term><note>The "Church Fathers" refers to influential Christian theologians and writers from the first few centuries of the Church.</note>, Councils, Popes, and the common people who say that souls appear. 213.

**CHAP. XIII.** Whether souls return from <term>Purgatory</term> and the <term>Limbo of Infants</term><note>original: "Limbo puerorum"; historically, a state or place for the souls of unbaptized infants who died before they could commit personal sins.</note>. 218.

**CHAP. XIV.** What those things are which are seen and heard: and first, that sometimes good angels appear. 225.

**CHAP. XV.** That sometimes, and indeed frequently, evil angels are seen. 229.

**CHAP. XVI.** Concerning <term>prodigies</term><note>original: "prodigiis"; supernatural events or signs that seem to violate the laws of nature.</note>, omens, and other things of that kind. 231.

**CHAP. XVII.** It is not difficult for the Devil to appear in various forms and to perform wondrous things. 235.

**CHAP. XVIII.** That devils sometimes command men to do what is good and to avoid the opposite; they also sometimes speak the truth, and why. 241.

**CAP. II.** What the reason is that in our times so few spirits are seen or heard. 259.

**CAP. III.** Why God [causes] unusual crashes <note>original: "fragores"; loud, sudden noises often associated with haunting or divine signaling.</note>...

...unusual and discordant noises <note>original: "strepitus"</note> to be heard before significant changes and other events. 263.

**CHAP. IV.** In what way those to whom good or evil spirits appear, and other wondrous things are presented, ought to conduct themselves; and in the first place, how <term>Gentiles</term><note>In this context, the author refers to the pagans of Greek and Roman antiquity.</note> and Jews conducted themselves in cases of this kind. 265.

**CHAP. V.** In what way Christians to whom <term>Specters</term> present themselves ought to conduct themselves; and first, they ought to be of a courageous heart and firm in their faith. 270.

**CHAP. VI.** It is especially fitting for those who are vexed by Specters to persist in prayers, fastings, sobriety, <term>vigils</term><note>Refers to the practice of staying awake through the night for devotional prayer.</note>, and to strive for a life of piety. 274.

**CHAP. VII.** Spirits that appear ought rightly to be suspicious to us: conversation <note>original: "sermones"</note> is not to be held with them, and nothing is to be learned from them. 277.

**CHAP. VIII.** Testimonies of Holy Scripture and an example showing that faith should not be placed in apparitions of this kind, and that we ought to be cautious. 285.

**CHAP. IX.** In what way the faithful in the <term>Ancient Church</term><note>original: "veteri Ecclesiae"; referring to the early Christian community of the first few centuries.</note> [reacted] at the encounter of Specters...

<note>These markings are identified as pen trials—practice strokes made by a writer to test the flow of ink or the sharpness of a quill or reed pen before beginning formal work. In the context of a scholarly manuscript from this era, it was common for writers to be proficient in several languages, including Arabic, which was studied for theology, philosophy, and science.</note>

->Lisa & Leonard Baskin<-

<note>This inscription identifies the manuscript as having belonged to the private collection of Leonard Baskin (1922–2000) and his wife, Lisa Unger Baskin. Leonard Baskin was a highly influential American sculptor, illustrator, and book designer, famous for founding the Gehenna Press. The Baskins were renowned for their extensive collection of rare books and manuscripts, which often focused on the history of printing, art, and social history.</note>

<summary>This page contains an ownership inscription for Lisa and Leonard Baskin, indicating the manuscript's modern provenance as part of a significant American private collection.</summary>
<keywords>provenance, ownership inscription, Leonard Baskin, Lisa Unger Baskin, 20th-century collectors, book history</keywords>
